Semimartingale characteristics of the two-dimensional stochastic heat equation at criticality
Yu-Ting Chen

Abstract
We characterize the sample-path structure of the two-dimensional stochastic heat equation at criticality. The main theorems provide a complete, pathwise description of its semimartingale characteristics and establish explicit, exact formulas for the covariation measure. The proof derives an asymptotic expansion for the covariation measures of regularized approximate solutions by integrating techniques from quantum-solvable operators into stochastic integration theory.



Support from an NSERC Discovery grant is gratefully acknowledged.

Keywords:
Stochastic heat equation; continuum directed random polymer; delta-Bose gas.

Mathematics Subject Classification (2020):
60H05, 60H15, 60H17.

Contents

1
Introduction

Following the construction of the critical two-dimensional stochastic heat flow (SHF) as a continuous random field by Caravenna, Sun, and Zygouras
[
12
]
, and its subsequent axiomatic characterization via moment matching by Tsai
[
64
]
, we consider the problem of characterizing the underlying space-time semimartingale dynamics. Our main results fully determine the semimartingale characteristics of the scaling limits in the stochastic heat equation (SHE) formulation, extending the classical Jacod–Shiryaev framework
[
44
]
to the infinite-dimensional setting. Explicit, exact representations for the space-time quadratic variations are established. These representations capture the continuous-time dynamics of the limiting SHF while embedding an essential moment formula to determine the underlying coefficients.

In general dimensions
d\geq 1
, the SHE is described by the following SPDE:

\displaystyle\frac{\partial X}{\partial t}(x,t)=\frac{\Delta X}{2}(x,t)+\Lambda^{1/2}X(x,t)\xi(x,t),\quad x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}.

(1.1)

Here,
\Delta
denotes the
d
-dimensional Laplacian,
\Lambda>0
is a coupling constant, and
\xi
represents space-time white noise, a distribution-valued Gaussian field formally characterized by the correlation function

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}[\xi(x,t)\xi(y,s)]=\delta_{0}(x-y)\delta_{0}(t-s).

(1.2)

In the physics literature, the SHE arises from the formal Cole–Hopf transformation of the Kardar–Parisi–Zhang (KPZ) equation
[
46
]
, which describes interface growth, and as a continuum model of directed polymers in random environments (DPRE)
[
37
,
46
]
. A rigorous, fully probabilistic treatment of DPRE originated in the discrete setting with Imbrie and Spencer
[
43
]
. This paved the way for the theory of continuum directed random polymers, first introduced within the SHE framework by Alberts, Khanin, and Quastel
[
1
,
2
]
for
d=1
and also regarded as central to higher-dimensional regimes. For further background and results on DPRE in statistical physics, particularly regarding phase transitions and localization, we refer to Comets’s survey
[
27
]
and the references therein.

Owing to its close resemblance to the heat equation with a potential, the SHE described above may appear to be “readily solved” from a heuristic standpoint using Duhamel’s formula. In one dimension, this intuition is supported by a rigorous framework: replacing the heuristic field
\xi(x,t)
with a space-time white noise random measure
\xi({\mathrm{d}}x,{\mathrm{d}}t)
, formally related via

\displaystyle\xi({\mathrm{d}}x,{\mathrm{d}}t)=\xi(x,t){\mathrm{d}}x{\mathrm{d}}t,

(1.3)

renders the SHE (
1.1
) for
d=1
classically well-posed via Walsh’s extension of Itô’s stochastic integration theory
[
65
]
; see also
[
62
]
for a survey. The equation thus serves as a canonical model within the one-dimensional KPZ universality class
[
28
,
59
]
. The situation changes dramatically for
d\geq 2
, however. In this context, the previously discussed formal solvability encounters significant challenges. This is particularly evident when preserving the multiplication in
\Lambda^{1/2}X\xi
from (
1.1
), which now involves the product of distributions. Furthermore, standard solution theories developed for subcritical singular SPDEs (such as
[
36
,
42
,
50
]
) are inherently restricted to subcritical regimes and do not apply here.

We study the two-dimensional SHE in the critical regime introduced by
Bertini and Cancrini
[
8
]
, focusing on bounded, nonnegative initial conditions (see Section
2.3
for extensions). Following
[
41
]
, we refer to the resulting continuous limit as the “two-dimensional SHE at criticality.” Within this framework, solutions are constructed via regularization and noise-normalized approximations. Specifically, for any constant
\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}
and mollifier
\varrho\in{\mathscr{C}}_{c}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})
, we consider

\displaystyle\frac{\partial X_{\varepsilon}}{\partial t}(x,t)=\frac{\Delta X_{\varepsilon}}{2}(x,t)+\Lambda_{\varepsilon}^{1/2}X_{\varepsilon}(x,t)\xi_{\varepsilon}(x,t),\quad x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2},

(1.4)

with fixed function
X_{0}(\cdot)
independnet of
{\varepsilon}
. Here, the coupling constant and mollified noise are defined by

\displaystyle\Lambda_{\varepsilon}=\Lambda_{\varepsilon}(\lambda)

\displaystyle\,{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm def}}{{=}}}\,\frac{2\pi}{\log{\varepsilon}^{-1}}+\frac{2\pi\lambda}{\log^{2}{\varepsilon}^{-1}},

(1.5)

\displaystyle\xi_{\varepsilon}(x,t)

\displaystyle\,{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm def}}{{=}}}\,\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}\varrho_{\varepsilon}(x-y)\xi(y,t){\mathrm{d}}y,

(1.6)

where

\displaystyle\varrho_{\varepsilon}(x)

\displaystyle\;{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm def}}{{=}}}\,{\varepsilon}^{-2}\varrho({\varepsilon}^{-1}x).

(1.7)

Modern SPDE theory ensures the existence of function-valued solutions
X_{\varepsilon}(x,t)
, whose weak formulation, using

\displaystyle X_{\varepsilon}(f,t)\,{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm def}}{{=}}}\,\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}f(x)X_{\varepsilon}({\mathrm{d}}x,t)\quad\mbox{($X_{\varepsilon}({\mathrm{d}}x,t)\,{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm def}}{{=}}}\,X_{\varepsilon}(x,t){\mathrm{d}}x$)},

(1.8)

is detailed in (
1.13
).

The distinctive feature of the approximate equations above is the presence of special coupling constants in (
1.5
). In
[
8
]
, these constants serve as the crucial condition for the tightness of finite-dimensional marginals of the approximate solutions. This also yields a characterization of the limiting covariance functions that explicitly depend on
(\lambda,\varrho)
. Furthermore, developments over the last decade or so show that, by analyzing higher moments, the limiting solutions are neither Gaussian nor Gaussian multiplicative chaos (GMC). In contrast, markedly different behaviour of the limiting solutions has been established when the definition of
\Lambda_{\varepsilon}
in (
1.5
) is replaced by
2\pi\lambda_{0}/\log{\varepsilon}^{-1}
for any fixed
\lambda_{0}\in(0,1)
. In that context, the approximate equations, while converging to the classical heat equation, exhibit Gaussian fluctuations. Specifically, after normalization of the central limit theorem type, the approximate solutions have finite-dimensional marginals that converge to those of the following
additive
SHEs, which fall again within the scope of modern SPDE solution theory:

\displaystyle\frac{\partial X}{\partial t}(x,t)=\frac{\Delta X}{2}(x,t)+\left(\frac{1}{1-\lambda_{0}}\right)^{1/2}P_{t}X_{0}(x)\xi(x,t),\quad x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}.

(1.9)

Here,
X_{0}(\cdot)
refers to the initial condition as above, and
\{P_{t}\}
denotes the probability semigroup of two-dimensional standard Brownian motion, with kernels given by the following formula:

\displaystyle P_{t}(x,y)=P_{t}(x-y)\,{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm def}}{{=}}}\,\frac{1}{2\pi t}\exp\left(-\frac{\lvert x-y\rvert^{2}}{2t}\right),\quad x,y\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}.

(1.10)

Further references and context regarding the effects of these special coupling constants are expanded in Section
1.2
.

At criticality, the two-dimensional SHE nevertheless presents a fundamental barrier to standard stochastic integration theory. When a sufficiently regular test function
f(x)
is applied, the weak formulation of the SHE in (
1.1
) with
d=2
naturally yields a formal semimartingale whose quadratic variation process is given by

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}\Lambda X(x,t)^{2}f(x)^{2}{\mathrm{d}}x{\mathrm{d}}t.

(1.11)

The main difficulty with directly interpreting (
1.11
) is that, at criticality in two dimensions, the SHE admits only measure-valued solutions (see, e.g.,
[
14
]
), rendering the pointwise term
X(x,t)^{2}
ill-defined and infinite. Thus, while the ill-defined multiplication in the noise term of (
1.1
) already complicates its interpretation as a stochastic integral, the difficulty persists even when considering quadratic variations.

We address this semimartingale structure problem, particularly the characterization of quadratic variations, from a new angle focused on moments. Precise limiting formulas for these moments are well-established via duality with the two-dimensional many-body delta-Bose gas. However, a major obstacle to using them directly is that the
N
-th moments of the two-dimensional SHE at criticality grow too rapidly with
N
(cf.
[
13
, Theorem 1.5]
). Therefore, they fail to uniquely determine the probability distribution via standard moment problems. Nevertheless, these explicit continuum formulas reveal that the
second moment
serves as a fundamental building block for all higher-order moments and imparts their unique character.

Considering the classical principle that the noise coefficient in a stochastic differential equation is determined by its instantaneous variance, we revisit our earlier analysis of second moments for the two-dimensional SHE at criticality
[
16
,
23
]
. This perspective reinforces the fundamental role of second moments in determining all higher-order moments. Furthermore, comparing the formal dynamics in (
1.11
) with the exact limiting second moments reveals that, even at the level of expectations, the heuristic formulation conceals several subtle, genuinely
second-order
effects. These subtleties extend beyond the specific choice of coupling constants in (
1.5
) or the classical interpretation of quadratic variations as second-order corrections in stochastic calculus, yet evade all heuristic approaches known to us. Section
2.1
provides further context and motivates the key formal expansions of covariation measures introduced in Section
2.2
. These expansions are the starting point of our methods.

1.1
Overview of main results

The main theorems of this paper provide a full pathwise characterization of the semimartingale characteristics of the two-dimensional SHE at criticality. We present explicit formulas for the quadratic variations in terms of the solution, obtained via a pathwise calculus approach. Our methods complement the constructive approach of Caravenna, Sun, and Zygouras
[
12
]
and the law characterization framework of Tsai
[
64
]
. Also, while our derivations start with the approximate solutions from (
1.4
), the resulting formulas are established without a limiting procedure, making them directly applicable to stochastic calculus.

By establishing key moment identities and bounds for subsequent applications, our first main result (Theorem
2.7
) proves the joint weak convergence of the approximate solution
X_{\varepsilon}({\mathrm{d}}x,t)
(defined in (
1.8
)) and the covariation measure
{\langle}M_{\varepsilon},M_{\varepsilon}{\rangle}
of its driving martingale measure
M_{\varepsilon}({\mathrm{d}}x,{\mathrm{d}}t)
under a suitable topology. In the limit, this yields the pair consisting of the solution
X
and the covariation measure
\langle M,M\rangle
of the limiting martingale measure
M
. Specifically, the martingale measure
M_{\varepsilon}
takes the following explicit form:

\displaystyle{M}_{\varepsilon}({\mathrm{d}}x,{\mathrm{d}}t)

\displaystyle\,{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm def}}{{=}}}\,\Lambda_{\varepsilon}^{1/2}X_{\varepsilon}(x,t)\xi_{\varepsilon}({\mathrm{d}}x,{\mathrm{d}}t),\quad(x,t)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\times{\mathbb{R}}_{+},

(1.12)

where

\xi_{\varepsilon}({\mathrm{d}}x,{\mathrm{d}}t)\,{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm def}}{{=}}}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}\varrho_{\varepsilon}(x-y)\xi({\mathrm{d}}y,{\mathrm{d}}t){\mathrm{d}}x

is the spatially mollified space-time white noise. Consequently, the standard weak formulation can be equivalently expressed as

\displaystyle X_{\varepsilon}(f,T)

\displaystyle=X_{\varepsilon}(f,0)+\int_{0}^{T}X_{\varepsilon}\left(\frac{\Delta f}{2},t\right){\mathrm{d}}t+\int_{0}^{T}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}f(x)M_{\varepsilon}({\mathrm{d}}x,{\mathrm{d}}t),\quad f\in{\mathscr{C}}^{2}_{pd}({\mathbb{R}}^{2}),

(1.13)

where
X_{\varepsilon}(f,t)
is defined as in (
1.8
), and
{\mathscr{C}}^{k}_{pd}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})
denotes the space of
{\mathscr{C}}^{k}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})
-functions whose partial derivatives up to order
k
exhibit arbitrary polynomial decay. Furthermore, spatial mollification of the white noise
\xi
introduces non-local interactions that regularize the system. This results in a covariation measure for
M_{\varepsilon}
that requires two spatial variables, denoted by
{\langle}M_{\varepsilon},M_{\varepsilon}{\rangle}({\mathrm{d}}x^{\prime},{\mathrm{d}}x,{\mathrm{d}}t)
. In contrast, the limiting martingale measure
M({\mathrm{d}}x,{\mathrm{d}}t)
for the process
X
, defined as the
{\varepsilon}\to 0
analogue of (
1.13
), is
orthogonal
: its covariation measure is supported on the spatial diagonal
\{(x^{\prime},x)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2};x^{\prime}=x\}
for all
t\geq 0
, and is accordingly denoted by
{\langle}M,M{\rangle}({\mathrm{d}}x,{\mathrm{d}}t)
. Section
2.3
provides further details on the setup, the statement of Theorem
2.7
, and the role of Tsai’s law characterization
[
64
]
in our main theorems. For background and foundational results on martingale and covariation measures, we refer to
[
29
,
34
,
65
]
.

While the weak formulation for
X
applies to many SPDEs in general and induces semimartingales, it is now distinguished by the following explicit formula for the covariation measure, which crucially reflects the critical nature of the approximate solutions under consideration: for
f\in{\mathscr{C}}_{pd}^{0}({\mathbb{R}}^{2}\times{\mathbb{R}}_{+})
(defined analogously to
{\mathscr{C}}^{0}_{pd}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})
above),

\displaystyle\int_{t=0}^{T}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}f(x,t)\langle M,M\rangle({\mathrm{d}}x,{\mathrm{d}}t)

\displaystyle\quad=\int_{t=0}^{T}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}f(x,t)\mathring{{\rm DBG}}\mbox{}^{\beta}_{t}X_{0}^{\otimes 2}(x){\mathrm{d}}x{\mathrm{d}}t

\displaystyle\quad\quad+2\int_{t=0}^{T}\int_{t^{\prime}=0}^{t}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}f(x,t)\mathring{{\rm DBG}}\mbox{}^{\beta}_{t-t^{\prime}}(x;z_{1},z_{2})X({\mathrm{d}}z_{2},t^{\prime}){\mathrm{d}}xM({\mathrm{d}}z_{1},{\mathrm{d}}t^{\prime}){\mathrm{d}}t.

(1.14)

Here,
X_{0}^{\otimes 2}(z_{1},z_{2})\,{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm def}}{{=}}}\,X_{0}(z_{1})X_{0}(z_{2})
, and
\{P_{t}\}
denotes the semigroup corresponding to two-dimensional standard Brownian motion, with kernels specified in (
1.10
). The formula in (
1.14
) explicitly involves the kernel
\mathring{{\rm DBG}}\mbox{}^{\beta}_{t}(x;z_{1},z_{2})
and the associated linear functional
\mathring{{\rm DBG}}\mbox{}^{\beta}_{t}F(x)
, which generate the semigroup for the two-dimensional two-body delta-Bose gas with parameter
\beta>0
:

\displaystyle\mathring{{\rm DBG}}\mbox{}^{\beta}_{t}(x;z_{1},z_{2})

\displaystyle\,{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm def}}{{=}}}\,\int_{s=0}^{t}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}P_{(t-s)/2}(x,y){\mathfrak{s}}^{\beta}(t-s)\prod_{j=1}^{2}P_{s}(y,z_{j}){\mathrm{d}}y{\mathrm{d}}s,

(1.15a)

\displaystyle\mathring{{\rm DBG}}\mbox{}^{\beta}_{t}F(x)

\displaystyle\,{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm def}}{{=}}}\,\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}\mathring{{\rm DBG}}\mbox{}^{\beta}_{t}(x;z_{1},z_{2})F(z_{1},z_{2}){\mathrm{d}}z_{1}{\mathrm{d}}z_{2}.

(1.15b)

Here,
{\mathfrak{s}}^{\beta}(\tau)
is defined via Euler’s gamma function
\Gamma(\cdot)
as

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{s}}^{\beta}(\tau)\,{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm def}}{{=}}}\,\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{4\pi\beta^{u}\tau^{u-1}}{\Gamma(u)}{\mathrm{d}}u.

(1.16)

Furthermore, the parameter
\beta
depends explicitly on the noise strength and mollifier
(\lambda,\varrho)
from the approximate SHE in (
1.4
) via

\displaystyle\frac{\log\beta}{2}=\log 2+\lambda-\gamma_{\mathsf{E}\mathsf{M}}-\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}\phi(y^{\prime})\phi(y)\log\lvert y^{\prime}-y\rvert{\mathrm{d}}y^{\prime}{\mathrm{d}}y,

(1.17)

where
\gamma_{\mathsf{E}\mathsf{M}}
is the Euler–Mascheroni constant, and

\displaystyle\phi(y)\,{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm def}}{{=}}}\,\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}\varrho(y^{\prime})\varrho(y^{\prime}-y){\mathrm{d}}y^{\prime}.

(1.18)

The formula for the covariation measure in (
1.14
), along with our technical framework, is fundamentally based on the system’s second-order characteristics, aligning with our original motivation (recall the discussion preceding Section
1.1
). The delta-Bose gas kernel
\mathring{{\rm DBG}}\mbox{}^{\beta}_{t}(x;z_{1},z_{2})
not only serves as the primary building block encoding these second-order characteristics, but also has the following direct consequences:

•

Whereas the subcritical additive SHE in (
1.9
) is governed by the classical heat kernel, (
1.14
) reveals that the critical two-dimensional regime is instead controlled by the highly nonlinear kernel
\mathring{{\rm DBG}}\mbox{}^{\beta}_{t}(x;z_{1},z_{2})
.

•

Furthermore, this kernel emerges directly from (
1.14
) since taking the expectation on both sides eliminates the second term on the right-hand side (Corollary
2.9
). As a result, (
1.14
) generalizes the classical second-moment formula for the two-dimensional SHE at criticality to the pathwise semimartingale setting.

More broadly, our analysis shows that the precise sample-path behavior of the two-dimensional SHE at criticality is shaped predominantly by second-order characteristics. Quadratic variations and second-moment formulas are essential, while higher moments are invoked exclusively to establish key bounds. Further features of (
1.14
), alongside several structural variants, are discussed in Section
2.3
.

Our semimartingale characterization of the two-dimensional SHE at criticality naturally raises the question of whether the weak formulation and our explicit formulas for the covariation measures uniquely determine the probability distributions. This uniqueness is particularly relevant given that these equations constitute a standard martingale problem formulation. In particular, establishing uniqueness in law via classical methods, as in
[
35
,
63
]
, appears to require techniques distinct from those of the moment-based axiomatic framework of Tsai
[
64
]
. Due to the length and scope of this paper, we leave this line of inquiry to separate work, which is currently under our investigation.

1.2
Context and literature review

To expand on the context surrounding the special coupling constant regimes and the associated limiting behavior, let us highlight several key lines of work in the literature. Regarding evidence of non-Gaussianity, early work by Feng
[
38
]
provided the first analysis; explicit higher-moment formulas were subsequently developed in
[
9
,
18
,
41
]
(see Section
2.1
for further discussion); and Caravenna, Sun, and Zygouras
[
13
]
established that the limiting solutions do not fall within the class of GMC. For results on the Gaussian fluctuations and the closely related KPZ formulations in the subcritical regime, we refer the reader to
[
10
,
11
,
15
,
39
,
56
]
, among others. In particular, the identification of (
1.9
) across the entire subcritical regime for general initial conditions is due to Nakajima and Nakashima
[
56
]
.

Caravenna, Sun, and Zygouras
[
12
]
constructed the critical two-dimensional stochastic heat flow (SHF) as a unique scaling limit. This provides a rigorous framework for canonical solutions to the two-dimensional stochastic heat equation at criticality, with the SHF serving as the fundamental solution. The constructions in
[
12
]
are centred on passing the limit of a specific coarse-grained model that is capable of handling two regularizations widely applied in the earlier literature simultaneously. The first regularization, also the main focus of study in
[
12
]
, is formulated using a discrete polymer model, in which the underlying random-walk generator and i.i.d. disorder serve as the discrete two-dimensional Laplacian and space-time white noise, respectively. Subject only to mild moment conditions on the disorder, the corresponding limit further exhibits disorder universality. The second regularization considered in
[
12
]
uses the mollified model described by (
1.4
), and is thus directly related to the framework employed in this paper.

The stochastic heat flow associated with the approximate model in (
1.4
) captures key properties of the critical two-dimensional SHF at the pre-limit level. In this context, it is precisely represented by a family of random bi-measures

{\rm SHE}_{{\varepsilon},(\lambda,\varrho)}(s,t)={\rm SHE}_{{\varepsilon},(\lambda,\varrho)}({\mathrm{d}}x,s;{\mathrm{d}}y,t),\quad x,y\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2},\;0\leq s\leq t<\infty,

whose kernels are defined by the following rigorous, though approximate, mathematical formulation for the continuum directed random polymer previously discussed:

\displaystyle\hskip-5.69046pt\frac{{\rm SHF}_{{\varepsilon},(\lambda,\varrho)}({\mathrm{d}}x,s;{\mathrm{d}}y,t)}{{\mathrm{d}}x{\mathrm{d}}y}

\displaystyle\hskip-5.69046pt\quad\,{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm def}}{{=}}}\,{\mathbb{E}}^{W}_{x,s}\left[\exp\left\{\Lambda_{\varepsilon}^{1/2}\int_{s}^{t}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}\varrho_{\varepsilon}(W_{r}-z)\xi({\mathrm{d}}z,{\mathrm{d}}r)-\frac{(t-s)\Lambda_{\varepsilon}}{2}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}\varrho_{\varepsilon}(z)^{2}{\mathrm{d}}z\right\}\delta_{y}(W_{t})\right].

(1.19)

Here,
W
denotes a two-dimensional standard Brownian motion, independent of
\xi
, starting from
x
at time
s
under
{\mathbb{E}}^{W}_{x,s}
, and
{\mathbb{E}}^{W}_{x,s}
leaves
\xi
unintegrated. For further discussion of the connection between (
1.19
) and the continuum directed random polymer, see
[
18
, Section 1]
, which utilizes a time-reversed Brownian motion approach instead. In the manner of (
1.19
), the approximate stochastic heat flow acts as the fundamental solution to (
1.4
), relating to
X_{\varepsilon}
via the following equation for appropriate test functions
f
:

\displaystyle\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}f(y)X_{\varepsilon}(y,t){\mathrm{d}}y=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}X_{\varepsilon}(x,s)f(y){\rm SHF}_{{\varepsilon},(\lambda,\varrho)}({\mathrm{d}}x,s;{\mathrm{d}}y,t),\quad 0\leq s\leq t<\infty.

(1.20)

Further properties of
{\rm SHF}_{{\varepsilon},(\lambda,\varrho)}
include the following independence and flow properties: For any integer
n\geq 1
and
0\leq s_{1}\leq s_{2}\leq\cdots\leq s_{n+1}
, the bi-measures over the associated non-overlapping intervals, given by
{\rm SHE}_{{\varepsilon},(\lambda,\varrho)}(s_{1},s_{2}),\ldots,{\rm SHE}_{{\varepsilon},(\lambda,\varrho)}(s_{n},s_{n+1})
, are independent, reflecting the correlation structure of space-time white noise. Additionally, the flow property is directly exhibited by the following identity, generalizing (
1.20
):

\displaystyle{\rm SHE}_{{\varepsilon},(\lambda,\varrho)}({\mathrm{d}}x,r;{\mathrm{d}}y,t)

\displaystyle\quad=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}{\rm SHE}_{{\varepsilon},(\lambda,\varrho)}({\mathrm{d}}x,r;{\mathrm{d}}z,s)\frac{{\rm SHE}_{{\varepsilon},(\lambda,\varrho)}({\mathrm{d}}z,s;{\mathrm{d}}y,t)}{{\mathrm{d}}z},\quad 0\leq r\leq s\leq t.

For the existence of the limiting analogues of these independence and flow properties, see Clark and Mian
[
26
]
.

Tsai’s axiomatic characterization
[
64
]
stands in contrast to the constructive approach by Caravenna, Sun, and Zygouras
[
12
]
. The approach in
[
64
]
focuses directly on the key role of known moment formulas for the two-dimensional SHE at criticality, together with independence and flow properties analogous to those discussed earlier for
X_{\varepsilon}
. In essence, the conditions isolated in
[
64
]
allow an extension of Lindeberg’s method
[
52
]
to the SHF context, replacing probability distributions over all small time intervals via moments while maintaining control over cumulative errors. It is notable that Tsai’s theorem
[
64
]
applies to the mollified SHE in (
1.4
), proving the law convergence of its solutions. Specifically,
[
64
, Section 1.2]
considers an extension of the SHF driven by space-time white noise defined across the entire time domain
(-\infty,\infty)
, and the SHE is also formulated this way.

Nakashima
[
57
]
studied the critical two-dimensional SHF by examining the continuum limit of a discretized model, which closely follows the discretization in Caravenna, Sun, and Zygouras
[
12
]
. By testing the discrete stochastic heat flow against sufficiently regular functions for both initial and terminal conditions (as in (
1.20
) with
s=0
), Nakashima demonstrated that the weak formulation of the distributional limit produces continuous semimartingales. This leads to a limiting bi-measure-valued process that shares the same finite-dimensional marginals as the critical two-dimensional SHF constructed in
[
12
]
. Additionally, Nakashima’s proof establishes
C
-tightness for the martingale components and their predictable quadratic variations. In Section
2.3
, we examine the description of the quadratic variations for these limiting semimartingales given in
[
57
]
and discuss how our formulation provides a complementary perspective.

Frequently used notation.
We write
C(T)\in(0,\infty)
to denote a constant depending only on
T
, which may change from inequality to inequality unless explicitly indexed. Other constants are defined analogously, and we write
A=\mathcal{O}_{\chi}(B)
if
\lvert A\rvert\leq C(\chi)\lvert B\rvert
. Furthermore,
A\lesssim B
(or
B\gtrsim A
) indicates that
\lvert A\rvert\leq C\lvert B\rvert
for a universal constant
C\in(0,\infty)
, and
A\asymp B
means that both
A\lesssim B
and
B\gtrsim A
. Other frequently used notations are listed below:

•

For a stochastic process
Y
, we use
{\mathbb{E}}^{Y}_{y}
to denote expectation and
{\mathbb{P}}^{Y}_{y}
to denote probability, both conditioned on
Y
starting at
y
.

•

\log
denotes the natural logarithm, and
\log^{b}a\,{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm def}}{{=}}}\,(\log a)^{b}
for all
b\in{\mathbb{R}}
.

•

For any subset
\Theta
of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
with
d\in\mathbb{N}
,
{\mathscr{C}}_{pd}^{k}(\Theta)
denotest the space of functions
f
on
\Theta
that extend to a
{\mathscr{C}}^{k}
-function on some open set containing
\Theta
, whose all partial derivatives up to order
k
exhibit arbitrary polynomial decay.

2
Statement of main theorems

2.1
From second moments to covariation measures

In this paper, we establish our main theorems by integrating asymptotic expansion techniques for quantum-solvable operators into stochastic integration theory. The following expectation formula provides the foundational link to these operators:

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\biggl[\prod_{j=1}^{2}X_{\varepsilon}(x_{j},t)\biggr]={\mathbb{E}}^{B^{1},B^{2}}_{(x_{1},x_{2})}\biggl[\exp\biggl\{\Lambda_{\varepsilon}\int_{0}^{t}\phi_{\varepsilon}(B^{2}_{s}-B^{1}_{s}){\mathrm{d}}s\biggr\}\prod_{j=1}^{2}X_{0}(B^{j}_{t})\biggr].

(2.1)

Here,
\Lambda_{\varepsilon}
is as defined in (
1.5
),
\phi_{\varepsilon}(x)\,{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm def}}{{=}}}\,{\varepsilon}^{-2}\phi({\varepsilon}^{-1}x)
is an approximation to the identity based on the mollifier
\phi
from (
1.18
), and
B^{1},B^{2}
are independent two-dimensional standard Brownian motions, starting from
x_{1},x_{2}
under the expectation
{\mathbb{E}}^{B^{1},B^{2}}_{(x_{1},x_{2})}
. Quantum-solvable operators give the purely analytic counterpart of the right-hand side of (
2.1
), because the Brownian exponential moment generates the Feynman–Kac semigroup associated with the following two-particle Schrödinger operator:

-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j=1}^{2}\Delta_{x_{j}}-\Lambda_{\varepsilon}\phi_{\varepsilon}(x_{2}-x_{1}),\quad x_{1},x_{2}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2},

where
\Delta_{x}
denotes the two-dimensional Laplacian with respect to
x
.
This operator rigorously approximates the Hamiltonian of the
two-dimensional two-body delta-Bose gas
:

-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j=1}^{2}\Delta_{x_{j}}-\Lambda^{\prime}\delta_{0}(x_{2}-x_{1}),\quad x_{1},x_{2}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}.

A central feature of the Brownian representation in (
2.1
) is the applicability of the following explicit limiting formula, which holds for all sufficiently large
q>0
:

\displaystyle\lim_{{\varepsilon}\to 0}\int_{0}^{\infty}{\mathrm{e}}^{-qt}{\mathbb{E}}^{B}_{x}\biggl[\exp\biggl\{\Lambda_{\varepsilon}\int_{0}^{t}\phi_{\varepsilon}({\sqrt{2}}B_{s}){\mathrm{d}}s\biggr\}g(B_{t})\biggr]{\mathrm{d}}t

\displaystyle\quad=\int_{0}^{\infty}{\mathrm{e}}^{-qt}P_{t}g(x)+\int_{0}^{\infty}{\mathrm{e}}^{-qt}P_{t}(x){\mathrm{d}}t\times\frac{2\pi}{\log(q/\beta)}\int_{0}^{\infty}{\mathrm{e}}^{-qt}P_{t}g(0){\mathrm{d}}t.

(2.2)

Here,
B
is a two-dimensional standard Brownian motion, starting from
x
under
{\mathbb{E}}^{B}_{x}
, and the parameter
\beta
is defined as in (
1.17
) via
(\lambda,\phi)
. Equation (
2.2
) highlights a subtle spatial behavior: for any nonzero, nonnegative, and bounded function
g
, the limit diverges at
x=0
but converges for all
x\neq 0
. Furthermore, this formula enables the exact representation of second moments by utilizing the independence of
B^{2}+B^{1}
and
B^{2}-B^{1}
, where
B^{2}-B^{1}
has the same distribution as
{\sqrt{2}}B
. Applying appropriate inverse Laplace transforms then leads to the ultimate untransformed representation (see
[
3
, Section 3.2]
and
[
18
, Proposition 5.1]
). Analytically, this probabilistic decoupling corresponds to the classical change of variables
x_{2}+x_{1}
and
x_{2}-x_{1}
for the two-dimensional two-body delta-Bose gas. The reduced operator in the relative coordinate
x_{2}-x_{1}
is the
two-dimensional Laplacian singularly perturbed at the origin
, a crucial relation that forms the foundation of the approach by Bertini and Cancrini
[
8
]
.

The study of (
2.2
) originated in the work of Albeverio, Gesztesy, Høegh-Krohn, and Holden
[
4
]
. They introduced functional-analytic techniques for resolvent decompositions to study the two-dimensional Laplacian singularly perturbed at the origin; see also
[
5
]
for a review and the general framework. Functional-analytic approaches extending this operator-theoretic setting to the many-body delta-Bose gas were subsequently developed in
[
32
,
33
]
. This connection provides a purely analytic route to determining limiting moments, expressing the higher moments of the two-dimensional SHE at criticality via the many-body semigroup. By utilizing this connection, Gu, Quastel, and Tsai
[
41
]
introduced resolvent expansions that generalize the Neumann series to construct the many-body delta-Bose gas semigroup in two dimensions, which directly applies to higher moments of the critical SHE. The motivation for considering the Brownian representation in (
2.1
) is detailed in Remark
2.2
.

Our approach in
[
23
, Section 4.4]
to proving (
2.2
) differs from earlier methods by recasting the problem in terms of asymptotic recursions, bypassing the heavy reliance on constructing preliminary exact identities. (This viewpoint is particularly useful for developing an asymptotic expansion in the more subtle scenario where the associated mollifier has zero integral. We do not employ such results in this paper, though.) In the current setting, where
\phi
is a probability density, the approach of
[
23
]
focuses on the following Green functions, evaluated with
a small initial condition

{\varepsilon}x
and an arbitrary nonzero bounded nonnegative function
g
:

\displaystyle G_{\varepsilon}(x)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm def}}{{=}}\int_{0}^{\infty}{\mathrm{e}}^{-qt}{\mathbb{E}}^{B}_{{\varepsilon}x}\biggl[\exp\biggl\{\Lambda_{\varepsilon}\int_{0}^{t}\phi_{\varepsilon}({\sqrt{2}}B_{s}){\mathrm{d}}s\biggr\}g(B_{t})\biggr]{\mathrm{d}}t.

(2.3)

The use of small conditions allows capturing the primary mechanism underlying the limiting formula in (
2.2
), as we have the following limit:

\displaystyle\lim_{{\varepsilon}\to 0}\Lambda_{\varepsilon}G_{\varepsilon}(x)=\frac{2\pi}{\log(q/\beta)}\int_{0}^{\infty}{\mathrm{e}}^{-qt}P_{t}g(0){\mathrm{d}}t,

(2.4)

which holds uniformly for
x
in compact subsets of
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
and all sufficiently large
q>0
.

The proof of (
2.4
) in
[
23
, Section 4.4]
centers on constructing preliminary asymptotic expansions for
G_{\varepsilon}(x)
, using only the standard asymptotic expansion of the Green’s function of two-dimensional Brownian motion (see (
7.5
)) and a priori bounds for
G_{\varepsilon}(x)
, which are independently obtained via a convolution-type Grönwall inequality in
[
18
]
. This leads to the conclusion that
G_{\varepsilon}(x)
admits the following asymptotic expansion, which can also be interpreted as an asymptotic recursion:

\displaystyle G_{\varepsilon}(x)=C_{1}+(1-s_{\varepsilon}C_{2})G_{\varepsilon}(x)+o(1),\quad{\varepsilon}\to 0.

(2.5)

Moreover, we have the following specifications of
s_{\varepsilon}
,
C_{1}
, and
C_{2}
:

s_{\varepsilon}\equiv\Lambda_{\varepsilon},\quad C_{1}\equiv\int_{0}^{\infty}{\mathrm{e}}^{-qt}P_{t}g(0){\mathrm{d}}t,\quad C_{2}\equiv\frac{\log(q/\beta)}{2\pi}.

A direct consequence of (
2.5
) is
\lim_{{\varepsilon}\to 0}s_{\varepsilon}G_{\varepsilon}(x)=C_{1}/C_{2}
. Here, the seemingly simple prefactor
1-s_{\varepsilon}C_{2}
plays a central role. The constant
1
identifies the system’s criticality: it is replaced by
\lambda_{0}
in the subcritical regime, where
\Lambda_{\varepsilon}
from (
1.5
) is replaced by
2\pi\lambda_{0}/\log{\varepsilon}^{-1}
for
\lambda_{0}\in(0,1)
. Furthermore, the term
s_{\varepsilon}C_{2}
highlights a crucial second-order contribution that underscores the system’s critical nature. In the subcritical limit, such a term plays no role, nor does introducing a second-order correction to the renormalizing constant.

We propose that the asymptotic recursion method from
[
23
]
, discussed above, is deeply connected to the microscopic sample-path dynamics of the two-dimensional SHE at criticality. An immediate challenge in this pursuit is determining whether the relationship between the second moments and the critical SHE is sufficiently robust. Nonetheless, as with SDEs (recall the discussion before Section
1.1
), the noise coefficient of an SPDE can be interpreted as an instantaneous variance. This perspective is particularly prominent in the derivation of SPDEs and martingale problems for super-Brownian motion, and more generally, the Dawson–Watanabe superprocesses (see, e.g.,
[
31
,
48
,
53
,
58
,
60
,
66
]
), which serve as natural analogues of the SHE in (
1.1
). In the context of super-Brownian motion, the noise coefficient
\Lambda^{1/2}X
in (
1.1
) is replaced by
\Lambda^{1/2}\sqrt{X}
. Moreover, an analogue of the formal variance of
\Lambda^{1/2}X(x,t)
for the regularized SHE in two dimensions satisfies the following equation (see
[
18
, Section 2.3.1]
for a detailed derivation):

\displaystyle{\rm Var}\big(\Lambda_{\varepsilon}^{1/2}X_{\varepsilon}(x,t)\big)+\Lambda_{\varepsilon}[P_{t}X_{0}(x)]^{2}

\displaystyle\quad=\Lambda_{\varepsilon}{\mathbb{E}}^{B}_{0}\biggl[\exp\left\{\Lambda_{\varepsilon}\int_{0}^{t}\phi_{\varepsilon}({\sqrt{2}}B_{s}){\mathrm{d}}s\right\}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}P_{t}({\sqrt{2}}x,y)\prod_{\varsigma\in\{\pm\}}X_{0}\left(\frac{y+\varsigma B_{t}}{{\sqrt{2}}}\right){\mathrm{d}}y\biggr].

This illustrates a direct connection to the Green function
G_{\varepsilon}(0)
as defined in (
2.3
).

The following problem translates the above heuristics into a concrete formulation that reflects our initial idea for the method of this paper.

Problem 2.1.

Rigorously formulate the following pathwise stochastic asymptotic recursion extending (
2.5
), and derive the associated limiting equation:

X_{\varepsilon}(x,t)^{2}=F_{0}(x,t)+\left[1-s_{\varepsilon}F_{1}(x,t)\right]X_{\varepsilon}(x,t)^{2}+F_{2,{\varepsilon}}(x,t)+o(1),\quad{\varepsilon}\to 0,

where
s_{\varepsilon}\to 0
,
F_{0},F_{1}
are
{\varepsilon}
-independent random fields, and
F_{2,{\varepsilon}}
is a mean-zero random field that converges as
{\varepsilon}\to 0
, all with explicit representations.

We stress that the expansion considered in Problem
2.1
is for
X_{\varepsilon}(x,t)^{2}
, not
\Lambda_{\varepsilon}X_{\varepsilon}(x,t)^{2}
. Therefore, applications to quadratic variations require
s_{\varepsilon}=\Theta(\log^{-1}{\varepsilon}^{-1})
as
{\varepsilon}\to 0
.

An immediate technical challenge posed by Problem
2.1
is the lack of resolvents, tools that have proven crucial in existing proofs for deriving limiting second moments and analyzing related quantum-solvable models. Nevertheless, by directly targeting the sample-path dynamics, our formulation in Problem
2.1
bypasses iterations of the mild formulation (see (
2.18
)). Specifically, this avoids relying on chaos series expansions. While effective throughout the subcritical regime, these expansions do not seem to provide a clear path for determining precise sample-path descriptions, such as the semimartingale characteristics considered here.

Remark 2.2 (Interacting paths in moments).

In contrast to the operator-theoretic techniques discussed above, our emphasis on the path-measure structure in (
2.1
) continues the line of development initiated in
[
18
]
, where we established an alternative, probabilistic-analytic construction of higher moments. This path-measure structure enriches the SPDE and semimartingale analysis in the present work.

The physical formulation of this approach goes back to Kardar and Zhang
[
47
]
, who studied
N
-th moments in the un-mollified regime using formal, real-valued Feynman path integrals. The associated
N
polymers were considered as paths that interact via the correlation function of the space-time white noise. A comprehensive review is provided in
[
45
]
. Bertini and Cancrini
[
8
]
provided the first mathematical formulation of this idea, viewing the second moments for
\varepsilon>0
as exponential moments of approximate intersection local times of two Brownian paths. In the continuum limit, these paths were expected to exhibit the attractive contact interactions predicted by Kardar and Zhang. However, a direct proof was obstructed by the absence of Brownian local times in two dimensions. A rigorous construction confirming that the limiting second moments indeed support such interacting paths via precise Feynman–Kac-type formulas was proved in
[
22
]
. Subsequently, Feynman–Kac-type formulas for higher-order moments were established across a series of works
[
19
,
20
,
21
,
24
]
, where the underlying interacting paths are constructed as multiple Brownian motions “conditioned for indefinite pairwise contact interactions.” Notably, this higher-order construction relies heavily on the second-moment diffusions established in
[
22
]
, underscoring the central role that second moments play throughout the perspective introduced in Section
1
and elaborated on above.
\blacklozenge

2.2
Formal expansions of covariation measures

In this subsection, we present a formal solution to Problem
2.1
in Heuristic
2.3
below, leaving the second-order term unspecified. This approach elevates moment analysis to the pathwise setting and constructs a fully independent dynamical asymptotic expansion, positioning Itô’s formula as the natural tool. Remark
2.5
addresses how this formal solution extends to the subcritical case. This provides a heuristic explanation for the emergence of the known additive SHE limit in (
1.9
) and unifies these approaches up to criticality. Finally, Remark
2.6
discusses the fundamental obstacles faced when extending our approach to the supercritical regime.

We adopt the following notation for the formal solution. Since our analysis examines the limiting behaviour of
X_{\varepsilon}(x,t)^{2}
as
{\varepsilon}\to 0
, we suppress explicit dependence on
{\varepsilon}
and let
X(x,t)
denote the formal limit of
X_{{\varepsilon}}(x,t)
as
{\varepsilon}\to 0
. In this context, rather than taking
\lim_{{\varepsilon}\to 0}\Lambda_{\varepsilon}=0
, we preserve the renormalizing effect of
\Lambda_{\varepsilon}
by using the placeholder
\Lambda
, which is interpreted as
0+
when we evaluate limiting expressions.
This notation is therefore fully consistent with the formal SHE (
1.1
) for
d=2
and applies throughout Section
2.2
unless stated otherwise.
Additionally, we work with the following purely formal martingale measure:

M({\mathrm{d}}x,{\mathrm{d}}t)=\Lambda^{1/2}X(x,t)\xi({\mathrm{d}}x,{\mathrm{d}}t),

which in turn has the following purely formal covariation measure:

\displaystyle{\langle}M,M{\rangle}({\mathrm{d}}x,{\mathrm{d}}t)=\Lambda X(x,t)^{2}{\mathrm{d}}x{\mathrm{d}}t;

(2.6)

see (
1.12
) for analogues at the approximate level. Note that while we specify the components of
M
for its description, we will avoid the ill-posed product
\Lambda^{1/2}X\xi
as in (
1.1
) by treating
M
as an indivisible object.

Heuristic 2.3.

Under the notation introduced above, we have

\displaystyle[P_{t}X_{0}(x)]^{2}

\displaystyle=X(x,t)^{2}-\int_{s=0}^{t}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}P_{t-s}(x,y)^{2}\Lambda X(y,s)^{2}{\mathrm{d}}y{\mathrm{d}}s

\displaystyle\quad-2\int_{t^{\prime}=0}^{t}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}\biggl(\prod_{j=1}^{2}P_{t-t^{\prime}}(x,z_{j})\biggr)X(z_{2},t^{\prime}){\mathrm{d}}z_{2}{M}({\mathrm{d}}z_{1},{\mathrm{d}}t^{\prime}),

(2.7)

\displaystyle[P_{t}X_{0}(x)]^{2}

\displaystyle=X(x,t)^{2}-X(x,t)^{2}

\displaystyle\quad-2\int_{t^{\prime}=0}^{t}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}\biggl(\prod_{j=1}^{2}P_{t-t^{\prime}}(x,z_{j})\biggr)X(z_{2},t^{\prime}){\mathrm{d}}z_{2}{M}({\mathrm{d}}z_{1},{\mathrm{d}}t^{\prime}),

(2.8)

where
P_{t}(x,y)
denotes the transition density of two-dimensional standard Brownian motion as defined in (
1.10
), and
\{P_{t}\}
is the associated heat semigroup.

Equation (
2.8
) plays a fundamental role in this work and refines the expansion in (
2.7
). We highlight two key features of (
2.8
), deferring further discussion to Remark
2.4
:

•

Heuristic interpretation of the formal components:
The terms on the right-hand side of (
2.8
) reflect two distinct roles:

–

We emphasize that the difference
X(x,t)^{2}-X(x,t)^{2}
in (
2.8
) is a purely symbolic representation rather than a vanishing real-valued difference. This notation is chosen both to signify a formal
\infty-\infty
indeterminate limit resulting from singular cancellation (because the limiting process is measure-valued, as discussed around (
1.11
)) and to encapsulate the second-order term considered in Section
2.1
. Specifically, the derivation below reveals the second
X(x,t)^{2}
as the leading-order term in the divergent integral
\int_{s=0}^{t}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}P_{t-s}(x,y)^{2}\Lambda X(y,s)^{2}{\mathrm{d}}y{\mathrm{d}}s
in (
2.7
). In turn, this integral arises naturally as the
{\varepsilon}\to 0
limit of a regularized integral whose precise form must be identified through precise
{\varepsilon}
-level calculations.

–

The formal equation (
2.8
) identifies a mean-zero random field given by the stochastic integral

\displaystyle-2\int_{t^{\prime}=0}^{t}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}\biggl(\prod_{j=1}^{2}P_{t-t^{\prime}}(x,z_{j})\biggr)X(z_{2},t^{\prime}){\mathrm{d}}z_{2}{M}({\mathrm{d}}z_{1},{\mathrm{d}}t^{\prime}).

(2.9)

•

Analytical challenges in rigorous justification:
The formal derivation of (
2.8
) proceeds via heuristic manipulations that fully bypass potential singularities. However, rigorously establishing the complete asymptotic expansion and extracting the second-order term pose two major analytical challenges:

–

The first major challenge arises from omitting perturbative terms in these calculations, an act that conceals the crucial but latent second-order contribution emphasized in Section
2.1
, and from managing any resulting singularities in the limiting process. To address this, our forthcoming proofs rely on the weak formulation to absorb pointwise singularities. We then systematically track all
{\varepsilon}
-perturbations, establish uniform-in-
{\varepsilon}
moment bounds, and identify the exact limiting second-order term. This analysis draws on baseline results for SPDEs with colored noise due to Dalang
[
29
]
and Mytnik, Perkins, and Sturm
[
55
]
, alongside a general theorem on
C
-tightness of laws of measure-valued processes due to Perkins
[
58
]
. Our earlier methods for bounding moments of the two-dimensional SHE at criticality are also adapted and extended.

–

Another major technical challenge is to provide rigorous justifications for the existence of the stochastic integral in (
2.9
), whose pre-limiting processes prove to be mean-zero random fields sought in Problem
2.1
, as well as for the convergence of the approximating sequences. To address the convergence, the Kurtz–Protter method
[
49
]
offers crucial tools for establishing the convergence of ordinary stochastic integrals
\int Y_{n}{\mathrm{d}}Z_{n}\to\int Y{\mathrm{d}}Z
as
(Y_{n},Z_{n})\to(Y,Z)
. In this context, we also make use of Cho’s extension
[
25
]
to stochastic integrals with respect to martingale measures.

Derivation of Heuristic
2.3
.

We start by expanding
X(x,t)^{2}
, which determines the covariation measure
in (
2.6
). To proceed, we assume and utilize the mild form satisfied by the formal limit
X(x,t)
mentioned above:

\displaystyle X(x,t)=P_{t}X_{0}(x)+\int_{s=0}^{t}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}P_{t-s}(x,y)M({\mathrm{d}}y,{\mathrm{d}}s).

(2.10)

We then apply (
2.10
) to expand
X(x,t)^{2}
and rearrange the resulting equation via the algebraic identity

a^{2}=c^{2}-2ab-b^{2}\quad\mbox{given }c=a+b.

This yields:

\displaystyle[P_{t}X_{0}(x)]^{2}

\displaystyle=X(x,t)^{2}-2P_{t}X_{0}(x)\int_{s=0}^{t}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}P_{t-s}(x,y){M}({\mathrm{d}}y,{\mathrm{d}}s)

\displaystyle\quad-\left(\int_{s=0}^{t}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}P_{t-s}(x,y){M}({\mathrm{d}}y,{\mathrm{d}}s)\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=X(x,t)^{2}-2P_{t}X_{0}(x)\int_{s=0}^{t}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}P_{t-s}(x,y){M}({\mathrm{d}}y,{\mathrm{d}}s)

\displaystyle\quad-2\int_{s^{\prime}=0}^{t}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}\int_{s=0}^{s^{\prime}}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}P_{t-s}(x,y){M}({\mathrm{d}}y,{\mathrm{d}}s)P_{t-s^{\prime}}(x,y^{\prime}){M}({\mathrm{d}}y^{\prime},{\mathrm{d}}s^{\prime})

\displaystyle\quad-\int_{s=0}^{t}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}P_{t-s}(x,y)^{2}\Lambda X(y,s)^{2}{\mathrm{d}}y{\mathrm{d}}s.

(2.11)

Here, we apply Itô’s formula to the martingale
\int_{s=0}^{s^{\prime}}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}P_{t-s}(x,y){M}({\mathrm{d}}y,{\mathrm{d}}s)
,
0\leq s^{\prime}\leq t
, which produces the last two terms in (
2.11
).

Next, the following calculation suffices to demonstrate that (
2.11
) simplifies to (
2.7
):

\displaystyle-2\int_{s=0}^{t}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}\int_{s^{\prime}=0}^{s}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}P_{t-s^{\prime}}(x,y^{\prime}){M}({\mathrm{d}}y^{\prime},{\mathrm{d}}s^{\prime})P_{t-s}(x,y){M}({\mathrm{d}}y,{\mathrm{d}}s)

\displaystyle\quad=-2\int_{s=0}^{t}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}\int_{s^{\prime}=0}^{s}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}P_{t-s}(x,z)P_{s-s^{\prime}}(z,y^{\prime}){\mathrm{d}}z{M}({\mathrm{d}}y^{\prime},{\mathrm{d}}s^{\prime})P_{t-s}(x,y){M}({\mathrm{d}}y,{\mathrm{d}}s)

\displaystyle\quad=-2\int_{s=0}^{t}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}P_{t-s}(x,z)[-P_{s}X_{0}(z)+X(z,s)]{\mathrm{d}}zP_{t-s}(x,y){M}({\mathrm{d}}y,{\mathrm{d}}s)

\displaystyle\quad=2P_{t}X_{0}(x)\int_{s=0}^{t}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}P_{t-s}(x,y){M}({\mathrm{d}}y,{\mathrm{d}}s)

\displaystyle\quad\quad-2\int_{s=0}^{t}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}P_{t-s}(x,y)P_{t-s}(x,z)X(z,s){\mathrm{d}}z{M}({\mathrm{d}}y,{\mathrm{d}}s),

where the first and last equalities rely on the Chapman–Kolmogorov equation, and the second equality follows from the mild form (
2.10
).

The crucial step in this derivation, leading to (
2.8
), is to apply the following observation. Because we are effectively in the regime
{\varepsilon}=0+
, a heat kernel expansion [see Lemma
7.1
(1
∘
)] suggests that, by neglecting subleading terms,

\displaystyle\Lambda P_{t-s}(x,y)^{2}{\mathrm{d}}s{\mathrm{d}}y=\delta_{t}({\mathrm{d}}s)\delta_{x}({\mathrm{d}}y),\quad 0\leq s\leq t,\;x,y\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2},

(2.12)

where
\delta_{t}({\mathrm{d}}s)
and
\delta_{x}({\mathrm{d}}y)
represent Dirac measures. Thus, we expect the following equation:

\int_{s=0}^{t}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}P_{t-s}(x,y)^{2}\Lambda X(y,s)^{2}{\mathrm{d}}y{\mathrm{d}}s=X(x,t)^{2}.

Applying this equation to (
2.7
) yields the formal equation (
2.8
).

\blacksquare

Remark 2.4.

(1
∘
) In (
2.11
), a double integrator
M({\mathrm{d}}y,{\mathrm{d}}s)M({\mathrm{d}}y^{\prime},{\mathrm{d}}s^{\prime})
is introduced. We reformulate this in (
2.7
) using
X(z_{2},t^{\prime}){\mathrm{d}}z_{2}M({\mathrm{d}}z_{1},{\mathrm{d}}t^{\prime})
, as Lebesgue integration with respect to
X(z_{2},t^{\prime}){\mathrm{d}}z_{2}
offers significant technical advantages, such as straightforward interchange of the order of integration. The moment bound in (
2.26
), which serves as a central tool in the proof of Theorem
2.11
, provides another example of this convenience.

(2
∘
) Proposition
5.7
provides a rigorous formulation of (
2.12
).

(3
∘
) A review of the derivation for (
2.7
) reveals that its counterpart for the one-dimensional SHE is mathematically rigorous. Specifically, for any function-valued solution
Y
to the one-dimensional SHE, given by (
1.1
) with
d=1
using an arbitrary order-
1
constant
\Lambda>0
, the following equation holds:

\displaystyle Y(x,t)^{2}-\int_{s=0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}}Q_{t-s}(x,y)^{2}\Lambda Y(y,s)^{2}{\mathrm{d}}y{\mathrm{d}}s

\displaystyle\quad=[Q_{t}Y_{0}(x)]^{2}+2\int_{t^{\prime}=0}^{t}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}\biggl(\prod_{j=1}^{2}Q_{t-t^{\prime}}(x,z_{j})\biggr)Y(z_{2},t^{\prime}){\mathrm{d}}z_{2}M_{Y}({\mathrm{d}}z_{1},{\mathrm{d}}t^{\prime}).

(2.13)

Here,
Q_{t}(x,y)
,
x,y\in{\mathbb{R}}
, are the transition densities of one-dimensional standard Brownian motion, and
M_{Y}({\mathrm{d}}x,{\mathrm{d}}t)
denotes the martingale measure
\Lambda^{1/2}Y(x,t)\xi({\mathrm{d}}x,{\mathrm{d}}t)
.
Furthermore, with appropriate adjustments to the contributions from covariation measures, (
2.7
) and (
2.11
) apply rigorously to any well-defined mild form of a general stochastic heat equation, including the approximate solutions
X_{\varepsilon}
to (
1.4
). Proposition
3.2
examines the details for the approximate solutions, focusing specifically on the formulation in (
2.11
).

\blacklozenge

Remark 2.5 (Subcritical regime).

The above argument can be adapted to formally derive Gaussian fluctuations in the subcritical case, where
\Lambda_{\varepsilon}
is replaced by
2\pi\lambda_{0}/\log{\varepsilon}^{-1}
for
\lambda_{0}\in(0,1)
. This derivation can also be made rigorous using the methods presented in this paper, and the corresponding proof is expected to be comparatively simpler.

In this subcritical regime, the formal equation in (
2.8
) becomes

\displaystyle[P_{t}X_{0}(x)]^{2}

\displaystyle=(1-\lambda_{0})X(x,t)^{2}

\displaystyle\quad-2\int_{t^{\prime}=0}^{t}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}\biggl(\prod_{j=1}^{2}P_{t-t^{\prime}}(x,z_{j})\biggr)X(z_{2},t^{\prime}){\mathrm{d}}z_{2}{M}({\mathrm{d}}z_{1},{\mathrm{d}}t^{\prime}).

(2.14)

Hence, multiplying both sides by
\Lambda=0+
yields

0=(1-\lambda_{0})\Lambda X(x,t)^{2}+0\Longrightarrow{\langle}M,M{\rangle}({\mathrm{d}}x,{\mathrm{d}}t)=0\Longrightarrow{M}({\mathrm{d}}x,{\mathrm{d}}t)=0,

and (
2.14
) simplifies to

[P_{t}X_{0}(x)]^{2}=(1-\lambda_{0})X(x,t)^{2}\Longrightarrow X(x,t)^{2}{\mathrm{d}}x{\mathrm{d}}t=\frac{1}{1-\lambda_{0}}[P_{t}X_{0}(x)]^{2}{\mathrm{d}}x{\mathrm{d}}t.

The final equation indicates that the covariation measure of
\Lambda^{-1/2}M({\mathrm{d}}x,{\mathrm{d}}t)
is deterministic. This martingale measure is thus expected to be Gaussian; see
[
65
, Proposition 2.10, p.302]
for a general theorem of this property.

\blacklozenge

Remark 2.6 (Supercritical regime).

The mathematical justification for the heuristic argument in Heuristic
2.3
and Remark
2.5
relies fundamentally on the tightness of the approximate solutions and their associated covariation measures. Consequently, extending this approach to the supercritical regime, where
\Lambda_{\varepsilon}
is replaced by
2\pi\lambda_{0}/\log{\varepsilon}^{-1}
for
\lambda_{0}\in(1,\infty)
, raises immediate questions about its mathematical validity. Moreover, although the same formal calculation yields (
2.14
) in this setting, taking formal expectations to eliminate the stochastic integral forces the condition
{\mathbb{E}}[X(x,t)^{2}]=0
, as the alternative leads to the contradictory inequality
[P_{t}X_{0}(x)]^{2}<0
. Rather than indicating an absolute barrier, this degenerate limiting behavior may simply reflect an intrinsic limitation of applying these solutions to the supercritical case. For precise asymptotic expansions of second moments of approximate solutions approached via time-changed additive functionals, see
[
23
]
; for a separate study establishing local extinction and collapse of mass via discrete polymers and stochastic heat flow, see
[
7
]
.
\blacklozenge

2.3
Precise equations for the limiting covariation measures

In this subsection, we present the precise statements of our main theorems (Theorems
2.7
,
2.10
, and
2.11
). The proof organization is provided at the end.

The first step in making the heuristic argument in Section
2.2
rigorous is to establish process-level weak convergence for the approximate solutions
X_{\varepsilon}
defined in (
1.4
), reformulated as measure-valued processes, along with their covariation measures. To achieve this goal, our formulation below highlights three key features:

•

We temper these measures at infinity so that a suitable state space exists on which the Skorokhod representation theorem applies, thereby greatly streamlining the subsequent asymptotic expansions.

•

Alongside weak convergence, we derive uniform-in-
\varepsilon
moment bounds; these extend naturally to the limiting process, which is the version stated in the theorem below for brevity. While similar weak convergence results and moment bounds appear in Nakashima
[
57
]
, our approach differs by utilizing the continuous approximation model (
1.4
) rather than a fully discrete one. Related results also appear in Tsai
[
64
]
. Furthermore, the moment bounds established here involve new formulations essential to our main theorems and yield improved Hölder exponents.

•

We assume that the initial condition
X_{0}(\cdot)
is bounded and nonnegative. Nonnegativity is imposed primarily for analytical convenience. The main theorems presented in this subsection remain fully valid without it, with the sole exception of (
2.25
) and (
2.26
). Because (
2.25
) and (
2.26
) serve only as auxiliary bounds, their absence does not affect the extensions of our main formulas. We elaborate on this in Remark
2.14
.

Theorem
2.7
offers a minimal summary of our main results from the first step. In this theorem,
C_{E}[0,\infty)
denotes the space of continuous functions from
[0,\infty)
into a topological space
E
, equipped with the topology of uniform convergence on compact sets. Additionally,
\mathcal{M}_{f}(E)
refers to the space of (nonnegative) finite measures on
E
, endowed with the topology of weak convergence. The pre-limit processes are then taken to be the following
\mathcal{M}_{f}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})
- and
\mathcal{M}_{f}({\mathbb{R}}^{4})
-valued processes, constructed from the approximate solutions
X_{\varepsilon}
to (
1.4
) and the associated martingale measure
M_{\varepsilon}
defined in (
1.12
):

\displaystyle X^{\sharp}_{\varepsilon}({\mathrm{d}}x,t)\,{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm def}}{{=}}}\,\psi(x)X_{\varepsilon}({\mathrm{d}}x,t)=\psi(x)X_{\varepsilon}(x,t){\mathrm{d}}x,\quad x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2},

(2.15)

\displaystyle{\langle}M_{\varepsilon},M_{\varepsilon}{\rangle}^{\sharp}({\mathrm{d}}x^{\prime},{\mathrm{d}}x,{\mathrm{d}}t)\,{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm def}}{{=}}}\,\psi^{\otimes 2}(x^{\prime},x){\langle}M_{\varepsilon},M_{\varepsilon}{\rangle}({\mathrm{d}}x^{\prime},{\mathrm{d}}x,{\mathrm{d}}t),\quad(x^{\prime},x,t)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\times{\mathbb{R}}_{+}.

(2.16)

Here,
\psi
is an auxiliary function introduced for convenience. Its explicit form, along with the associated tensor product
\psi^{\otimes 2}
, is given below:

\displaystyle\psi(x)\,{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm def}}{{=}}}\,(1+\lvert x\rvert^{10})^{-1},\quad\psi^{\otimes 2}(x^{\prime},x)\,{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm def}}{{=}}}\,\psi(x^{\prime})\psi(x).

(2.17)

Consequently,
X_{\varepsilon}^{\sharp}({\mathrm{d}}x,0)
is a finite measure, even though we only assume that
X_{0}(\cdot)
is bounded. Additionally, the covariation measure of
M_{\varepsilon}
refers to the unique measure
{\langle}M_{\varepsilon},M_{\varepsilon}{\rangle}({\mathrm{d}}x^{\prime},{\mathrm{d}}x,{\mathrm{d}}t)
on
\mathscr{B}({\mathbb{R}}^{2}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\times{\mathbb{R}}_{+})
such that, for any
f\in{\mathscr{C}}^{0}_{pd}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})
,

\biggl(\int_{0}^{T}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}f(x)M_{\varepsilon}({\mathrm{d}}x,{\mathrm{d}}t)\biggr)^{2}-\int_{0}^{T}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}f(x^{\prime})f(x){\langle}M_{\varepsilon},M_{\varepsilon}{\rangle}({\mathrm{d}}x^{\prime},{\mathrm{d}}x,{\mathrm{d}}t)

is a continuous martingale. Finally, beyond the weak formulation (
1.13
) that expresses
M_{\varepsilon}
in terms of
X_{\varepsilon}
, the following theorem also considers the mild formulation (specifically, the Duhamel form) as well as the mild formulation integrated against a test function, as captured by the next two equations:

\displaystyle X_{\varepsilon}(x^{\prime},T)

\displaystyle=P_{T}X_{0}(x^{\prime})+\int_{0}^{T}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}P_{T-t}(x^{\prime},x)M_{\varepsilon}({\mathrm{d}}x,{\mathrm{d}}t),\quad x^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2},

(2.18a)

\displaystyle\begin{split}X_{\varepsilon}(f,T)&=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}f(x)P_{T}X_{0}(x){\mathrm{d}}x+\int_{0}^{T}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}P_{T-t}f(x)M_{\varepsilon}({\mathrm{d}}x,{\mathrm{d}}t),\quad f\in{\mathscr{C}}_{pd}^{0}({\mathbb{R}}^{2}),\end{split}

(2.18b)

Here,
{\mathscr{C}}_{pd}^{k}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})
denotes the subspace of
{\mathscr{C}}^{k}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})
specified at the end of Section
1
.

Theorem 2.7.

Fix
\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}
, and let
\varrho\in{\mathscr{C}}_{c}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})
be a mollifier. Recall that
\phi\in{\mathscr{C}}_{c}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})
is the probability density defined in (
1.18
). Given a bounded nonnegative initial condition
X_{0}(\cdot)
independent of
{\varepsilon}
, the solutions
X_{\varepsilon}
to (
1.4
) satisfy the following properties:

(1
∘
)
The family of laws of
\{(X^{\sharp}_{\varepsilon}({\mathrm{d}}x,t),\langle M_{\varepsilon},M_{\varepsilon}\rangle^{\sharp}({\mathrm{d}}x^{\prime},{\mathrm{d}}x,(0,t])\}_{{\varepsilon}>0}
converges in law as probability measures on the Polish space
C_{\mathcal{M}_{f}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})}[0,\infty)\times C_{\mathcal{M}_{f}({\mathbb{R}}^{2}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2})}[0,\infty)
as
{\varepsilon}\to 0
.

Let
(X^{\sharp}({\mathrm{d}}x,t),\mu^{\sharp}({\mathrm{d}}x^{\prime},{\mathrm{d}}x,(0,t]))
denote the limiting process, and define

\displaystyle X({\mathrm{d}}x,t)\,{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm def}}{{=}}}\,\psi(x)^{-1}X^{\sharp}({\mathrm{d}}x,t),\quad x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2},

(2.19)

\displaystyle X(f,t)\,{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm def}}{{=}}}\,\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}f(x)X({\mathrm{d}}x,t),\quad f\in{\mathscr{C}}^{0}_{pd}({\mathbb{R}}^{2}),

\displaystyle\mu({\mathrm{d}}x^{\prime},{\mathrm{d}}x,{\mathrm{d}}t)\,{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm def}}{{=}}}\,\psi^{\otimes 2}(x^{\prime},x)^{-1}\mu^{\sharp}({\mathrm{d}}x^{\prime},{\mathrm{d}}x,{\mathrm{d}}t),\quad(x^{\prime},x,t)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\times{\mathbb{R}}_{+}.

(2.20)

Then
M
is uniquely determined as an orthogonal martingale measure by the relation

\displaystyle X(f,T)

\displaystyle=X(f,0)+\int_{0}^{T}X\left(\frac{\Delta f}{2},t\right){\mathrm{d}}t+\int_{0}^{T}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}f(x)M({\mathrm{d}}x,{\mathrm{d}}t),\quad f\in{\mathscr{C}}^{2}_{pd}({\mathbb{R}}^{2}),

(2.21)

where
\mu
and the covariation measure
{\langle}M,M{\rangle}({\mathrm{d}}x,{\mathrm{d}}t)
satisfy

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}g(x,x){\langle}M,M{\rangle}({\mathrm{d}}x,{\mathrm{d}}t)=\int_{0}^{T}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}g(x^{\prime},x)\mu({\mathrm{d}}x^{\prime},{\mathrm{d}}x,{\mathrm{d}}t),\;\;g\in{\mathscr{C}}^{0}_{pd}({\mathbb{R}}^{2}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2}).

(2.22)

Moreover, the following weak formulation of the mild form holds:

\displaystyle X(f,T)

\displaystyle=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}f(x)P_{T}X_{0}(x){\mathrm{d}}x+\int_{0}^{T}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}P_{T-t}f(x)M({\mathrm{d}}x,{\mathrm{d}}t),\quad f\in{\mathscr{C}}^{0}_{pd}({\mathbb{R}}^{2}).

(2.23)

(2
∘
)
The covariation measure
{\langle}M,M{\rangle}({\mathrm{d}}x,{\mathrm{d}}t)
satisfies the following measure identity:

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}[{\langle}M,M{\rangle}({\mathrm{d}}x,{\mathrm{d}}t)]=\mathring{{\rm DBG}}\mbox{}^{\beta}_{t}X_{0}^{\otimes 2}(x){\mathrm{d}}x{\mathrm{d}}t,\quad(x,t)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\times{\mathbb{R}}_{+},

(2.24)

where
X_{0}^{\otimes 2}(z_{1},z_{2}){\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm def}}{{=}}}X_{0}(z_{1})X_{0}(z_{2})
, and
\mathring{{\rm DBG}}\mbox{}^{\beta}_{t}F(x)
is defined in (
1.15
). Furthermore, the following measure inequalities hold for all
0\leq T_{1}\leq T_{2}\leq T<\infty
,
p\in(0,1)
,
a,a^{\prime}\in(0,T]
, and
y,y^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
:

\displaystyle\int_{t=T_{1}}^{T_{2}}\int_{t^{\prime}=t}^{T_{2}}{\mathbb{E}}[{\langle}M,M{\rangle}({\mathrm{d}}x^{\prime},{\mathrm{d}}t^{\prime}){\langle}M,M{\rangle}({\mathrm{d}}x,{\mathrm{d}}t)]{\mathrm{d}}t^{\prime}{\mathrm{d}}t

\displaystyle\qquad\leq C(\lambda,\phi,\lVert X_{0}\rVert_{\infty},T,p)(T_{2}-T_{1})^{2-p}{\mathrm{d}}x^{\prime}{\mathrm{d}}x,\quad(x^{\prime},x)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2},

(2.25)

\displaystyle\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}P_{a^{\prime}}(y^{\prime},z^{\prime})P_{a}(y,z){\mathbb{E}}[X_{\infty}({\mathrm{d}}z^{\prime},t)X_{\infty}({\mathrm{d}}z,t){\langle}M_{\infty},M_{\infty}{\rangle}({\mathrm{d}}x,{\mathrm{d}}t)]

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\frac{C(\lambda,\phi,\lVert X_{0}\rVert_{\infty},T,p)}{\min\{a^{\prime},a\}^{p}}{\mathrm{d}}x{\mathrm{d}}t,\quad(x,t)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\times(0,T].

(2.26)

Remark 2.8.

As in the case of
M_{\varepsilon}
, the following martingale property provides a characterization of
{\langle}M,M{\rangle}({\mathrm{d}}x,{\mathrm{d}}t)
: for any
f\in{\mathscr{C}}^{0}_{pd}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})
,

\biggl(\int_{0}^{T}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}f(x)M({\mathrm{d}}x,{\mathrm{d}}t)\biggr)^{2}-\int_{0}^{T}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}f(x)^{2}{\langle}M,M{\rangle}({\mathrm{d}}x,{\mathrm{d}}t)

is a continuous martingale.

\blacklozenge

The following immediate corollary of Theorem
2.7
reproduces the known second-moment formulas, which were first established in
[
8
]
in a different yet equivalent form.

Corollary 2.9.

For all
f_{1},f_{2}\in{\mathscr{C}}_{pd}^{0}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})
, we have

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\biggl[\prod_{j=1}^{2}X_{T}(f_{j},T)\biggr]

\displaystyle=\prod_{j=1}^{2}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}P_{T}X_{0}(w_{j})f_{j}(w_{j}){\mathrm{d}}w_{j}+\int_{0}^{T}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}\prod_{j=1}^{2}P_{T-t}f(x)\mathring{{\rm DBG}}\mbox{}^{\beta}_{t}X_{0}^{\otimes 2}(x){\mathrm{d}}x{\mathrm{d}}t.

Proof.
The required identity is obtained more directly by applying the moment duality (
2.1
) (see the proof of (
2.24
)), although it can also be derived a posteriori from Theorem
2.7
as follows. Expressing
X(f_{j},T)
as the sum of a deterministic component and a stochastic integral via the mild formulation (
2.23
), we expand
\prod_{j=1}^{2}X(f_{j},T)
and take expectations. By (
2.25
), the cross term vanishes in expectation. Consequently, since the covariation measure determines the quadratic variation of products of stochastic integrals with respect to
M(\mathrm{d}x,\mathrm{d}t)
, we get

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\biggl[\prod_{j=1}^{2}X(f_{j},T)\biggr]=\prod_{j=1}^{2}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}P_{T}X_{0}(w_{j})f_{j}(w_{j}){\mathrm{d}}w_{j}+{\mathbb{E}}\biggl[\int_{0}^{T}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}\prod_{j=1}^{2}P_{T-t}f_{j}(x){\langle}M,M{\rangle}({\mathrm{d}}x,{\mathrm{d}}t)\biggr].

The required equality follows upon applying (
2.24
) to the last expectation.

\blacksquare

Our proof of weak convergence in Theorem
2.7
(1
∘
) aims specifically to establish tightness. The unique determination of
\mu
in (
2.22
) rests on the fact that the quadratic variation of a continuous local martingale
N
is the unique continuous, finite variation process
A
with zero initial condition for which
N^{2}-A
remains a continuous local martingale. Therefore, establishing uniqueness for the limit in Theorem
2.7
reduces to identifying the limit law of
X_{\varepsilon}
as
{\varepsilon}\to 0
, which follows from Tsai’s axiomatic characterization
[
64
]
. Independently of this full convergence, all proofs in this paper applies along any convergent subsequence
\{(X^{\sharp}_{{\varepsilon}_{n}}({\mathrm{d}}x,t),{\langle}M_{{\varepsilon}_{n}},M_{{\varepsilon}_{n}}{\rangle}^{\sharp}({\mathrm{d}}x^{\prime},{\mathrm{d}}x,(0,t]))\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
as
{\varepsilon}_{n}\to 0
. Thus, our results apply to the corresponding limiting process, denoted by
\{(X^{\sharp}_{\infty}({\mathrm{d}}x,t),{\langle}M_{\infty},M_{\infty}{\rangle}^{\sharp}({\mathrm{d}}x^{\prime},{\mathrm{d}}x,(0,t]))\}
, which we use throughout the proofs and for future reference. Analogues of the processes
(X,\mu)
defined in (
2.19
) and (
2.20
) can also be identified. In particular, the limiting measure
\mu_{\infty}
associated with
\mu
is uniquely determined as the covariation measure
{\langle}M_{\infty},M_{\infty}{\rangle}
of the orthogonal martingale measure
M_{\infty}
of the limiting solution
X_{\infty}
.

The following theorem offers a rigorous counterpart to the formal asymptotic expansions discussed in Section
2.2
, using a weak formulation to absorb pointwise singularities.

Theorem 2.10.

The covariation measure
\langle M,M\rangle
of the limiting martingale measure
M
in Theorem
2.7
satisfies the following identity for all
T\in(0,\infty)
and
f(x,t)\in{\mathscr{C}}_{pd}^{1}({\mathbb{R}}^{2}\times[0,T])
:

\displaystyle\int_{t=0}^{T}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}\mathfrak{L}f(x,t,T)\langle M,M{\rangle}({\mathrm{d}}x,{\mathrm{d}}t)

\displaystyle\quad=\int_{t=0}^{T}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}f(x,t)[P_{t}X_{0}(x)]^{2}{\mathrm{d}}x{\mathrm{d}}t

\displaystyle\quad\quad+2\int_{t^{\prime}=0}^{T}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}\int_{t=t^{\prime}}^{T}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}f(x,t)\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}\biggl(\prod_{j=1}^{2}P_{t-t^{\prime}}(x,z_{j})\biggr)X({\mathrm{d}}z_{2},t^{\prime}){\mathrm{d}}x{\mathrm{d}}t{M}({\mathrm{d}}z_{1},{\mathrm{d}}t^{\prime}).

(2.27)

Here,
\mathfrak{L}
refers to the following integro-multiplication operator:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{L}f(x,t,T)

\displaystyle\,{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm def}}{{=}}}-\frac{1}{2\pi}\biggl(\frac{\log(T-t)}{2}+\log 2+\lambda-\frac{\gamma_{\sf EM}}{2}-\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}\phi(y^{\prime})\phi(y)\log\rvert y^{\prime}-y\rvert{\mathrm{d}}y^{\prime}{\mathrm{d}}y\biggr)f(x,t)

\displaystyle\quad\,-\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}\int_{t^{\prime}=0}^{T-t}[f(x-x^{\prime},t+t^{\prime})-f(x,t)]P_{t^{\prime}}(x^{\prime})P_{t^{\prime}}(x^{\prime}){\mathrm{d}}t^{\prime}{\mathrm{d}}x^{\prime},

(2.28)

where
(\lambda,\phi)
is as given in (
1.5
) and (
1.18
), and
\gamma_{\mathsf{E}\mathsf{M}}
denotes the Euler–Mascheroni constant. All the stochastic integrals and Lebesgue integrals in (
2.27
) and (
2.28
) are well-defined.

By formulating the integro-multiplication operator
\mathfrak{L}
, Theorem
2.10
identifies the crucial second-order term absent from formal expansions. However, the presence of
\mathfrak{L}
also accounts for why Theorem
2.10
does not explicitly capture the covariation measure
\langle M,M\rangle({\mathrm{d}}x,{\mathrm{d}}t)
. Furthermore, the analytical properties of
\mathfrak{L}
, discussed in more detail below, make it difficult to invert directly when deriving explicit formulas for
\langle M,M\rangle({\mathrm{d}}x,{\mathrm{d}}t)
, so the next theorem manages to bypass this technical issue. Ultimately, owing to its direct link to the expansions in Section
2.2
, Theorem
2.10
is essential for deriving our full formulas and provides a general formulation that may prove useful for studying related SPDEs.

The individual components of
\mathfrak{L}
reveal both classical and new characteristics of the two-dimensional SHE at criticality. On the classical side, the derivation of
\mathfrak{L}
(Sections
4.1
and
4.2
) closely parallels one step in deriving the asymptotic Green function expansion recalled in (
2.3
) (see especially the final display in
[
23
, Section 4.4]
). In particular, the multiplication symbol in
\mathfrak{L}
nearly reproduces the constant
(\log\beta)/2
from (
1.17
), where
\beta
parametrizes the second moments at criticality. The new characteristics stem from the logarithmic multiplier
-(4\pi)^{-1}\log(T-t)
and the
integral part
(the iterated integral term in (
2.28
) with respect to
{\mathrm{d}}t^{\prime}{\mathrm{d}}x^{\prime}
). While this logarithmic multiplier may account for the remaining
-\gamma_{\mathsf{E}\mathsf{M}}/2
in (
1.17
), using the formula
\gamma_{\mathsf{E}\mathsf{M}}=-\int_{0}^{\infty}{\mathrm{e}}^{-t}\log t{\mathrm{d}}t

[
51
, (1.3.19), p. 8]
(see also Remark
7.2
), it is inherently time-reversed (appearing with
T-t
). This time reversal complicates the picture and highlights the essential role of the integral part. Further delicate properties of
\mathfrak{L}
are discussed in Section
5.1
.

The following theorem gives a full account of the covariation measure
{\langle}M,M{\rangle}({\mathrm{d}}x,{\mathrm{d}}t)
, thereby completely determining the semimartingale characteristics of the two-dimensional SHE at criticality via (
2.21
) and (
2.23
).

Theorem 2.11.

Let
\mathring{{\rm DBG}}\mbox{}^{\beta}_{t}(x;z_{1},z_{2})
and
{\mathfrak{s}}^{\beta}(\cdot)
be as defined in (
1.15
) and (
1.16
), respectively. The covariation measure
{\langle}M,M{\rangle}({\mathrm{d}}x,{\mathrm{d}}t)
of the martingale measure
M
described in Theorem
2.7
admits the following formulas: for all
T\in(0,\infty)
and

f(x,t)\in{\mathscr{C}}^{0}_{pd}({\mathbb{R}}^{2}\times[0,T])
,

\displaystyle\int_{t=0}^{T}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}f(x,t)\langle M,M\rangle({\mathrm{d}}x,{\mathrm{d}}t)

\displaystyle\quad=\int_{t=0}^{T}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}f(x,t)\mathring{{\rm DBG}}\mbox{}^{\beta}_{t}X^{\otimes 2}_{0}(x){\mathrm{d}}x{\mathrm{d}}t

\displaystyle\quad\quad+2\int_{t^{\prime}=0}^{T}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}\int_{t=t^{\prime}}^{T}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}f(x,t)\mathring{{\rm DBG}}\mbox{}^{\beta}_{t-t^{\prime}}(x;z_{1},z_{2})X({\mathrm{d}}z_{2},t^{\prime}){\mathrm{d}}x{\mathrm{d}}tM({\mathrm{d}}z_{1},{\mathrm{d}}t^{\prime}),

(2.29)

and

\displaystyle\int_{t=0}^{T}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}f(x,t)\langle M,M\rangle({\mathrm{d}}x,{\mathrm{d}}t)

\displaystyle\quad=\int_{t=0}^{T}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}f(x,t)\mathring{{\rm DBG}}\mbox{}^{\beta}_{t}X^{\otimes 2}_{0}(x){\mathrm{d}}x{\mathrm{d}}t

\displaystyle\quad\quad+2\int_{t=0}^{T}\int_{t^{\prime}=0}^{t}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}f(x,t)\mathring{{\rm DBG}}\mbox{}^{\beta}_{t-t^{\prime}}(x;z_{1},z_{2})X({\mathrm{d}}z_{2},t^{\prime}){\mathrm{d}}xM({\mathrm{d}}z_{1},{\mathrm{d}}t^{\prime}){\mathrm{d}}t,

(2.30)

where

X_{0}^{\otimes}(z_{1},z_{2})\,{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm def}}{{=}}}\,X_{0}(z_{1})X_{0}(z_{2}).

Additionally, for all
f(x)\in{\mathscr{C}}^{0}_{pd}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{t=0}^{T}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}f(x)\langle M,M\rangle({\mathrm{d}}x,{\mathrm{d}}t)

\displaystyle\quad=\int_{t=0}^{T}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}f(x)\mathring{{\rm DBG}}\mbox{}^{\beta}_{t}X_{0}^{\otimes 2}(x){\mathrm{d}}x{\mathrm{d}}t

\displaystyle\quad\quad+2\int_{s=0}^{T}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}P_{s/2}f(y){\mathfrak{s}}^{\beta}(s)\int_{t^{\prime}=0}^{T-s}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}\int_{t=s}^{T-t^{\prime}}\prod_{j=1}^{2}P_{t-s}(y,z_{j})

\displaystyle\quad\quad\times{\mathrm{d}}tX({\mathrm{d}}z_{2},t^{\prime})M({\mathrm{d}}z_{1},{\mathrm{d}}t^{\prime}){\mathrm{d}}y{\mathrm{d}}s.

(2.31)

All the stochastic and Lebesgue integrals in (
2.29
)–(
2.31
) are well-defined.

Remark 2.12.

(1
∘
) Feynman–Kac-type representations involving
\mathring{{\rm DBG}}\mbox{}^{\beta}_{t}(x;z_{1},z_{2})
, along with related analytic properties, have been developed in
[
19
,
22
]
.

(2
∘
) The bound in (
2.26
) provides a key tool for extending (
2.29
)–(
2.31
) to more general test functions. However, (
2.31
) should remain restricted to time-independent test functions.

\blacklozenge

Theorem
2.11
highlights both differences and connections with several established results, some of which were discussed in Section
1
. We also emphasize the following observations:

•

Equation (
1.14
) reflects the quadratic structure of the covariation measure in the solution
X
, analogous to (
1.11
), via
X_{0}^{\otimes 2}
and the integrator
X({\mathrm{d}}z_{2},t^{\prime})M({\mathrm{d}}z_{1},{\mathrm{d}}t^{\prime})
.

•

Combined with the weak formulation (see (
2.21
)), our formulas for the covariation measures as in (
2.30
) provide precise descriptions of the martingale problem for the two-dimensional SHE at criticality.
However, in standard settings, such as finite-dimensional SDEs or Dawson–Watanabe superprocesses, the semimartingale characteristics are specified by non-random functions or functionals of the state. In contrast, specifying the coefficients in our setting inherently requires Itô integration with respect to the martingale measure
M({\mathrm{d}}z_{1},{\mathrm{d}}t^{\prime})
, reflecting a covariation structure that is fundamentally stochastic and path-dependent.

A further aspect of Theorem
2.11
concerns the singularities of the kernel
\mathring{{\rm DBG}}\mbox{}^{\beta}_{t}(x;z_{1},z_{2})
, which render changes in the order of integration delicate. Consequently, although formulas (
2.29
)–(
2.31
) differ only in the order of integration of their final terms, our analysis shows that any such rearrangement requires thorough justification. In particular, this motivates the restriction on (
2.31
) highlighted in Remark
5.3
(2
∘
). If, instead, one assumes full interchangeability of the order of integration without rigorous justification, the covariation measure
\langle M,M\rangle({\mathrm{d}}x,{\mathrm{d}}t)
appears to have the density

\displaystyle\mathring{{\rm DBG}}\mbox{}^{\beta}_{t}X_{0}^{\otimes 2}(x)+2\int_{t^{\prime}=0}^{t}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}\mathring{{\rm DBG}}\mbox{}^{\beta}_{t-t^{\prime}}(x;z_{1},z_{2})X({\mathrm{d}}z_{2},t^{\prime})M({\mathrm{d}}z_{1},{\mathrm{d}}t^{\prime})

(2.32)

with respect to the Lebesgue measure
{\mathrm{d}}x{\mathrm{d}}t
. Standard arguments would then imply that the martingale
\int_{t=0}^{T}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}f(x)M({\mathrm{d}}x,{\mathrm{d}}t)
in the weak formulation (
2.21
) can be represented as a stochastic integral with respect to a space-time white noise. However, beyond the technical obstacles that the current analytical bounds encounter in justifying this interchange, the presence of space-time white noise as the driving noise seems overly idealized given the singular nature of the two-dimensional SHE at criticality, making its validity implausible. See
[
40
]
for a rigorous proof of this obstruction.

Although formal, the density in (
2.32
) provides an analogue of the right-hand side of (
2.13
) for the one-dimensional SHE, replacing, in particular, the product of heat kernels with the special kernel
\mathring{{\rm DBG}}\mbox{}^{\beta}_{t}(x;z_{1},z_{2})
. This transition from the Gaussian regime to the delta-Bose gas, along with that from the subcritical additive SHE discussed in Section
1.1
, further illustrates the heuristics introduced in
[
20
,
22
]
. These heuristics reflect the fundamental interplay between the Laplacian and noise at criticality in two dimensions, motivating the Feynman–Kac formulas for the two-body delta-Bose gas and the related analytical properties established in those works.

Some additional connections between the formulas in Theorem
2.11
and existing literature are outlined below:

•

The explicit semimartingale characteristics of the two-dimensional SHE at criticality also bear a close structural resemblance to those found in the superprocess within a random environment constructed by Mytnik
[
54
]
, despite the significant differences in frameworks and methods between
[
54
]
and the present work. One of the main theorems from
[
54
]
establishes the existence of
Z\in C_{\mathcal{M}_{f}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}[0,\infty)
for any dimension
d\geq 1
, such that for every
f\in{\mathscr{C}}_{c}^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
, the process

\displaystyle M_{Z}(f,T)\,{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm def}}{{=}}}\,Z(f,T)-Z(f,0)-\int_{0}^{T}Z\left(\frac{\Delta f}{2},t\right){\mathrm{d}}t

(2.33)

is an
L^{2}
-martingale, where
Z(f,T)\,{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm def}}{{=}}}\,\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}f(x)Z({\mathrm{d}}x,T)
, and
\Delta
now refers to the
d
-dimesnsional Laplacian. Equation (
2.33
), in turn, induces a martingale measure given by
M_{Z}({\mathrm{d}}x,{\mathrm{d}}t)
, so that
M_{Z}(f,T)=\int_{t=0}^{T}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}f(x)M_{Z}({\mathrm{d}}x,{\mathrm{d}}t)
. Furthermore, the covariation measure of
M_{Z}({\mathrm{d}}x,{\mathrm{d}}t)
is non-orthogonal and given by the following formula: for all nonnegative functions
g\in{\mathscr{C}}_{b}({\mathbb{R}}^{d}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d})
,

\displaystyle\int_{t=0}^{T}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}g(x^{\prime},x)\langle M_{Z},M_{Z}\mathcal{{\rangle}}({\mathrm{d}}x^{\prime},{\mathrm{d}}x,{\mathrm{d}}t)

\displaystyle\quad=\int_{t=0}^{T}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}g(x,x)Z({\mathrm{d}}x,t){\mathrm{d}}t+\int_{t=0}^{T}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}g(x^{\prime},x)\kappa(x^{\prime},x)Z({\mathrm{d}}x^{\prime},t)Z({\mathrm{d}}x,t){\mathrm{d}}t.

(2.34)

Here,
\kappa(x^{\prime},x)
is a deterministic, bounded, and continuous function characterizing this process
Z
. Notably, the original results in
[
54
]
are formulated in far greater generality, extending well beyond the specific triple
(\frac{\Delta}{2},{\mathbb{R}}^{d},{\mathscr{C}}_{c}^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{d}))
presented here.

The close structural resemblance mentioned above emerges when comparing (
2.30
) and (
2.34
), though the martingale measure
M_{Z}({\mathrm{d}}x,{\mathrm{d}}t)
is not orthogonal. For (
2.34
), taking
\kappa\equiv 0
reduces
Z
to the standard superprocess, while the random environment generates an additional quadratic term involving
Z({\mathrm{d}}x^{\prime},t)Z({\mathrm{d}}x,t)
. This mirrors the right-hand side of (
2.30
): the first term alone defines a Gaussian covariation measure, as in the subcritical regime, but at criticality, noise additionally produces a quadratic term involving
X({\mathrm{d}}z_{2},t^{\prime})M({\mathrm{d}}z_{1},{\mathrm{d}}t^{\prime})
.

•

Nakashima
[
57
]
recently provided an alternative description of the covariation measure for the martingale measure in the critical two-dimensional stochastic heat flow, along with additional results discussed in Section
1
. To restate this description from
[
