57
]
, let
{\rm SHF}^{\prime}({\mathrm{d}}x,0;{\mathrm{d}}y,t)
represent the limiting stochastic heat flow from
[
57
]
, and define

X^{\prime}(f,T)\,{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm def}}{{=}}}\,\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}X_{0}(x)f(y){\rm SHF}^{\prime}({\mathrm{d}}x,0;{\mathrm{d}}y,T),

with
M^{\prime}(f)=M^{\prime}(f,T)
denoting the martingale part. (We do not directly identify
X^{\prime}
with the limiting process
X
from Theorem
2.7
, as parameter identification remains unresolved in the corresponding limiting schemes; see
[
57
, Remark 1.10]
.) Specifically, Nakashima
[
57
]
proves that for all nonnegative
X_{0}(\cdot)\in{\mathscr{C}}_{c}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})
,
f\in{\mathscr{C}}^{2}_{b}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})
, and
0<T_{0}<\infty
, the following convergence in probability holds:

\displaystyle\sup_{0\leq T\leq T_{0}}\left|\frac{4\pi}{\log\eta^{-1}}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}\big[X^{\prime}(P_{\eta}(x,\cdot),t\big)f(x)\big]^{2}{\mathrm{d}}x{\mathrm{d}}t-\langle M^{\prime}(f),M^{\prime}(f)\rangle_{T}\right|\xrightarrow[\eta\searrow 0]{{\mathbb{P}}}0,

(2.35)

where
P_{\eta}(x,y)
, as defined in (
1.10
), denotes the transition probability of two-dimensional standard Brownian motion.

The descriptions in Theorem
2.11
and (
2.35
) differ in key analytical aspects. In particular, (
2.35
) provides an approximation scheme for the covariation measure, whereas Theorem
2.11
yields an exact expression that explicitly incorporates the dependence on the parameter
\beta
from (
1.17
).

The following theorem presents an analogue of Nakashima’s description discussed above. We provide a brief sketch of the proof, as the technical argument requires only straightforward modifications of the methods developed later.

Theorem 2.13.

Assume that
X_{0}(\cdot)\in{\mathscr{C}}_{b}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})
, and let
X
denote the associated limiting process from Theorem
2.7
. Then for all
f\in{\mathscr{C}}_{pd}^{1}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})
and
0<T_{0}<\infty
,

\displaystyle\sup_{0\leq T\leq T_{0}}\biggl|\frac{4\pi}{\log\eta^{-1}}\!\int_{0}^{T}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}\big[X\big(P_{\eta}(x,\cdot),t\big)f(x)\big]^{2}{\mathrm{d}}x{\mathrm{d}}t-\int_{0}^{T}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}f(x)^{2}\langle M,M\rangle({\mathrm{d}}x,{\mathrm{d}}t)\bigg|\xrightarrow[\eta\searrow 0]{{\mathbb{P}}}0,

where the limit denotes convergence in probability.

Sketch of the proof of Theorem
2.13
.

It is enough to show that the convergence in probability under consideration holds for each fixed
0\leq T\leq T_{0}
, rather than uniformly for all
0\leq T\leq T_{0}
. To justify this weaker requirement, we use the following fact along with a Helly-selection-type argument: if a sequence of non-decreasing functions
f_{n}:[a,b]\to{\mathbb{R}}
converges pointwise to a continuous function
f
, then the convergence is actually uniform.

The central step in this proof is to demonstrate that, for any fixed
T>0
, the left-hand side of the following equation converges in probability to the required limit as
\eta\to 0
:

\displaystyle\frac{4\pi}{\log\eta^{-1}}\!\int_{t=0}^{T}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}X\big(P_{\eta}(x,\cdot),t\big)^{2}f(x)^{2}{\mathrm{d}}x{\mathrm{d}}t=\sum_{k=1}^{3}I^{k}_{\ref{appsq}}(T),

(2.36)

where we define

\displaystyle I^{1}_{\ref{appsq}}(T)

\displaystyle\,{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm def}}{{=}}}\,\frac{4\pi}{\log\eta^{-1}}\int_{t=0}^{T}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}\langle P_{\eta}(x,\cdot),P_{t}X_{0}{\rangle}^{2}f(x)^{2}{\mathrm{d}}x{\mathrm{d}}t,

(2.37a)

\displaystyle I^{2}_{\ref{appsq}}(T)

\displaystyle\,{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm def}}{{=}}}\,\frac{8\pi}{\log\eta^{-1}}\int_{t=0}^{T}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}\int_{t^{\prime}=0}^{t}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}X\big(P_{t-t^{\prime}+\eta}(x,\cdot),t^{\prime}\big)P_{t-t^{\prime}+\eta}(x,x^{\prime})

\displaystyle\quad\times M({\mathrm{d}}x^{\prime},{\mathrm{d}}t^{\prime})f(x)^{2}{\mathrm{d}}x{\mathrm{d}}t,

(2.37b)

\displaystyle I^{3}_{\ref{appsq}}(T)

\displaystyle\,{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm def}}{{=}}}\,\frac{4\pi}{\log\eta^{-1}}\int_{t=0}^{T}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}\int_{t^{\prime}=0}^{t}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}P_{t-t^{\prime}+\eta}(x,x^{\prime})^{2}{\langle}M,M{\rangle}({\mathrm{d}}x^{\prime},{\mathrm{d}}t^{\prime})f(x)^{2}{\mathrm{d}}x{\mathrm{d}}t.

(2.37c)

Here, the decomposition in (
2.36
) is derived by first applying the smooth test function
P_{\eta}(x,\cdot)
to (
2.23
), which produces the following analogue of (
2.7
):

\displaystyle X\big(P_{\eta}(x,\cdot),t\big)^{2}

\displaystyle=\langle P_{\eta}(x,\cdot),P_{t}X_{0}{\rangle}^{2}+2\int_{t^{\prime}=0}^{t}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}X(P_{t-t^{\prime}+\eta}(x,\cdot),t^{\prime})P_{t-t^{\prime}+\eta}(x,x^{\prime})M({\mathrm{d}}x^{\prime},{\mathrm{d}}t^{\prime})

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{t^{\prime}=0}^{t}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}P_{t-t^{\prime}+\eta}(x,x^{\prime})^{2}{\langle}M,M{\rangle}({\mathrm{d}}x^{\prime},{\mathrm{d}}t^{\prime}).

Then, integrating both sides of this equation against
(4\pi/\log\eta^{-1})f(x)^{2}{\mathrm{d}}x{\mathrm{d}}t
for
(x,t)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\times[0,T]
yields (
2.36
).

To complete the proof, it suffices to show that for all
1\leq j\leq 2
,
I^{j}_{\ref{appsq}}(T)
converges to zero in probability as
\eta\searrow 0
, ensuring that the required limit is determined by the behavior of
I^{3}_{\ref{appsq}}(T)
. The argument for
j=2
involves first justifying the use of the stochastic Fubini theorem, so that
{\langle}M,M{\rangle}({\mathrm{d}}x^{\prime},{\mathrm{d}}t^{\prime}){\mathrm{d}}x{\mathrm{d}}t
in
I^{2}_{\ref{appsq}}(T)
can be interchanged with
{\mathrm{d}}x{\mathrm{d}}t{\langle}M,M{\rangle}({\mathrm{d}}x^{\prime},{\mathrm{d}}t^{\prime})
for any
fixed

\eta\in(0,1)
. Here, the verification uses Walsh’s
L^{2}
-condition
[
65
, Theorem 2.6, pp.296–297]
and the moment bounds from Theorem
2.7
. These moment bounds are also essential for proving the required zero limits. To identify the limit in probability of
I^{3}_{\ref{appsq}}(T)
, we consider the following calculation:

\displaystyle\lim_{\eta\searrow 0}I^{3}_{\ref{appsq}}(T)

\displaystyle=\lim_{\eta\searrow 0}\frac{4\pi}{\log\eta^{-1}}\int_{t^{\prime}=0}^{T}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}\biggl(\int_{t=t^{\prime}}^{T}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}P_{t-t^{\prime}+\eta}(x,x^{\prime})^{2}{\mathrm{d}}x{\mathrm{d}}t\biggr)f(x^{\prime})^{2}{\langle}M,M{\rangle}({\mathrm{d}}x^{\prime},{\mathrm{d}}t^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\lim_{\eta\searrow 0}\int_{t^{\prime}=0}^{T}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}\biggl(\frac{4\pi}{\log\eta^{-1}}\frac{\log(T+\eta)-\log\eta}{4\pi}\biggr)f(x^{\prime})^{2}{\langle}M,M{\rangle}({\mathrm{d}}x^{\prime},{\mathrm{d}}t^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\int_{t^{\prime}=0}^{T}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}f(x^{\prime})^{2}{\langle}M,M{\rangle}({\mathrm{d}}x^{\prime},{\mathrm{d}}t^{\prime}),

where the first equality replaces
f(x)^{2}
by
f(x^{\prime})^{2}
, as justified by (
7.9
). This limit suffices to establish the theorem.

\blacksquare

We conclude this discussion of the main theorems with a remark on the use of signed initial conditions and their connection to the critical two-dimensional stochastic heat flow:

Remark 2.14 (Signed initial conditions and SHF).

The main results presented above, with the sole exception of the auxiliary bounds (
2.25
) and (
2.26
), naturally extend to general signed bounded initial conditions (see (1
∘
) below). Further extensions apply to the stochastic heat flow setting discussed in Section
1.2
; see (2
∘
) for the precise setting.

(1
∘
) To extend the exact formulas of covariation measures to a general signed, bounded initial condition
X_{0}
, consider a polar decomposition approach using three solutions:
X^{+}
,
X^{-}
, and
\overline{X}\,{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm def}}{{=}}}\,X^{+}+X^{-}
. They correspond to the bounded nonnegative initial conditions
X_{0}^{+}
,
X_{0}^{-}
, and
\overline{X}_{0}=\lvert X_{0}\rvert=X_{0}^{+}+X_{0}^{-}
, respectively, where
X_{0}^{\pm}
denote the positive and negative parts of
X_{0}
. The associated martingale measures are denoted by
M^{+}
,
M^{-}
for
X^{+}
and
X^{-}
, and the martingale measure
\overline{M}
of
\overline{X}
satisfies
\overline{M}=M^{+}+M^{-}
. By linearity, the solution and martingale measure for the two-dimensional SHE at criticality with initial condition
X_{0}
are recovered in the following way:

X\;{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm def}}{{=}}}\,X^{+}-X^{-},\quad M\;{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm def}}{{=}}}\,M^{+}-M^{-}.

Then
M
satisfies (
2.24
) since by bilinearity,

\displaystyle\langle M,M{\rangle}({\mathrm{d}}x,{\mathrm{d}}t)

\displaystyle=2\sum_{\varsigma\in\{\pm\}}{\langle}M^{\varsigma},M^{\varsigma}{\rangle}({\mathrm{d}}x,{\mathrm{d}}t)-{\langle}\overline{M},\overline{M}{\rangle}({\mathrm{d}}x,{\mathrm{d}}t).

(2.38)

We also have extensions of Theorems
2.10
and
2.11
to the pair
(X,M)
under consideration since

\displaystyle X({\mathrm{d}}z_{2},t^{\prime})M({\mathrm{d}}z_{1},{\mathrm{d}}t^{\prime})

\displaystyle=2\sum_{\varsigma\in\{\pm\}}X^{\varsigma}({\mathrm{d}}z_{2},t^{\prime})M^{\varsigma}({\mathrm{d}}z_{1},{\mathrm{d}}t^{\prime})-\overline{X}({\mathrm{d}}z_{2},t^{\prime})\overline{M}({\mathrm{d}}z_{1},{\mathrm{d}}t^{\prime}).

(2.39)

By virtue of (
2.38
) and (
2.39
), directly extending (
2.25
) and (
2.26
) to
(X,M)
is unnecessary for applications to the exact formulas for covariation measures, thereby avoiding the need to treat the resulting cross-terms directly.

To make the above argument rigorous, note that the algebraic identities in (
2.38
) and (
2.39
) hold analogously for the approximate solutions
(X_{\varepsilon},M_{\varepsilon})
, whose tightness is justified by
[
35
, Proposition 2.4, p. 107]
, as well as the tightness behind Theorem
2.7
(1
∘
). Consequently, tightness of the approximations alone is sufficient to pass to the exact formulas.

(2
∘
) The present framework applies to any countable set of initial times
\mathcal{S}\subset[0,\infty)
rather than restricting to
s=0
. Given any collection of bounded initial conditions
\{X_{s}^{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N},s\in\mathcal{S}}
, the tightness result in Theorem
2.7
extends to the corresponding families of approximate solutions
\{X^{k}_{s,t,\varepsilon}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N},t\geq s}
constructed on a shared probability space, again justified by
[
35
, Proposition 2.4, p. 107]
. Applying a density argument and the Kakutani–Riesz–Markov representation theorem (the latter as in Tsai’s axiomatic characterization
[
64
]
) then connects this construction to the stochastic heat flow for all
s\in\mathcal{S}
and
t\geq s
.

Dependencies among Section

Organization of the proofs of Theorems
2.7
,
2.10
, and
2.11
.
The proof of Theorem
2.7
(1
∘
), excluding the uniqueness of limits, (
2.22
), and (
2.23
), relies on the more refined moment bounds and tightness estimates in Theorems
3.4
and
3.5
. These finer theorems are stated in Section
3
alongside the baseline stochastic analytic framework. To prioritize our main quantitative results (Theorems
2.10
and
2.11
), the proofs of Theorems
3.4
,
3.5
, and Theorem
2.7
(2
∘
) are deferred to Section
6
. The arguments for Theorems
2.10
and

build on Corollary
3.6
, which introduces distinct approximate solutions that need not share the same space-time white noise (see Convention
3.7
for detailed conventions). Section

then concludes the proof of Theorem
2.7
(1
∘
) and presents the proof of Theorem
2.10
. Susequently, Section

is dedicated to the proof of Theorem
2.11
. Finally, Section
7
provides the necessary heat kernel expansions and integral bounds underlying the proofs in Sections
4
and
5
.

Setup for proofs.
From this point forward, we employ the following specialized notation:

•

We focus exclusively on
\Lambda_{\varepsilon}=\Lambda_{\varepsilon}(\lambda)
, as defined in (
1.5
) for any
\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}
, under the following condition:

\displaystyle{{\varepsilon}_{\ref{def:ovl}}(\lambda)}\in(0,1/4)\quad\mbox{and}\quad\Lambda_{\varepsilon}\geq\pi/\log{\varepsilon}^{-1},\;\forall\;{\varepsilon}\in(0,{{\varepsilon}_{\ref{def:ovl}}(\lambda)}].

(2.40)

•

For higher-order iterated integrals, we often use the shorthand
\int_{x}f(x){\mathrm{d}}x
in place of
\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}f(x){\mathrm{d}}x
. Similar notation applies for integrals over other spatial variables in
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
.

•

In the context of two-body dynamics, the following notation is commonly used to represent products of quantities and measures, typically indicating that the factors operate in the same time interval:

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}a_{2}\\
a_{1}\end{bmatrix}_{\times}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}a_{1}\times a_{2},\quad\begin{bmatrix}{\mathrm{d}}x\\
{\mathrm{d}}y\end{bmatrix}_{\otimes}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}{\mathrm{d}}x{\mathrm{d}}y.

(2.41)

3
Expansions and tightness of approximate solutions

In this section, we provide a baseline stochastic analytic framework for solutions to (
1.1
), formulate a precise version of the associated decomposition in (
2.11
), and state Theorems
3.4
and
3.5
mentioned in the above organization of proofs.

3.1
Pathwise properties and moment bounds

The following proposition provides explicit expressions for the covariation measures, specifies nonnegativity, and presents essential moment bounds for the approximate solutions. Most results in part (2
∘
) follow from general theorems in
[
29
,
55
]
.

Proposition 3.1.

Let
{\varepsilon}\in(0,{{\varepsilon}_{\ref{def:ovl}}(\lambda)}]
.

(1
∘
)
The mollified noise
\xi_{\varepsilon}
, as defined in (
1.6
), admits an explicit covariation measure given by the following formula:

\displaystyle{\langle}\xi_{\varepsilon},\xi_{\varepsilon}{\rangle}({\mathrm{d}}x^{\prime},{\mathrm{d}}x,{\mathrm{d}}s)=\phi_{\varepsilon}(x^{\prime}-x){\mathrm{d}}x^{\prime}{\mathrm{d}}x{\mathrm{d}}s,\quad(x^{\prime},x,s)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\times{\mathbb{R}}_{+}.

(3.1)

Here,
\{\phi_{\varepsilon}\}
is an approximation to the identity defined as follows:

\displaystyle\phi_{\varepsilon}(x)\,{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm def}}{{=}}}\,{\varepsilon}^{-2}\phi({\varepsilon}^{-1}x),\quad\phi(x)\,{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm def}}{{=}}}\,\int_{y}\varrho(y)\varrho(y-x){\mathrm{d}}y,

(3.2)

and
\phi
is an even probability density with compact support.
In particular, the martingale measure
M_{\varepsilon}
, defined in (
1.12
), satisfies the following formulas: for all nonnegative
g(x^{\prime},x,s)
,

\displaystyle\int_{s=0}^{\infty}\int_{x}\int_{x^{\prime}}{g}(x^{\prime},x,s){\langle}M_{\varepsilon},M_{\varepsilon}{\rangle}({\mathrm{d}}x^{\prime},{\mathrm{d}}x,{\mathrm{d}}s)

\displaystyle\quad=\Lambda_{\varepsilon}\int_{s=0}^{\infty}\int_{x}\int_{x^{\prime}}{g}(x^{\prime},x,s)X_{\varepsilon}(x^{\prime},s)X_{\varepsilon}(x,s)\phi_{\varepsilon}(x^{\prime}-x){\mathrm{d}}x^{\prime}{\mathrm{d}}x{\mathrm{d}}s

(3.3a)

\displaystyle\quad=\Lambda_{\varepsilon}\int_{s=0}^{\infty}\int_{x}\int_{\widetilde{x}}{g}({\varepsilon}\widetilde{x}+x,x,s)X_{\varepsilon}({\varepsilon}\widetilde{x}+x,s)X_{\varepsilon}(x,s)\phi(\widetilde{x}){\mathrm{d}}\widetilde{x}{\mathrm{d}}x{\mathrm{d}}s.

(3.3b)

(2
∘
)
Given any bounded, nonnegative initial condition
X_{0}(\cdot)
, the mild formulation (
2.18
) admits a unique pathwise strong solution
X_{\varepsilon}
taking values in
C_{{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm tem}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})}[0,\infty)
; moreover,
X_{\varepsilon}(x,t)
remains nonnegative for all
t\geq 0
and
x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
, and becomes strictly positive for all
t>0
and
x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
whenever
\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}X_{0}(x){\mathrm{d}}x>0
. Here,
{\mathscr{C}}_{\rm tem}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})
denotes a topological space defined by the following norms:

\displaystyle\lVert f\rVert_{\infty,\gamma}\,{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm def}}{{=}}}\,\sup_{x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}\lvert f(x)\rvert{\mathrm{e}}^{-\gamma x},\quad\gamma\in(0,\infty).

(3.4)

Specifically, this space consists of all functions
f\in{\mathscr{C}}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})
satisfying
\lVert f\rVert_{\infty,\gamma}<\infty
for all
\gamma>0
, with the topology induced by the norms in (
3.4
). Moreover, the following moment bounds hold:

\displaystyle\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\sup_{x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}{\mathbb{E}}[X_{\varepsilon}(x,t)^{p}]<\infty,\quad\forall\;T,p\in(0,\infty),

(3.5)

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\biggl[\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\sup_{x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}\lvert X_{\varepsilon}(x,t)\rvert^{p}{\mathrm{e}}^{-\gamma\lvert x\rvert}\biggr]<\infty,\quad\forall\;T,p,\gamma,\in(0,\infty).

(3.6)

Proof.
(1
∘
) We first show that
\phi
is even and has compact support. First, the even parity follows upon applying the change of variables
y^{\prime}=y-x
to the definition of
\phi
in (
3.2
). To see the compact support property of
\phi
, choose
M>0
such that
\varrho(\widetilde{y})=0
for all
\lvert\widetilde{y}\rvert>M
. Then
\varrho(y)\varrho(y-x)=0
for all
y\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
and
\lvert x\rvert>2M
since
\lvert y\rvert\leq M
implies
\rvert y-x\rvert\geq\lvert x\rvert-\lvert y\rvert>M
.

To prove (
3.1
), let
f\in{\mathscr{C}}_{c}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})
, and consider

\displaystyle\int_{s=0}^{t}\int_{x}f(x)\xi_{\varepsilon}({\mathrm{d}}x,{\mathrm{d}}s)=\int_{s=0}^{t}\int_{\widetilde{x}}\left(\int_{x}f(x)\varrho_{\varepsilon}(x-\widetilde{x}){\mathrm{d}}x\right)\xi({\mathrm{d}}\widetilde{x},{\mathrm{d}}s),

(3.7)

which follows from the definition (
1.6
) of
\xi_{\varepsilon}
and the stochastic Fubini theorem for stochastic integrals with respect to space-time white noise (cf.
[
30
, Theorem 2.4.1, pp.82–83]
or
[
65
, Theorem 2.6, pp.296–297]
). By (
3.7
), we have

\displaystyle\left\langle\int_{s=0}^{\cdot}\int_{x}f(x)\xi_{\varepsilon}({\mathrm{d}}x,{\mathrm{d}}s),\int_{s=0}^{\cdot}\int_{x}f(x)\xi_{\varepsilon}({\mathrm{d}}x,{\mathrm{d}}s)\right\rangle_{t}

\displaystyle\quad=\int_{s=0}^{t}\int_{\widetilde{x}}\left(\int_{x^{\prime}}f(x^{\prime})\varrho_{\varepsilon}(x^{\prime}-\widetilde{x}){\mathrm{d}}x^{\prime}\right)\left(\int_{x}f(x)\varrho_{\varepsilon}(x-\widetilde{x}){\mathrm{d}}x\right){\mathrm{d}}\widetilde{x}{\mathrm{d}}s

\displaystyle\quad=\int_{s=0}^{t}\int_{x}\int_{x^{\prime}}f(x^{\prime})f(x)\left(\int_{\widetilde{x}}\varrho_{\varepsilon}(x^{\prime}-\widetilde{x})\varrho_{\varepsilon}(x-\widetilde{x}){\mathrm{d}}\widetilde{x}\right){\mathrm{d}}x^{\prime}{\mathrm{d}}x{\mathrm{d}}s

\displaystyle\quad=\int_{s=0}^{t}\int_{x}\int_{x^{\prime}}f(x^{\prime})f(x)\left(\int_{y}\varrho_{\varepsilon}(y)\varrho_{\varepsilon}(x+y-x^{\prime}){\mathrm{d}}y\right){\mathrm{d}}x^{\prime}{\mathrm{d}}x{\mathrm{d}}s\quad\mbox{($\because y=x^{\prime}-\widetilde{x}$)}

\displaystyle\quad=\int_{s=0}^{t}\int_{x}\int_{x^{\prime}}f(x^{\prime})f(x)\phi_{\varepsilon}(x^{\prime}-x){\mathrm{d}}x^{\prime}{\mathrm{d}}x{\mathrm{d}}s,

(3.8)

where (
3.8
) uses the definition of
\phi_{\varepsilon}
in (
3.2
). The last equality is enough to prove (
3.1
).

(2
∘
) All properties stated in (2
∘
), except for nonnegativity, follow by applying general theorems from
[
29
,
55
]
. For convenience, we rely on
[
55
, Theorem 1.2, Proposition 1.8 (a), and Theorem A.1]
throughout. The required conditions are met by taking
k(x,y)\equiv\phi_{\varepsilon}(x-y)
. In particular, condition
(A)_{1}
in
[
55
, Theorem A.1]
is satisfied because, using the Fourier transform
\mathcal{F}f(z)\,{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm def}}{{=}}}\int\exp\{-2\pi{\mathtt{i}}z\cdot x\}f(x){\mathrm{d}}x
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}f(x)\phi_{\varepsilon}(x){\mathrm{d}}x=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}\mathcal{F}f(z)\lvert\mathcal{F}\varrho_{\varepsilon}(z)\rvert^{2}{\mathrm{d}}z,

(3.9)

\displaystyle\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}\frac{\lvert\mathcal{F}\varrho_{\varepsilon}(z)\rvert^{2}}{1+\lvert z\rvert^{2}}{\mathrm{d}}z<\infty,

(3.10)

\displaystyle\sum_{j,k=1}^{n}c_{j}\overline{c}_{k}\phi_{\varepsilon}(y_{j}-y_{k})=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}\sum_{j,k=1}^{n}c_{j}\overline{c}_{k}\varrho_{\varepsilon}(y+y_{j})\varrho_{\varepsilon}(y+y_{k}){\mathrm{d}}y\geq 0,

(3.11)

for any rapidly decreasing test function
f
and any
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
y_{1},\cdots,y_{n}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
, and
c_{1},\cdots,c_{n}\in\mathbb{C}
. Here, Plancherel’s theorem and (
3.2
)
provide (
3.9
). Moreover, (
3.10
) holds because
\varrho\in{\mathscr{C}}_{c}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})
by assumption, and (
3.11
) uses the definition of
\phi_{\varepsilon}
from (
3.2
).

Finally, the nonnegativity of
X_{\varepsilon}
and its strict positivity under the condition
\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}X_{0}(x){\mathrm{d}}x>0
follow directly from pathwise uniqueness. Specifically, in this framework, the Feynman–Kac representation (see, e.g.,
[
18
, p.129]
) produces an explicit solution that inherits nonnegativity from
X_{0}(\cdot)
and becomes strictly positive for all
t>0
whenever the initial mass is non-zero.

\blacksquare

3.2
Quasi-Itô expansion of covariation measures

The main result of this subsection, stated as Proposition
3.2
, formulates a precise version of the decomposition in (
2.11
) at the level of
{\varepsilon}>0
. We use a weak formulation by integrating over space and time with respect to a test function
f(x,t)
for
x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
and
0\leq t\leq T
. Thus, the decomposition in (
2.11
) is made precise through (
3.12
) and the following identification, utilizing rigorously defined functionals
I_{\varepsilon}^{j}(f,T)
,
1\leq j\leq 4
, introduced below in (
3.13
)–(
3.16
):

\displaystyle I_{\varepsilon}^{1}(f,T)+I_{\varepsilon}^{2}(f,T)

\displaystyle\longleftrightarrow X(x,t)^{2}-\int_{s=0}^{t}\int_{y}P_{t-s}(x,y)^{2}\Lambda X(y,s)^{2}{\mathrm{d}}y{\mathrm{d}}s,

\displaystyle-2I^{3}_{\varepsilon}(f,T)

\displaystyle\longleftrightarrow-2P_{t}X_{0}(x)\int_{s=0}^{t}\int_{y}P_{t-s}(x,y){M}({\mathrm{d}}y,{\mathrm{d}}s),

\displaystyle-2I^{4}_{\varepsilon}(f,T)

\displaystyle\longleftrightarrow-2\int_{s^{\prime}=0}^{t}\int_{y^{\prime}}\int_{s=0}^{s^{\prime}}\int_{y}P_{t-s^{\prime}}(x,y^{\prime})P_{t-s}(x,y){M}({\mathrm{d}}y,{\mathrm{d}}s){M}({\mathrm{d}}y^{\prime},{\mathrm{d}}s^{\prime}).

In particular, the functionals
I_{\varepsilon}^{j}(f,T)
employ either the standard or stochastic Fubini theorem, both of which allow the test function
f(x,t)
to regularize singularities in the limit.

Proposition 3.2.

For all
{\varepsilon}\in(0,{{\varepsilon}_{\ref{def:ovl}}(\lambda)}]
,
T>0
, and
f(x,t)\in{\mathscr{C}}_{pd}^{0}({\mathbb{R}}^{2}\times[0,T])
, it holds that

\displaystyle\int_{t=0}^{T}\int_{x}f(x,t)[P_{t}X_{0}(x)]^{2}{\mathrm{d}}x{\mathrm{d}}t=I_{\varepsilon}^{1}(f,T)+I_{\varepsilon}^{2}(f,T)-2I_{\varepsilon}^{3}(f,T)-2I_{\varepsilon}^{4}(f,T),

(3.12)

where

\displaystyle\begin{split}I^{1}_{{\varepsilon}}(f,T)&\,{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm def}}{{=}}}\,\int_{t=0}^{T}\int_{x}f(x,t)X_{\varepsilon}(x,t)^{2}{\mathrm{d}}x{\mathrm{d}}t-\int_{s=0}^{T}\int_{y}\int_{\widetilde{y}}\mathfrak{L}_{\varepsilon}^{1}f(\widetilde{y},y,s,T)X_{\varepsilon}(y,s)^{2}{\mathrm{d}}\widetilde{y}{\mathrm{d}}y{\mathrm{d}}s,\end{split}

(3.13)

\displaystyle\begin{split}I^{2}_{\varepsilon}(f,T)&\,{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm def}}{{=}}}\,-\int_{s=0}^{T}\int_{y}\int_{\widetilde{y}}\mathfrak{L}_{\varepsilon}^{1}f(\widetilde{y},y,s,T)[X_{\varepsilon}({\varepsilon}\widetilde{y}+y,s)-X_{\varepsilon}(y,s)]X_{\varepsilon}(y,s){\mathrm{d}}\widetilde{y}{\mathrm{d}}y{\mathrm{d}}s,\end{split}

(3.14)

\displaystyle\begin{split}I^{3}_{\varepsilon}(f,T)&\,{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm def}}{{=}}}\,\int_{s=0}^{T}\int_{y}\mathfrak{L}_{0}^{3}f(y,s,T){M}_{\varepsilon}({\mathrm{d}}y,{\mathrm{d}}s),\end{split}

(3.15)

\displaystyle\begin{split}I^{4}_{\varepsilon}(f,T)&\,{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm def}}{{=}}}\,\int_{s^{\prime}=0}^{T}\int_{y^{\prime}}\int_{s=0}^{s^{\prime}}\int_{y}\mathfrak{L}_{0}^{4}f(y^{\prime},y,s^{\prime},s,T){M}_{\varepsilon}({\mathrm{d}}y,{\mathrm{d}}s){M}_{\varepsilon}({\mathrm{d}}y^{\prime},{\mathrm{d}}s^{\prime}),\end{split}

(3.16)

and the integrands use the following integral transformations of
f(x,t)
:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{L}^{1}_{\varepsilon}f(\widetilde{y},y,s,T)

\displaystyle\,{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm def}}{{=}}}\;\mathring{\mathfrak{L}}^{1}_{\varepsilon}f(\widetilde{y},y,s,T)\phi(\widetilde{y}),

(3.17)

\displaystyle\mathring{\mathfrak{L}}^{1}_{\varepsilon}f(\widetilde{y},y,s,T)

\displaystyle\,{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm def}}{{=}}}\,\Lambda_{\varepsilon}\int_{t=s}^{T}\int_{x}f(x,t)P_{t-s}({\varepsilon}\widetilde{y}+y,x)P_{t-s}(x,y){\mathrm{d}}x{\mathrm{d}}t,

(3.18)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{L}_{0}^{3}f(y,s,T)

\displaystyle\,{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm def}}{{=}}}\,\int_{t=s}^{T}\int_{x}f(x,t)P_{t}X_{0}(x)P_{t-s}(x,y){\mathrm{d}}x{\mathrm{d}}t,

(3.19)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{L}_{0}^{4}f(y^{\prime},y,s^{\prime},s,T)

\displaystyle\,{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm def}}{{=}}}\,\int_{t=s^{\prime}}^{T}\int_{x}f(x,t)P_{t-s^{\prime}}(x,y^{\prime})P_{t-s}(x,y){\mathrm{d}}x{\mathrm{d}}t.

(3.20)

Proof.
We first derive the main equation of this proof.
By squaring both sides of (
2.18
) and rearranging, we have

\displaystyle[P_{t}X_{0}(x)]^{2}

\displaystyle=X_{\varepsilon}(x,t)^{2}-2P_{t}X_{0}(x)\int_{s=0}^{t}\int_{y}P_{t-s}(x,y)M_{\varepsilon}({\mathrm{d}}y,{\mathrm{d}}s)

\displaystyle\quad-\left(\int_{s=0}^{t}\int_{y}P_{t-s}(x,y)M_{\varepsilon}({\mathrm{d}}y,{\mathrm{d}}s)\right)^{2}.

Integrating both sides against
f(x,t)
over
x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
and
0\leq t\leq T
yields

\displaystyle\int_{t=0}^{T}\int_{x}f(x,t)[P_{t}X_{0}(x)]^{2}{\mathrm{d}}x{\mathrm{d}}t

\displaystyle\quad=\int_{t=0}^{T}\int_{x}f(x,t)X_{\varepsilon}(x,t)^{2}{\mathrm{d}}x{\mathrm{d}}t-2\int_{t=0}^{T}\int_{x}f(x,t)P_{t}X_{0}(x)\int_{s=0}^{t}\int_{y}P_{t-s}(x,y)M_{\varepsilon}({\mathrm{d}}y,{\mathrm{d}}s){\mathrm{d}}x{\mathrm{d}}t

\displaystyle\quad\quad-\int_{t=0}^{T}\int_{x}f(x,t)\left(\int_{s=0}^{t}\int_{y}P_{t-s}(x,y)M_{\varepsilon}({\mathrm{d}}y,{\mathrm{d}}s)\right)^{2}{\mathrm{d}}x{\mathrm{d}}t

\displaystyle\quad=\int_{t=0}^{T}\int_{x}f(x,t)X_{\varepsilon}(x,t)^{2}{\mathrm{d}}x{\mathrm{d}}t-2\int_{t=0}^{T}\int_{x}f(x,t)P_{t}X_{0}(x)\int_{s=0}^{t}\int_{y}P_{t-s}(x,y){M}_{\varepsilon}({\mathrm{d}}y,{\mathrm{d}}s){\mathrm{d}}x{\mathrm{d}}t

\displaystyle\quad\quad-2\int_{t=0}^{T}\int_{x}f(x,t)\int_{s^{\prime}=0}^{t}\int_{y^{\prime}}\int_{s=0}^{s^{\prime}}\int_{y}P_{t-s}(x,y)P_{t-s^{\prime}}(x,y^{\prime}){M}_{\varepsilon}({\mathrm{d}}y,{\mathrm{d}}s){M}_{\varepsilon}({\mathrm{d}}y^{\prime},{\mathrm{d}}s^{\prime}){\mathrm{d}}x{\mathrm{d}}t

\displaystyle\quad\quad-\Lambda_{\varepsilon}\int_{t=0}^{T}\int_{x}f(x,t)\int_{s=0}^{t}\int_{y}\int_{\widetilde{y}}P_{t-s}(x,{\varepsilon}\widetilde{y}+y)P_{t-s}(x,y)

\displaystyle\quad\quad\times X_{\varepsilon}({\varepsilon}\widetilde{y}+y,s)X_{\varepsilon}(y,s)\phi(\widetilde{y}){\mathrm{d}}\widetilde{y}{\mathrm{d}}y{\mathrm{d}}s{\mathrm{d}}x{\mathrm{d}}t.

(3.21)

Here, (
3.21
) is derived by applying Itô’s formula and (
3.3b
) to expand the squared process
(\int_{s=0}^{t}\int_{y}P_{t-s}(x,y){M}_{\varepsilon}({\mathrm{d}}y,{\mathrm{d}}s))^{2}
, as
\int_{s=0}^{s^{\prime}}\int_{y}P_{t-s}(x,y){M}_{\varepsilon}({\mathrm{d}}y,{\mathrm{d}}s)
, for
0\leq s^{\prime}\leq t
, forms an
L^{2}
-bounded martingale. This martingale property and
L^{2}
-integrability are ensured by (
3.3
), (
3.5
), and the estimate below, which employs the change of variables
\widetilde{y}=y^{\prime}-y
and the Chapman–Kolmogorov equation:

\displaystyle\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\sup_{x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}\int_{s=0}^{t}\int_{y}\int_{y^{\prime}}P_{t-s}(x,y^{\prime})P_{t-s}(x,y)\phi_{\varepsilon}(y^{\prime}-y){\mathrm{d}}y^{\prime}{\mathrm{d}}y{\mathrm{d}}s

\displaystyle\quad=\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\int_{s=0}^{t}\int_{\widetilde{y}}P_{2(t-s)}(\widetilde{y})\phi_{\varepsilon}(\widetilde{y}){\mathrm{d}}\widetilde{y}{\mathrm{d}}s\leq\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}t\lVert\phi_{\varepsilon}\rVert_{\infty}<\infty.

(3.22)

Equation (
3.21
) is the main equation of this proof, from which we will derive (
3.12
).

To proceed, we demonstrate that the right-hand sides of (
3.12
) and (
3.21
) are equal. To derive
I^{1}_{{\varepsilon}}(f,T)
and
I^{2}_{{\varepsilon}}(f,T)
from (
3.21
), we utilize the standard Fubini theorem to switch the order of integration in the last term of (
3.21
) from
{\mathrm{d}}\widetilde{y}{\mathrm{d}}y{\mathrm{d}}s{\mathrm{d}}x{\mathrm{d}}t
to
{\mathrm{d}}x{\mathrm{d}}t{\mathrm{d}}\widetilde{y}{\mathrm{d}}y{\mathrm{d}}s
:

\displaystyle\Lambda_{\varepsilon}\int_{t=0}^{T}\int_{x}f(x,t)\int_{s=0}^{t}\int_{y}\int_{\widetilde{y}}P_{t-s}(x,{\varepsilon}\widetilde{y}+y)P_{t-s}(x,y)

\displaystyle\quad\times X_{\varepsilon}({\varepsilon}\widetilde{y}+y,s)X_{\varepsilon}(y,s)\phi(\widetilde{y}){\mathrm{d}}\widetilde{y}{\mathrm{d}}y{\mathrm{d}}s{\mathrm{d}}x{\mathrm{d}}t

\displaystyle\quad=\int_{s=0}^{T}\int_{y}\int_{\widetilde{y}}\left(\Lambda_{\varepsilon}\int_{t=s}^{T}\int_{x}f(x,t)P_{t-s}({\varepsilon}\widetilde{y}+y,x)P_{t-s}(x,y){\mathrm{d}}x{\mathrm{d}}t\phi(\widetilde{y})\right)

\displaystyle\quad\quad\times X_{\varepsilon}({\varepsilon}\widetilde{y}+y,s)X_{\varepsilon}(y,s){\mathrm{d}}\widetilde{y}{\mathrm{d}}y{\mathrm{d}}s

\displaystyle\quad=\int_{s=0}^{T}\int_{y}\int_{\widetilde{y}}\mathfrak{L}_{\varepsilon}^{1}f(\widetilde{y},y,s,T)X_{\varepsilon}({\varepsilon}\widetilde{y}+y,s)X_{\varepsilon}(y,s){\mathrm{d}}\widetilde{y}{\mathrm{d}}y{\mathrm{d}}s,

(3.23)

where the last equality uses the definition (
3.17
) of
\mathfrak{L}_{\varepsilon}^{1}f(\widetilde{y},y,s,T)
. Hence, the sum of the first and last terms on the right-hand side of (
3.21
) equals
I^{1}_{{\varepsilon}}(f,T)+I^{2}_{{\varepsilon}}(f,T)
.

Next, to derive
I^{3}_{{\varepsilon}}(f,T)
and
I^{4}_{{\varepsilon}}(f,T)
from (
3.21
), we use the stochastic Fubini theorem (see
[
30
, Theorem 2.4.1, p.82]
or
[
65
, Theorem 2.6, pp.296–297]
). This theorem allows us to repeatedly interchange the order of integration in the second and third terms on the right-hand side of (
3.21
). The following two calculations give the details of these interchanges:

\displaystyle-2\int_{t=0}^{T}\int_{x}f(x,t)P_{t}X_{0}(x)\int_{s=0}^{t}\int_{y}P_{t-s}(x,y){M}_{\varepsilon}({\mathrm{d}}y,{\mathrm{d}}s){\mathrm{d}}x{\mathrm{d}}t

\displaystyle\quad=-2\int_{s=0}^{T}\int_{y}\left(\int_{t=s}^{T}\int_{x}f(x,t)P_{t}X_{0}(x)P_{t-s}(x,y){\mathrm{d}}x{\mathrm{d}}t\right){M}_{\varepsilon}({\mathrm{d}}y,{\mathrm{d}}s)

(3.24)

\displaystyle\quad=-2I^{3}_{\varepsilon}(f,T),

(3.25)

and

\displaystyle-2\int_{t=0}^{T}\int_{x}f(x,t)\int_{s^{\prime}=0}^{t}\int_{y^{\prime}}\int_{s=0}^{s^{\prime}}\int_{y}P_{t-s}(x,y)P_{t-s^{\prime}}(x,y^{\prime}){M}_{\varepsilon}({\mathrm{d}}y,{\mathrm{d}}s){M}_{\varepsilon}({\mathrm{d}}y^{\prime},{\mathrm{d}}s^{\prime}){\mathrm{d}}x{\mathrm{d}}t

\displaystyle\quad=-2\int_{s^{\prime}=0}^{T}\int_{y^{\prime}}\int_{t=s^{\prime}}^{T}\int_{x}f(x,t)\int_{s=0}^{s^{\prime}}\int_{y}P_{t-s}(x,y)P_{t-s^{\prime}}(x,y^{\prime}){M}_{\varepsilon}({\mathrm{d}}y,{\mathrm{d}}s){\mathrm{d}}x{\mathrm{d}}t

\displaystyle\quad\quad\times{M}_{\varepsilon}({\mathrm{d}}y^{\prime},{\mathrm{d}}s^{\prime})

(3.26)

\displaystyle\quad=-2\int_{s^{\prime}=0}^{T}\int_{y^{\prime}}\int_{s=0}^{s^{\prime}}\int_{y}\left(\int_{t=s^{\prime}}^{T}\int_{x}f(x,t)P_{t-s}(x,y)P_{t-s^{\prime}}(x,y^{\prime}){\mathrm{d}}x{\mathrm{d}}t\right){M}_{\varepsilon}({\mathrm{d}}y,{\mathrm{d}}s)

\displaystyle\quad\quad\times{M}_{\varepsilon}({\mathrm{d}}y^{\prime},{\mathrm{d}}s^{\prime})

(3.27)

\displaystyle\quad=-2I^{4}_{\varepsilon}(f,T).

(3.28)

The verification of the integrability conditions associated with (
3.24
), (
3.26
), and (
3.27
) is deferred until the end of the proof. By (
3.23
), (
3.25
), and (
3.28
), we have proved that the right-hand sides of (
3.12
) and (
3.21
) are equal.

To complete the proof, it remains to justify the previous uses of the stochastic Fubini theorem. We verify the relevant
L^{2}
-condition as stated in
[
65
, Theorem 2.6, pp.296–297]

(see also
[
30
, Corollary 2.4.3, p.90]
). By Itô’s isometry, (
3.3b
), and (
3.5
), this verification amounts to establishing the following three bounds, which justify (
3.24
), (
3.26
), and (
3.27
), respectively:

\displaystyle\int_{t=0}^{T}\int_{x}\lvert f(x,t)\rvert P_{t}X_{0}(x){\mathbb{E}}\biggl[\,\int_{s=0}^{t}\int_{y_{1}}\int_{y_{2}}P_{t-s}(x,y_{2})P_{t-s}(x,y_{1}){\langle}{M}_{\varepsilon},M_{\varepsilon}{\rangle}({\mathrm{d}}y_{2},{\mathrm{d}}y_{1},{\mathrm{d}}s)\biggr]{\mathrm{d}}x{\mathrm{d}}t<\infty,

\displaystyle\int_{t=0}^{T}\int_{x}\lvert f(x,t)\rvert{\mathbb{E}}\biggl[\,\int_{s^{\prime}=0}^{t}\int_{y_{1}^{\prime}}\int_{y_{2}^{\prime}}P_{t-s^{\prime}}(x,y_{2}^{\prime})P_{t-s^{\prime}}(x,y_{1}^{\prime})\biggl(\int_{s=0}^{s^{\prime}}\int_{y}P_{t-s}(x,y){M}_{\varepsilon}({\mathrm{d}}y,{\mathrm{d}}s)\biggr)^{2}

\displaystyle\quad\times{\langle}{M}_{\varepsilon},{M}_{\varepsilon}{\rangle}({\mathrm{d}}y_{2}^{\prime},{\mathrm{d}}y_{1}^{\prime},{\mathrm{d}}s^{\prime})\biggr]{\mathrm{d}}x{\mathrm{d}}t<\infty,

(3.29)

\displaystyle\int_{t=s^{\prime}}^{T}\int_{x}\lvert f(x,t)\rvert P_{t-s^{\prime}}(x,y^{\prime}){\mathbb{E}}\biggl[\,\int_{s=0}^{s^{\prime}}\int_{y_{1}}\int_{y_{2}}P_{t-s}(x,y_{2})P_{t-s}(x,y_{1}){\langle}{M}_{\varepsilon},{M}_{\varepsilon}{\rangle}({\mathrm{d}}y_{2},{\mathrm{d}}y_{1},{\mathrm{d}}s)\biggr]

\displaystyle\quad\times{\mathrm{d}}x{\mathrm{d}}t<\infty,

where
(s^{\prime},y^{\prime})
in the third bound ranges over
0<s^{\prime}<T
and
y^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
. To obtain the first and third of these bounds, we apply (
3.3a
), (
3.5
), and (
3.22
). Additionally, to obtain (
3.29
), we apply (
3.22
) again, now together with the following bound:

\displaystyle\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\sup_{0\leq s^{\prime}\leq t}\sup_{x,y_{2}^{\prime},y_{1}^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}{\mathbb{E}}\biggl[\biggl(\int_{s=0}^{s^{\prime}}\int_{y}P_{t-s}(x,y){M}_{\varepsilon}({\mathrm{d}}y,{\mathrm{d}}s)\biggr)^{2}X_{\varepsilon}(y_{2}^{\prime},s^{\prime})X_{\varepsilon}(y_{1}^{\prime},s^{\prime})\biggr]

\displaystyle\quad\lesssim\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\sup_{x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}{\mathbb{E}}\biggl[\biggl(\int_{s=0}^{t}\int_{y_{1}}\int_{y_{2}}P_{t-s}(x,y_{2})P_{t-s}(x,y_{1}){\langle}{M}_{\varepsilon},{M}_{\infty}{\rangle}({\mathrm{d}}y_{2},{\mathrm{d}}y_{1},{\mathrm{d}}s)\biggr)^{2}\,\biggr]^{1/2}

\displaystyle\quad\quad\times\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\sup_{y_{2}^{\prime},y_{1}^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[X_{\varepsilon}(y_{2}^{\prime},t)^{2}X_{\varepsilon}(y_{1}^{\prime},t)^{2}\right]^{1/2}<\infty,

where the first inequality follows from the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality and the Burkholder–Davis–Gundy inequality
[
61
, (4.1) Theorem, p.160]
, and the last inequality can be deduced from (
3.5
) and (
3.22
). We have justified all of the uses of the stochastic Fubini theorem in (
3.24
), (
3.26
), and (
3.27
). The proof is complete.

\blacksquare

The bound in (
3.22
) will be used frequently in the upcoming applications of the stochastic Fubini theorem when
{\varepsilon}>0
. For clarity, we state it separately in the following lemma.

Lemma 3.3.

For all
{\varepsilon}\in(0,{{\varepsilon}_{\ref{def:ovl}}(\lambda)}]
and
T>0
,

\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\sup_{x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}\int_{s=0}^{t}\int_{y}\int_{y^{\prime}}P_{t-s}(x,y^{\prime})P_{t-s}(x,y)\phi_{\varepsilon}(y^{\prime}-y){\mathrm{d}}y^{\prime}{\mathrm{d}}y{\mathrm{d}}s<\infty.

3.3
From tightness to Skorokhod’s representation

In this section, we refine Theorem
2.7
and introduce the framework for the Skorokhod representation, setting the stage for the proof of Theorem
2.10
alongside. The essential results focus on specific moment bounds detailed in the next theorem: parts (1
∘
) and (2
∘
) parallel (
2.25
) and (
2.26
), while part (3
∘
) corresponds to (
2.24
), though it is stated here in a form tailored for the forthcoming applications.

Theorem 3.4.

Let
{\varepsilon}\in(0,{{\varepsilon}_{\ref{def:ovl}}(\lambda)}]
,
p\in(0,1)
,
T\in(0,\infty)
and
x_{0}^{1},x_{0}^{0},\widetilde{x}_{0}^{1},\widetilde{x}_{0}^{0}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
.

(1
∘
)

For all
0\leq T_{1}\leq T_{2}\leq T
,

\displaystyle\int_{t=T_{1}}^{T_{2}}\int_{t^{\prime}=t}^{T_{2}}\Lambda_{\varepsilon}^{2}{\mathbb{E}}[X_{\varepsilon}(x_{0}^{1},t)X_{\varepsilon}(x_{0}^{1}+{\varepsilon}x_{0}^{0},t)X_{\varepsilon}(\widetilde{x}_{0}^{1},t^{\prime})X_{\varepsilon}(\widetilde{x}_{0}^{1}+{\varepsilon}\widetilde{x}_{0}^{0},t^{\prime})]{\mathrm{d}}t^{\prime}{\mathrm{d}}t

\displaystyle\quad\leq C(\lambda,\phi,\lVert X_{0}\rVert_{\infty},T,p)\times\biggl(\Lambda_{\varepsilon}\int_{s=0}^{T}\int_{\overline{z}}\phi(\overline{z})P_{2s}({\varepsilon}x_{0}^{0}-{\varepsilon}\overline{z}){\mathrm{d}}\overline{z}{\mathrm{d}}s+\Lambda_{\varepsilon}\biggr)

\displaystyle\quad\quad\times\biggl(\Lambda_{\varepsilon}\int_{s=0}^{T}\int_{\overline{z}}\phi(\overline{z})P_{2s}({\varepsilon}\widetilde{x}_{0}^{0}-{\varepsilon}\overline{z}){\mathrm{d}}\overline{z}{\mathrm{d}}s+\Lambda_{\varepsilon}\biggr)\times(T_{2}-T_{1})^{2-p}.

(3.30)

(2
∘
)

For all
t,a^{\prime},a\in(0,T]
,

\displaystyle\int_{z_{2}}\int_{z_{1}}P_{a^{\prime}}(\widetilde{x}_{0}^{1}+{\varepsilon}\widetilde{x}_{0}^{0},z_{2})P_{a}(\widetilde{x}_{0}^{1},z_{1})\Lambda_{\varepsilon}{\mathbb{E}}[X_{\varepsilon}(z_{1},t)X_{\varepsilon}(z_{2},t)X_{\varepsilon}(x_{0}^{1},t)X_{\varepsilon}(x_{0}^{1}+{\varepsilon}x_{0}^{0},t)]{\mathrm{d}}z_{1}{\mathrm{d}}z_{2}

\displaystyle\quad\leq\frac{C(\lambda,\phi,\lVert X_{0}\rVert_{\infty},T,p)}{\min\{a^{\prime},a\}^{p}}\times\biggl(\Lambda_{\varepsilon}\int_{s=0}^{T}\int_{\overline{z}}\phi(\overline{z})P_{2s}({\varepsilon}x_{0}^{0}-{\varepsilon}\overline{z}){\mathrm{d}}\overline{z}{\mathrm{d}}s+\Lambda_{\varepsilon}\biggr).

(3.31)

(3
∘
)

For all
t\in(0,T]
,

\displaystyle\Lambda_{\varepsilon}{\mathbb{E}}[X_{\varepsilon}(x_{0}^{1},t)X_{\varepsilon}(x_{0}^{1}+{\varepsilon}x_{0}^{0},t)]

\displaystyle\quad\leq C(\lambda,\phi,\lVert X_{0}\rVert_{\infty},T)\times\biggl(\Lambda_{\varepsilon}\int_{s=0}^{T}\int_{\overline{z}}\phi(\overline{z})P_{2s}({\varepsilon}x_{0}^{0}-{\varepsilon}\overline{z}){\mathrm{d}}\overline{z}{\mathrm{d}}s+\Lambda_{\varepsilon}\biggr).

(3.32)

Moreover, we have

\displaystyle\Lambda_{\varepsilon}\int_{s=0}^{T}\int_{\overline{z}}\phi(\overline{z})P_{2s}({\varepsilon}x_{0}^{0}-{\varepsilon}\overline{z}){\mathrm{d}}\overline{z}{\mathrm{d}}s

\displaystyle\leq C(\lambda,T)\left(\int_{\overline{z}}\phi(\overline{z})\lvert\log\lvert x_{0}^{0}-\overline{z}\rvert\rvert{\mathrm{d}}\overline{z}+1\right),

(3.33)

which leads to improved bounds in (1
∘
)–(3
∘
) when
x_{0}^{0}
and
\widetilde{x}_{0}^{0}
are both of order one as
{\varepsilon}\to 0
.

The proof of Theorem
3.4
, presented in Section
6.1
, builds upon the diagrammatic expansion method of
[
18
]
(see also
[
9
,
41
]
). The argument begins by extending the uniform-in-
{\varepsilon}
bound for
{\mathbb{E}}\big[\prod_{j=1}^{N}X_{\varepsilon}(x_{0}^{j},t)\big]
from
[
18
, Theorem 2.3]
, which holds for all integers
N\geq 3
given fixed initial conditions
x_{0}=(x_{0}^{1},\ldots,x_{0}^{N})\in\mathbb{R}^{2N}
with distinct components. However, new challenges emerge when initial points coalesce, such as in (
3.30
) and (
3.31
) where
x_{0}^{0}
and
\widetilde{x}_{0}^{0}
remain of order one as
{\varepsilon}\to 0
. This proximity induces new singularities that lead to divergent moments. Resolving these singularities requires both explicit renormalization via direct multiplication by
\Lambda_{\varepsilon}
and substantially more delicate analysis. By contrast, while the moments in (
3.32
) exhibit a comparable formulation, their second-moment structure renders them far more tractable.

Theorem
3.5
below offers a partial refinement of the weak convergence statement in Theorem
2.7
(1
∘
) by introducing two additional measure-valued processes. These processes perturb the covariation measures
{\langle}M_{\varepsilon},M_{\varepsilon}{\rangle}({\mathrm{d}}x^{\prime},{\mathrm{d}}x,{\mathrm{d}}s)
and arise from the terms
I^{j}_{\varepsilon}(f,T)
in Proposition
3.2
. Specifically, Theorem
3.5
addresses the joint tightness of four measure-valued processes defined by the following equations using
f_{0}\in{\mathscr{C}}_{b}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})
and
g_{0}\in{\mathscr{C}}_{b}({\mathbb{R}}^{2}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2})
:

\displaystyle X^{\sharp}_{\varepsilon}(f_{0},t)

\displaystyle\,{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm def}}{{=}}}\,X_{\varepsilon}(\psi f_{0},t),\quad\;\;\;\nu^{\sharp}_{\varepsilon}(f_{0},t)\,{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm def}}{{=}}}\,\nu_{\varepsilon}(\psi f_{0},t),

(3.34a)

\displaystyle\mu^{\sharp}_{\varepsilon}(g,t)

\displaystyle\,{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm def}}{{=}}}\,\mu_{\varepsilon}(\psi^{\otimes 2}g_{0},t),\quad\mathring{\mu}^{\sharp}_{\varepsilon}(g_{0},t)\,{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm def}}{{=}}}\,\mathring{\mu}_{\varepsilon}(\psi^{\otimes 2}g_{0},t).

(3.34b)

Here, we use the auxiliary function
\psi
from (
2.17
), as well as the following definitions, where we permit time-dependent test functions for subsequent applications:

\displaystyle X_{\varepsilon}(f,t)

\displaystyle\,{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm def}}{{=}}}\,\int_{x}f(x,t)X_{\varepsilon}(x,t){\mathrm{d}}x,

(3.35a)

\displaystyle\nu_{\varepsilon}(f,t)

\displaystyle\,{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm def}}{{=}}}\,\int_{s=0}^{t}\int_{x}f(x,s)\Lambda_{\varepsilon}X_{\varepsilon}(x,s)^{2}{\mathrm{d}}x{\mathrm{d}}s,

(3.35b)

\displaystyle\mu_{\varepsilon}(g,t)

\displaystyle\,{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm def}}{{=}}}\,\int_{s=0}^{t}\int_{x}\int_{x^{\prime}}g(x^{\prime},x,s){\langle}M_{\varepsilon},M_{\varepsilon}{\rangle}({\mathrm{d}}x^{\prime},{\mathrm{d}}x,{\mathrm{d}}s)

\displaystyle=\int_{s=0}^{t}\int_{x}\int_{\widetilde{x}}g({\varepsilon}\widetilde{x}+x,x,s)\Lambda_{\varepsilon}X_{\varepsilon}({\varepsilon}\widetilde{x}+x,s)X_{\varepsilon}(x,s)\phi(\widetilde{x}){\mathrm{d}}\widetilde{x}{\mathrm{d}}x{\mathrm{d}}s,

(3.35c)

\displaystyle\mathring{\mu}_{\varepsilon}(g,t)

\displaystyle\,{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm def}}{{=}}}\,\int_{s=0}^{t}\int_{x}\int_{\widetilde{x}}g(\widetilde{x},x,s)\Lambda_{\varepsilon}X_{\varepsilon}({\varepsilon}\widetilde{x}+x,s)X_{\varepsilon}(x,s)\phi(\widetilde{x}){\mathrm{d}}\widetilde{x}{\mathrm{d}}x{\mathrm{d}}s.

(3.35d)

Owing to the choice of
\psi
and (
3.5
), all the processes in (
3.34
) are finite-valued.

Theorem 3.5.

The family of joint laws of
\{(X^{\sharp}_{\varepsilon},\nu^{\sharp}_{\varepsilon},\mu^{\sharp}_{\varepsilon},\mathring{\mu}^{\sharp}_{\varepsilon})\}_{{\varepsilon}\in(0,{{\varepsilon}_{\ref{def:ovl}}(\lambda)}]}
is tight as a family of probability measures on
the Polish space
C_{\mathcal{M}_{f}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})}[0,\infty)\times C_{\mathcal{M}_{f}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})}[0,\infty)\times C_{\mathcal{M}_{f}({\mathbb{R}}^{4})}[0,\infty)\times C_{\mathcal{M}_{f}({\mathbb{R}}^{4})}[0,\infty)
, where
\mathcal{M}_{f}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
denotes the space of (nonnegative) finite measures equipped with the topology of weak convergence.

The proof of this theorem appears in Section
6.2
. As an immediate consequence, we obtain the following corollary, which is justified by Theorem
3.5
together with Prohorov’s theorem
[
35
, 2.2 Theorem, p.104]
and Skorokhod’s representation theorem
[
35
, 1.8 Theorem, p.102]
.

Corollary 3.6.

Given any sequence
\{{\varepsilon}_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
with
{\varepsilon}_{n}\in(0,{{\varepsilon}_{\ref{def:ovl}}(\lambda)}]
and
{\varepsilon}_{n}\searrow 0
, there exist a subsequence, still denoted by
\{{\varepsilon}_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
, and a change of the underlying probability space (making the space-time white noise
\xi
to possibly depend on
n\in\mathbb{N}
) such that

\displaystyle(X^{\sharp}_{n},\nu^{\sharp}_{n},\mu^{\sharp}_{n},\mathring{\mu}^{\sharp}_{n})\,{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm def}}{{=}}}\,(X^{\sharp}_{{\varepsilon}_{n}},\nu^{\sharp}_{{\varepsilon}_{n}},\mu^{\sharp}_{{\varepsilon}_{n}},\mathring{\mu}^{\sharp}_{{\varepsilon}_{n}}),\quad n\in\mathbb{N},

(3.36)

forms an almost-sure convergent sequence in
C_{\mathcal{M}_{f}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})}[0,\infty)\times C_{\mathcal{M}_{f}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})}[0,\infty)\times C_{\mathcal{M}_{f}({\mathbb{R}}^{4})}[0,\infty)\times C_{\mathcal{M}_{f}({\mathbb{R}}^{4})}[0,\infty)
. Hence, with probability one,

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\lim_{n\to\infty}(X^{\sharp}_{n},\nu^{\sharp}_{n},\mu^{\sharp}_{n},\mathring{\mu}^{\sharp}_{n})=(X^{\sharp}_{\infty},\nu^{\sharp}_{\infty},\mu^{\sharp}_{\infty},\mathring{\mu}^{\sharp}_{\infty})\end{split}

(3.37)

for some
(X^{\sharp}_{\infty},\nu^{\sharp}_{\infty},\mu^{\sharp}_{\infty},\mathring{\mu}^{\sharp}_{\infty})\in C_{\mathcal{M}_{f}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})}[0,\infty)\times C_{\mathcal{M}_{f}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})}[0,\infty)\times C_{\mathcal{M}_{f}({\mathbb{R}}^{4})}[0,\infty)\times C_{\mathcal{M}_{f}({\mathbb{R}}^{4})}[0,\infty)
.

Unless stated otherwise, the rest of this paper will use a fixed sequence as given in Corollary
3.6
.
Furthermore, for simplicity, we will often write “
n
” instead of the subscript “
{\varepsilon}_{n}
” from this point onward.

Although the quadruplets in (
3.36
) may no longer share the same space-time white noise, they still uniquely determine the corresponding processes without the “
\sharp
” notation. These processes, together with the extension to
n=\infty
, are explicitly defined via the following equations:

\displaystyle\big(X_{n}(f,t),\nu_{n}(f,t),\mu_{n}(g,t),\mathring{\mu}_{n}(g,t)\big)

\displaystyle\quad\,{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm def}}{{=}}}\,\big(X^{\sharp}_{n}(f/\psi,t),\nu^{\sharp}_{n}(f/\psi,t),\mu^{\sharp}_{n}(g/\psi\otimes\psi,t),\mathring{\mu}^{\sharp}_{n}(g/\psi\otimes\psi,t)\big),\quad n\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\},

(3.38)

where
f\in{\mathscr{C}}^{0}_{pd}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})
and
g\in{\mathscr{C}}^{0}_{pd}({\mathbb{R}}^{2}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2})
. Additionally, we define

\displaystyle M_{n}(f,t)\,{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm def}}{{=}}}\,X_{n}(f,t)-X_{n}(f,0)-\int_{0}^{t}X_{n}\left(\frac{\Delta f}{2},s\right){\mathrm{d}}s,\quad f\in{\mathscr{C}}^{2}_{pd}({\mathbb{R}}^{2}),\;n\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}.

(3.39)

Proposition
4.11
(to be established below) demonstrates that for
n=\infty
, the triple
(\nu_{n},\mu_{n},\mathring{\mu}_{n})
and the martingale measure
M_{n}
, as defined by (
3.39
), are uniquely determined by
X_{n}
. For finite
n
, this property follows immediately.

Convention 3.7.

Under the notation introduced after Corollary
3.6
up to (
3.39
), the equations in (
3.35
) specialized to
{\varepsilon}={\varepsilon}_{n}
hold as equalities rather than definitions throughout the remainder of this work. This same interpretation applies to all preceding notation, definitions, and equations, provided they do not explicitly reference the underlying space-time white noise.

\blacklozenge

Corollary 3.8.

We can choose an event
\Omega_{0}
with
{\mathbb{P}}(\Omega_{0})=1
on which for all
f_{0}\in{\mathscr{C}}_{b}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})
and
g_{0}\in{\mathscr{C}}_{b}({\mathbb{R}}^{2}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2})
, the following limit holds in
C_{{\mathbb{R}}}[0,\infty)\times C_{{\mathbb{R}}}[0,\infty)\times C_{{\mathbb{R}}}[0,\infty)\times C_{{\mathbb{R}}}[0,\infty)
:

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\lim_{n\to\infty}\big(X_{n}^{\sharp}(f_{0},t),\nu_{n}^{\sharp}(f_{0},t),\mathring{\mu}_{n}^{\sharp}(g_{0},t),\mu_{n}^{\sharp}(g_{0},t)\big)\\
&\quad=\big(X_{\infty}^{\sharp}(f_{0},t),\nu_{\infty}^{\sharp}(f_{0},t),\mathring{\mu}_{\infty}^{\sharp}(g_{0},t),\mu_{\infty}^{\sharp}(g_{0},t)\big).\end{split}

(3.40)

Proof.
The proof readily follows from (
3.37
) and the observation that for any integer
d\geq 1
and
f\in{\mathscr{C}}_{b}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
, the following mappings are continuous:

\displaystyle\mu\mapsto\mu(f):\mathcal{M}_{f}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})\to{\mathbb{R}},

(3.41)

\displaystyle\{\mu_{t};t\geq 0\}\mapsto\{\mu(f,t);t\geq 0\}:C_{\mathcal{M}_{f}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}[0,\infty)\to C_{{\mathbb{R}}}[0,\infty).

(3.42)

Here,
\mu(f,t)\,{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm def}}{{=}}}\,\int f{\mathrm{d}}\mu_{t}
as before, and
the continuity of (
3.41
) follows directly from the topology of
\mathcal{M}_{f}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
. Additionally, the continuity of (
3.42
) is established via a subsequence argument:
\mu^{n}(f,t_{n})\to\mu^{\infty}(f,t_{\infty})
whenever
t_{n}\to t_{\infty}\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
and
\{\mu^{n}_{t};t\geq 0\}\to\{\mu^{\infty}_{t};t\geq 0\}
in
C_{\mathcal{M}_{f}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}[0,\infty)
.

\blacksquare

4
Limiting expansions of covariation measures

This section is dedicated to proving Theorem
2.10
. The main arguments are organized across Sections
4.1
,
4.2
, and
4.3
, where we establish the precise subsequential limits of the four terms
I^{j}_{n}(f,T)=I^{j}_{{\varepsilon}_{n}}(f,T)
for
1\leq j\leq 4
defined in (
3.12
). Section
4.4
completes the section by combining these results to finalize the proof of Theorem
2.7
(1
∘
). Recall that from Corollary
3.6
onward, we work with the fixed sequence chosen there and adhere to Convention
3.7
.

4.1
Limit theorem for
\boldsymbol{I}^{\boldsymbol{1}}_{\boldsymbol{n}}\boldsymbol{(}\boldsymbol{f}\boldsymbol{,}\boldsymbol{T}\boldsymbol{)}

The following proposition initiates the identification of subsequential limits of the functionals
I_{{\varepsilon}}^{j}(f,T)
defined in Proposition
3.2
, specifically focusing on the case
j=1
.

Proposition 4.1.

For all
0<T<\infty
and
f(x,t)\in{\mathscr{C}}_{pd}^{1}({\mathbb{R}}^{2}\times[0,T])
, it holds that

\displaystyle I^{1}_{n}(f,T)\,{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm def}}{{=}}}\,\int_{s=0}^{T}\int_{x}f(x,t)X_{n}(x,t)^{2}{\mathrm{d}}x{\mathrm{d}}t-\int_{s=0}^{T}\int_{x}\left(\int_{y}\mathfrak{L}^{1}_{n}f(y,x,s,T){\mathrm{d}}y\right)X_{n}(x,s)^{2}{\mathrm{d}}x{\mathrm{d}}s

\displaystyle\quad\xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{{\mathbb{P}}}I^{1}_{\infty}(f,T)\,{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm def}}{{=}}}\,\int_{t=0}^{T}\int_{x}\mathfrak{L}_{0}f(x,t,T)\nu_{\infty}({\mathrm{d}}x,{\mathrm{d}}t),

and the limit defines an absolutely convergent integral. Here,
\mathfrak{L}^{1}_{n}\,{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm def}}{{=}}}\,\mathfrak{L}^{1}_{{\varepsilon}_{n}}
for
\mathfrak{L}^{1}_{\varepsilon}
in (
3.17
), and

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathfrak{L}_{0}f(x,t,T)&\,{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm def}}{{=}}}-\frac{1}{2\pi}\biggl(\frac{\log[4(T-t)]}{2}+\lambda-\frac{\gamma_{\sf EM}}{2}-\int_{y}\phi(y)\log\lvert y\rvert{\mathrm{d}}y\biggr)f(x,t)\\
&\quad\,-\int_{x^{\prime}}\int_{t^{\prime}=0}^{T-t}[f(x-x^{\prime},t+t^{\prime})-f(x,t)]P_{t^{\prime}}(x^{\prime})P_{t^{\prime}}(x^{\prime}){\mathrm{d}}t^{\prime}{\mathrm{d}}x^{\prime}.\end{split}

(4.1)

Lemma
4.2
below provides the asymptotic expansion of
\mathring{\mathfrak{L}}^{1}_{\varepsilon}f
as
{\varepsilon}\to 0
, accompanied by relevant estimates. This expansion is a key ingredient in the proof of Proposition
4.1
, as it allows the cancellation of the divergent terms (
\infty-\infty
) originating from
X_{n}(x,s)^{2}{\mathrm{d}}x{\mathrm{d}}s
in the two integrals defining
I^{1}_{n}(f,T)
, achieved through the use of test functions. Lemma
4.2
also provides results that are not used here but will be needed in Section
4.2
, as
\mathfrak{L}^{1}_{\varepsilon}
appears in the definition (
3.14
) of
I^{2}_{\varepsilon}(f,T)
.

Lemma 4.2.

Let
0<T<\infty
,
{\varepsilon}\in(0,{{\varepsilon}_{\ref{def:ovl}}(\lambda)}]
,
f(x,s)\in{\mathscr{C}}_{pd}^{1}({\mathbb{R}}^{2}\times[0,T])
, and
m\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
.

(1
∘
)

For all
M\in(0,\infty)
,
(y,x)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
with
0<\lvert y\rvert\leq M
, and
0<s<T
,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathring{\mathfrak{L}}^{1}_{\varepsilon}f(y,x,s,T)&=f(x,s)+\frac{1}{\log{\varepsilon}^{-1}}\biggl(\frac{\log[4(T-s)]}{2}+\lambda-\frac{\gamma_{\sf EM}}{2}-\log\lvert y\rvert\biggr)f(x,s)\\
&\quad+\frac{2\pi}{\log{\varepsilon}^{-1}}\int_{x^{\prime}}\int_{t^{\prime}=0}^{T-s}[f(x-x^{\prime},s+t^{\prime})-f(x,s)]P_{t^{\prime}}(x^{\prime})P_{t^{\prime}}(x^{\prime}){\mathrm{d}}t^{\prime}{\mathrm{d}}x^{\prime}\\
&\quad+R_{\ref{key}}(y,x,s,T).\end{split}

(4.2)

Here,
\lambda
defines
\Lambda_{\varepsilon}
through (
1.5
),
\gamma_{\mathsf{E}\mathsf{M}}
is the Euler–Mascheroni constant, the double integral is absolutely convergent
with

\displaystyle\int_{x^{\prime}}\int_{t^{\prime}=0}^{T-s}\lvert f(x-x^{\prime},s+t^{\prime})-f(x,s)\rvert P_{t^{\prime}}(x^{\prime})P_{t^{\prime}}(x^{\prime}){\mathrm{d}}t^{\prime}{\mathrm{d}}x^{\prime}\leq\frac{C(T,f,m)}{1+\lvert x\rvert^{m}},

(4.3)

and
R_{\ref{key}}(y,x,s,T)=R_{\ref{key},{\varepsilon}}(y,x,s,T)
, independent of
m
, satisfies the following estimate:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\lvert R_{\ref{key}}(y,x,s,T)\rvert&\leq\frac{C(\lambda,T,f,m,M)}{(\log^{2}{\varepsilon}^{-1})(1+\lvert x\rvert^{m})}\left(\lvert\log(T-s)\rvert+1+\lvert\log\lvert y\rvert\rvert\right)\\
&\quad+\frac{C(\lambda,T,f,m,M)}{(\log{\varepsilon}^{-1})(1+\lvert x\rvert^{m})}{\varepsilon}_{\ref{limT:glue}}\left(\frac{4(T-s){\varepsilon}^{-2}}{\lvert y\rvert^{2}}\right),\end{split}

(4.4)

where
0\leq{\varepsilon}_{\ref{limT:glue}}(a)\lesssim a^{-1}\mathds{1}(a\geq 1)+(1-\log a)\mathds{1}(a<1)
. Moreover,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\lvert\mathring{\mathfrak{L}}^{1}_{\varepsilon}f(y,x,s,T)-f(x,s)\rvert&\leq\frac{C(\lambda,T,f,m,M)}{(\log{\varepsilon}^{-1})(1+\lvert x\rvert^{m})}\\
&\quad\times\left[\lvert\log(T-s)\rvert+1+\lvert\log\lvert y\rvert\rvert+{\varepsilon}_{\ref{limT:glue}}\left(\frac{4(T-s){\varepsilon}^{-2}}{\lvert y\rvert^{2}}\right)\right].\end{split}

(4.5)

(2
∘
)
For all
(y,x)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
, we have

\displaystyle\nabla_{x}\mathring{\mathfrak{L}}^{1}_{\varepsilon}f(y,x,s,T)

\displaystyle=\Lambda_{\varepsilon}\int_{x^{\prime}}\left(\int_{t^{\prime}=0}^{T-s}\nabla_{x}f(x-x^{\prime},s+t^{\prime})P_{t^{\prime}}({\varepsilon}y+x^{\prime})P_{t^{\prime}}(x^{\prime}){\mathrm{d}}t^{\prime}\right){\mathrm{d}}x^{\prime},\;0\leq s\leq T.

In particular, for all
y,x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
,
0<s<T
, and
m\in\mathbb{N}
,

\displaystyle\lvert\nabla_{x}\mathring{\mathfrak{L}}^{1}_{\varepsilon}f(y,x,s,T)\rvert\leq\frac{C(f,\lambda,m)}{1+\lvert x\rvert^{m}}\left[\lvert\log(T-s)\rvert+1+\lvert\log\lvert y\rvert\rvert+{\varepsilon}_{\ref{limT:glue}}\left(\frac{4(T-s){\varepsilon}^{-2}}{\lvert y\rvert^{2}}\right)\right].

(4.6)

(3
∘
)
Suppose that
m\geq 5
. For
0<s<T
, it holds that

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \int_{y}\frac{1}{1+\lvert y\rvert^{m}}\left|{\varepsilon}_{\ref{limT:glue}}\left(\frac{4(T-s){\varepsilon}^{-2}}{\lvert y\rvert^{2}}\right)\right|{\mathrm{d}}y&\lesssim C(m)\min\left\{\frac{1}{4(T-s){\varepsilon}^{-2}},1\right\}\\
&\quad+C(m)\lvert\log[4(T-s){\varepsilon}^{-2}]\rvert\mathds{1}_{\{4(T-s){\varepsilon}^{-2}<1\}}.\end{aligned}

(4.7)

Proof of Lemma
4.2
.

(1
∘
) By (
3.18
), we can write

\displaystyle\mathring{\mathfrak{L}}^{1}_{\varepsilon}f(y,x,s,T)

\displaystyle=\Lambda_{\varepsilon}\int_{t=s}^{T}\int_{x_{0}}f(x_{0},t)P_{t-s}({\varepsilon}y+x,x_{0})P_{t-s}(x_{0},x){\mathrm{d}}x_{0}{\mathrm{d}}t

\displaystyle=\Lambda_{\varepsilon}\int_{t^{\prime}=0}^{T-s}\int_{x^{\prime}}f(x-x^{\prime},s+t^{\prime})P_{t^{\prime}}({\varepsilon}y+x^{\prime})P_{t^{\prime}}(x^{\prime}){\mathrm{d}}x^{\prime}{\mathrm{d}}t^{\prime}

(4.8)

\displaystyle=I^{1}_{\ref{asymp11}}f(x,s)+\Lambda_{\varepsilon}I^{2}_{\ref{asymp11}}+\Lambda_{\varepsilon}I^{3}_{\ref{asymp11}},

(4.9)

where

\displaystyle I^{1}_{\ref{asymp11}}

\displaystyle\,{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm def}}{{=}}}\,\Lambda_{\varepsilon}\int_{t^{\prime}=0}^{T-s}\int_{x^{\prime}}P_{t^{\prime}}({\varepsilon}y+x^{\prime})P_{t^{\prime}}(x^{\prime}){\mathrm{d}}x^{\prime}{\mathrm{d}}t^{\prime},

(4.10)

\displaystyle I^{2}_{\ref{asymp11}}

\displaystyle\,{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm def}}{{=}}}\,\int_{x^{\prime}}\int_{t^{\prime}=0}^{T-s}[f(x-x^{\prime},s+t^{\prime})-f(x,s)]P_{t^{\prime}}(x^{\prime})P_{t^{\prime}}(x^{\prime}){\mathrm{d}}t^{\prime}{\mathrm{d}}x^{\prime},

\displaystyle I^{3}_{\ref{asymp11}}

\displaystyle\,{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm def}}{{=}}}\,\int_{x^{\prime}}\int_{t^{\prime}=0}^{T-s}[f(x-x^{\prime},s+t^{\prime})-f(x,s)][P_{t^{\prime}}({\varepsilon}y+x^{\prime})-P_{t^{\prime}}(x^{\prime})]P_{t^{\prime}}(x^{\prime}){\mathrm{d}}t^{\prime}{\mathrm{d}}x^{\prime}.

The remainder of the proof of (1
∘
) proceeds as follows.
Steps 1–3 below obtain, respectively: a second-order expansion of
I^{1}_{\ref{asymp11}}
in terms of
1/\log{\varepsilon}^{-1}
; the absolute convergence of
I^{2}_{\ref{asymp11}}
as an integral; and a bound on
\Lambda_{\varepsilon}I^{3}_{\ref{asymp11}}
of order
\Lambda_{\varepsilon}{\varepsilon}^{1/2}
. Additionally, these results proven for
I^{2}_{\ref{asymp11}}
and
\Lambda_{\varepsilon}I^{3}_{\ref{asymp11}}
include bounds that decay polynomially in
\lvert x\rvert
as
\lvert x\rvert\to\infty
. Finally, Step 4 concludes the proof.

Step 1.

To obtain the required property of
I^{1}_{\ref{asymp11}}
, we first recall the definition (
1.5
) of
\Lambda_{\varepsilon}
. We then apply Lemma
7.1
(1
∘
) after integrating out
x^{\prime}
, applying the Chapman-Kolmogorov equation, and making the substitution
t^{\prime\prime}={\varepsilon}^{-2}t^{\prime}
:

\displaystyle I^{1}_{\ref{asymp11}}

\displaystyle=\Lambda_{\varepsilon}\int_{t^{\prime\prime}=0}^{(T-s){\varepsilon}^{-2}}P_{2t^{\prime\prime}}(y){\mathrm{d}}t^{\prime\prime}

\displaystyle=\biggl(\frac{2\pi}{\log{\varepsilon}^{-1}}+\frac{2\pi\lambda}{\log^{2}{\varepsilon}^{-1}}\biggr)\biggl[\frac{\log[4(T-s){\varepsilon}^{-2}]}{4\pi}-\frac{\gamma_{\sf EM}}{4\pi}-\frac{\log\lvert y\rvert}{2\pi}+{\varepsilon}_{\ref{limT:glue}}\left(\frac{4(T-s){\varepsilon}^{-2}}{\lvert y\rvert^{2}}\right)\biggr]

\displaystyle\begin{split}&=1+\frac{1}{\log{\varepsilon}^{-1}}\biggl(\frac{\log[4(T-s)]}{2}+\lambda-\frac{\gamma_{\sf EM}}{2}-\log\lvert y\rvert\biggr)\\
&\quad+\frac{\lambda}{\log^{2}{\varepsilon}^{-1}}\biggl(\frac{\log[4(T-s)]}{2}-\frac{\gamma_{\sf EM}}{2}-\log\lvert y\rvert\biggr)+\Lambda_{\varepsilon}{\varepsilon}_{\ref{limT:glue}}\left(\frac{4(T-s){\varepsilon}^{-2}}{\lvert y\rvert^{2}}\right).\end{split}

(4.11)

Step 2.

To obtain the absolute convergence of
I^{2}_{\ref{asymp11}}
as an integral, we apply the assumption
f\in{\mathscr{C}}^{1}_{pd}({\mathbb{R}}^{2}\times[0,T])
and the mean-value theorem
to get the following bound for any
m\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
:

\displaystyle\sup_{\scriptstyle 0\leq s\leq T\atop\scriptstyle s+t^{\prime}\leq T}\lvert f(x-x^{\prime},s+t^{\prime})-f(x,s)\rvert\leq(\lvert x^{\prime}\rvert+t^{\prime})\left(\frac{C(T,f,m)}{1+\lvert x-x^{\prime}\rvert^{m}}+\frac{C(T,f,m)}{1+\lvert x\rvert^{m}}\right).

(4.12)

When applied to
I^{2}_{\ref{asymp11}}
,
these bounds allow (
7.9
) with
(p_{1},p_{2},p)=(1,1,m)
and
(p_{1},p_{2},p)=(1,1,0)
. Therefore, (
4.3
) holds, and

I^{2}_{\ref{asymp11}}
defines an absolutely convergent integral with

\displaystyle\lvert I^{2}_{\ref{asymp11}}\rvert\leq\frac{C(T,f,m)}{1+\lvert x\rvert^{m}}.

(4.13)

Moreover, by the definition (
1.5
) of
\Lambda_{\varepsilon}
along with (
4.3
), we get

\displaystyle\Lambda_{\varepsilon}I^{2}_{\ref{asymp11}}=\frac{2\pi}{\log{\varepsilon}^{-1}}I^{2}_{\ref{asymp11}}+\frac{2\pi}{\log^{2}{\varepsilon}^{-1}}\mathcal{O}_{\lambda,T,f,m}\left(\frac{1}{1+\lvert x\rvert^{m}}\right).

(4.14)

Step 3.

We bound
I^{3}_{\ref{asymp11}}
in this step.
For fixed
\delta_{\varepsilon}\in(0,\infty)
such that
{\varepsilon}/\delta_{\varepsilon}^{1/2}\leq 1
, we write the integral in
I^{3}_{\ref{asymp11}}

as the sum of two integrals according to
1=\mathds{1}_{(0,\delta_{\varepsilon})}(t^{\prime})+\mathds{1}_{[\delta_{\varepsilon},\infty)}(t^{\prime})
. The bound in (
4.12
) is also applied again.
Hence, for any
m\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
and
0<\lvert y\rvert\leq M
,

\displaystyle\lvert I^{3}_{\ref{asymp11}}\rvert

\displaystyle\leq C(T,f,m)\int_{x^{\prime}}\int_{t^{\prime}=0}^{T-s}\mathds{1}_{(0,\delta_{\varepsilon})}(t^{\prime})(\lvert x^{\prime}\rvert+t^{\prime})\left(\frac{1}{1+\lvert x-x^{\prime}\rvert^{m}}+\frac{1}{1+\lvert x\rvert^{m}}\right)

\displaystyle\quad\times[P_{t^{\prime}}({\varepsilon}y+x^{\prime})+P_{t}(x^{\prime})]P_{t^{\prime}}(x^{\prime}){\mathrm{d}}t^{\prime}{\mathrm{d}}x^{\prime}

\displaystyle\quad+C(T,f,m,M)\frac{{\varepsilon}}{\delta_{\varepsilon}^{1/2}}\int_{x^{\prime}}\int_{t^{\prime}=0}^{T-s}\mathds{1}_{[\delta_{\varepsilon},\infty)}(t^{\prime})(\lvert x^{\prime}\rvert+t^{\prime})

\displaystyle\quad\times\left(\frac{1}{1+\lvert x-x^{\prime}\rvert^{m}}+\frac{1}{1+\lvert x\rvert^{m}}\right)P_{2t^{\prime}}(x^{\prime})P_{t^{\prime}}(x^{\prime}){\mathrm{d}}t^{\prime}{\mathrm{d}}x^{\prime},

where the last term uses (
7.3
). We take
\delta_{\varepsilon}={\varepsilon}
. Then by (
7.9
) of Lemma
7.3
,

\displaystyle\Lambda_{\varepsilon}I^{3}_{\ref{asymp11}}=\Lambda_{\varepsilon}{\varepsilon}^{1/2}\mathcal{O}_{T,f,m,M}\biggl(\frac{1}{1+\lvert x\rvert^{m}}\biggr).

(4.15)

Step 4.

Finally, we get (
4.2
) by applying
(
4.11
), (
4.14
) and (
4.15
) to
(
4.9
) with

\displaystyle R_{\ref{key}}(y,x,s,T)

\displaystyle\,{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm def}}{{=}}}\,\frac{\lambda}{\log^{2}{\varepsilon}^{-1}}\biggl(\frac{\log[4(T-s)]}{2}-\frac{\gamma_{\sf EM}}{2}-\log\lvert y\rvert\biggr)f(x,s)

\displaystyle\quad+\Lambda_{\varepsilon}{\varepsilon}_{\ref{limT:glue}}\left(\frac{4(T-s){\varepsilon}^{-2}}{\lvert y\rvert^{2}}\right)f(x,s)+\frac{2\pi}{\log^{2}{\varepsilon}^{-1}}\mathcal{O}_{\lambda,T,f,m}\left(\frac{1}{1+\lvert x\rvert^{m}}\right)

\displaystyle\quad+\Lambda_{\varepsilon}{\varepsilon}^{1/2}\mathcal{O}_{T,f,m,M}\biggl(\frac{1}{1+\lvert x\rvert^{m}}\biggr).

The remaining properties in (1
∘
) can be obtained as follows: (
4.3
) has been established as (
4.13
); the function
R_{\ref{key}}(y,x,s,T)
satisfies (
4.4
) by direct application of the previous display and the assumption
f\in{\mathscr{C}}^{1}_{pd}({\mathbb{R}}^{2}\times[0,T])
; and the bound in (
4.5
) is obtained from (
4.2
) using the assumption
f\in{\mathscr{C}}^{1}_{pd}({\mathbb{R}}^{2}\times[0,T])
, together with (
4.3
) and (
4.4
).

(2
∘
) Both derivative formulas follow from the integral representation given in (
4.8
). Specifically, the time derivative formula is obtained by applying the Leibniz integral rule. Furthermore, the bound in (
4.6
) holds since, by the first derivative formula and (
7.8
), we have

\displaystyle\lvert\nabla_{x}\mathring{\mathfrak{L}}^{1}_{\varepsilon}f(y,x,s,T)\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\Lambda_{\varepsilon}\int_{x^{\prime}}\left(\int_{t^{\prime}=0}^{T-s}\frac{C(f,m,T)(1+\lvert x^{\prime}\rvert^{m})}{1+\lvert x\rvert^{m}}P_{t^{\prime}}({\varepsilon}y+x^{\prime})P_{t^{\prime}}(x^{\prime}){\mathrm{d}}t^{\prime}\right){\mathrm{d}}x^{\prime},

so that the bound in (
4.11
) (
I^{1}_{\ref{asymp11}}
is defined by (
4.10
)) and (
7.9
) apply.

(3
∘
)
As stated below (
4.4
),
0\leq{\varepsilon}_{\ref{limT:glue}}(a)\lesssim a^{-1}\mathds{1}(a\geq 1)+(1-\log a)\mathds{1}(a<1)
. Hence,

\displaystyle\int_{y}\frac{1}{1+\lvert y\rvert^{m}}\left|{\varepsilon}_{\ref{limT:glue}}\left(\frac{4(T-s){\varepsilon}^{-2}}{\lvert y\rvert^{2}}\right)\right|{\mathrm{d}}y

\displaystyle\quad\leq\int_{y}\frac{1}{1+\lvert y\rvert^{m}}\left(\frac{4(T-s){\varepsilon}^{-2}}{\lvert y\rvert^{2}}\right)^{-1}\mathds{1}\left(\frac{4(T-s){\varepsilon}^{-2}}{\lvert y\rvert^{2}}\geq 1\right){\mathrm{d}}y

\displaystyle\quad\quad+\int_{y}\frac{1}{1+\lvert y\rvert^{m}}\mathds{1}\left(\frac{4(T-s){\varepsilon}^{-2}}{\lvert y\rvert^{2}}<1\right){\mathrm{d}}y

\displaystyle\quad\quad+\int_{y}\frac{1}{1+\lvert y\rvert^{m}}\left(-\log\frac{4(T-s){\varepsilon}^{-2}}{\lvert y\rvert^{2}}\right)\mathds{1}\left(\frac{4(T-s){\varepsilon}^{-2}}{\lvert y\rvert^{2}}<1\right){\mathrm{d}}y.

(4.16)

Then observe that for
m\geq 5
, the first and second integrals on the right-hand side are bounded by
C(m)\min\{1/[4(T-s){\varepsilon}^{-2}],1\}
. In particular, the bound
C(m)/[4(T-s){\varepsilon}^{-2}]
applies to the second integral since for
A>0
,

\int_{y}\frac{1}{1+\lvert y\rvert^{m}}\mathds{1}\left(\frac{A}{\lvert y\rvert^{2}}<1\right){\mathrm{d}}y\lesssim\int_{r=\sqrt{A}}^{\infty}\frac{r}{1+r^{m}}{\mathrm{d}}r\lesssim\int_{r=\sqrt{A}}^{\infty}\frac{1}{r^{3}}{\mathrm{d}}r\lesssim\frac{1}{A}.

To bound the last integral in (
4.16
), note that for any
A\geq 1/2
,

\displaystyle\int_{\frac{A}{\lvert y\rvert^{2}}<1}\frac{1}{1+\lvert y\rvert^{m}}\left\lvert\log\frac{A}{\lvert y\rvert^{2}}\right|{\mathrm{d}}y\leq\int_{\frac{\lvert y\rvert^{2}}{A}>1}\frac{1}{1+\lvert y\rvert^{m}}\left|\frac{\lvert y\rvert^{2}}{A}\right|{\mathrm{d}}y\leq C(m)\min\left\{\frac{1}{A},1\right\},

where the first inequality follows since
\log x\lesssim x
for all
x\geq 1
, and the last inequality holds as
m\geq 5
by assumption. Additionally, for any
0<A<1/2
,

\displaystyle\int_{\frac{A}{\lvert y\rvert^{2}}<1}\frac{1}{1+\lvert y\rvert^{m}}\left\lvert\log\frac{A}{\lvert y\rvert^{2}}\right|{\mathrm{d}}y

\displaystyle\leq\int_{y}\frac{\lvert\log A\rvert+\lvert\log\lvert y\rvert^{2}\rvert}{1+\lvert y\rvert^{m}}{\mathrm{d}}y

\displaystyle\leq C(m)(\lvert\log A\rvert+1)\leq C(m)\lvert\log A\rvert.

Hence, (
4.16
) is sufficient to obtain (
4.7
).

\blacksquare

Proof of Proposition
4.1
.

We begin by proving the following two limits:

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\int_{s=0}^{T}\int_{x}\left(\int_{y}\lvert R_{\ref{key},n}(y,x,s,T)\rvert\phi(y){\mathrm{d}}y\right)X_{n}(x,s)^{2}{\mathrm{d}}x{\mathrm{d}}s\right]=0,

(4.17)

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}{\mathbb{E}}\biggl[\int_{s=0}^{T}\int_{x}\biggl|-\frac{1}{2\pi}\biggl(\frac{\log[4(T-s)]}{2}+\lambda-\frac{\gamma_{\sf EM}}{2}-\int_{y}\phi(y)\log\lvert y\rvert{\mathrm{d}}y\biggr)f(x,s)

\displaystyle\quad-\int_{x^{\prime}}\int_{t^{\prime}=0}^{T-s}[f(x-x^{\prime},s+t^{\prime})-f(x,s)]P_{t^{\prime}}(x^{\prime})P_{t^{\prime}}(x^{\prime}){\mathrm{d}}t^{\prime}{\mathrm{d}}x^{\prime}\biggr|\frac{X_{n}(x,s)^{2}}{\log^{2}{\varepsilon}_{n}^{-1}}{\mathrm{d}}x{\mathrm{d}}s\biggr]=0.

(4.18)

Here, (
4.17
) follows from Theorem
3.4
(3
∘
), (
4.4
), and Lemma
4.2
(3
∘
), and (
4.18
) follows from Theorem
3.4
(3
∘
), (
4.3
) and the assumption
f(x,s)\in{\mathscr{C}}_{pd}^{1}({\mathbb{R}}^{2}\times[0,T])
.

We are ready to prove the required limit of
I^{1}_{n}(f,T)
. Recall that we have
\mathfrak{L}^{1}_{n}f(y,x,s,T)=\mathring{\mathfrak{L}}^{1}_{n}f(y,x,s,T)\phi(y)
by (
3.17
), where
\phi
is a probability density by Proposition
3.1
(1
∘
). Hence, by (
3.13
), we can rewrite
I^{1}_{n}(f,T)
as

I^{1}_{n}(f,T)=\int_{s=0}^{T}\int_{x}\left(\int_{y}[f(x,s)-\mathring{\mathfrak{L}}_{n}^{1}f(y,x,s,T)]\phi(y){\mathrm{d}}y\right)X_{n}(x,s)^{2}{\mathrm{d}}x{\mathrm{d}}s.

With this expression,
(
4.2
) and (
4.17
) imply the first limit in probability below:

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}\mbox{-}\lim_{n\to\infty}I^{1}_{n}(f,T)

\displaystyle\quad={\mathbb{P}}\mbox{-}\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{s=0}^{T}\int_{x}\biggl[-\frac{1}{\log{\varepsilon}_{n}^{-1}}\biggl(\frac{\log[4(T-s)]}{2}+\lambda-\frac{\gamma_{\sf EM}}{2}-\int_{y}\phi(y)\log\lvert y\rvert{\mathrm{d}}y\biggr)f(x,s)

\displaystyle\quad\quad-\frac{2\pi}{\log{\varepsilon}_{n}^{-1}}\int_{x^{\prime}}\int_{t^{\prime}=0}^{T-s}[f(x-x^{\prime},s+t^{\prime})-f(x,s)]P_{t^{\prime}}(x^{\prime})P_{t^{\prime}}(x^{\prime}){\mathrm{d}}t^{\prime}{\mathrm{d}}x^{\prime}\biggr]X_{n}(x,s)^{2}{\mathrm{d}}x{\mathrm{d}}s

\displaystyle\quad={\mathbb{P}}\mbox{-}\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{2\pi}{\log{\varepsilon}_{n}^{-1}}\int_{s=0}^{T}\int_{x}\biggl[-\frac{1}{2\pi}\biggl(\frac{\log[4(T-s)]}{2}+\lambda-\frac{\gamma_{\sf EM}}{2}-\int_{y}\phi(y)\log\lvert y\rvert{\mathrm{d}}y\biggr)f(x,s)

\displaystyle\quad\quad-\int_{x^{\prime}}\int_{t^{\prime}=0}^{T-s}[f(x-x^{\prime},s+t^{\prime})-f(x,s)]P_{t^{\prime}}(x^{\prime})P_{t^{\prime}}(x^{\prime}){\mathrm{d}}t^{\prime}{\mathrm{d}}x^{\prime}\biggr]X_{n}(x,s)^{2}{\mathrm{d}}x{\mathrm{d}}s

\displaystyle\quad={\mathbb{P}}\mbox{-}\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{s=0}^{T}\int_{x}\biggl[-\frac{1}{2\pi}\biggl(\frac{\log[4(T-s)]}{2}+\lambda-\frac{\gamma_{\sf EM}}{2}-\int_{y}\phi(y)\log\lvert y\rvert{\mathrm{d}}y\biggr)f(x,s)

\displaystyle\quad\quad-\int_{x^{\prime}}\int_{t^{\prime}=0}^{T-s}[f(x-x^{\prime},s+t^{\prime})-f(x,s)]P_{t^{\prime}}(x^{\prime})P_{t^{\prime}}(x^{\prime}){\mathrm{d}}t^{\prime}{\mathrm{d}}x^{\prime}\biggr]\nu_{n}({\mathrm{d}}x,{\mathrm{d}}s)

\displaystyle\quad=\int_{s=0}^{T}\int_{x}\mathfrak{L}_{0}f(x,s,T)\nu_{\infty}({\mathrm{d}}x,{\mathrm{d}}s).

Here, the third equality follows from (
4.18
), the definition (
1.5
) of
\Lambda_{n}=\Lambda_{n}
, and the relationship in (
3.35b
) between
X_{n}=X_{{\varepsilon}_{n}}
and
\nu_{n}=\nu_{{\varepsilon}_{n}}
. Additionally,
the last equality can be deduced from the definition (
4.1
) of
\mathfrak{L}_{0}f(x,s,T)
and Corollary
3.8
. In more detail, Corollary
3.8
is applicable in this context for two reasons. First, the singularity of
\log(T-s)
at
s=T
does not pose an obstruction, as Theorem
3.4
(3
∘
) implies

\limsup_{\eta\searrow 0+}\limsup_{n\to\infty}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\int_{s=T-\eta}^{T}\int_{x}\frac{\lvert\log(T-s)\rvert}{1+\lvert x\rvert^{m}}\Lambda_{n}X_{n}(x,s)^{2}{\mathrm{d}}x{\mathrm{d}}s\right]=0.

Second,

(x,s)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\times[0,T]\mapsto\int_{x^{\prime}}\int_{t^{\prime}=0}^{T-s}[f(x-x^{\prime},s+t^{\prime})-f(x,s)]P_{t^{\prime}}(x^{\prime})P_{t^{\prime}}(x^{\prime}){\mathrm{d}}t^{\prime}{\mathrm{d}}x^{\prime}\in{\mathscr{C}}^{0}_{pd}({\mathbb{R}}^{2}\times[0,T]),

where the continuity can be deduced from dominated convergence, thanks to (
4.12
) and (
7.9
), and the polynomial decay of any order follows from (
4.3
). The proof of Proposition
4.1
is complete.

\blacksquare

4.2
Limit theorem for
\boldsymbol{I}^{\boldsymbol{2}}_{\boldsymbol{n}}\boldsymbol{(}\boldsymbol{f}\boldsymbol{,}\boldsymbol{T}\boldsymbol{)}

The following proposition specifies the limit and
also establishes relations among the three random measures
\nu_{\infty},\mu_{\infty},\mathring{\mu}_{\infty}
from Corollary
3.6
.

Proposition 4.3.

Fix
0<T<\infty
. For all nonnegative
g\in{\mathscr{C}}({\mathbb{R}}^{2}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\times[0,T])
,

\displaystyle\int_{s=0}^{T}\int_{x}\int_{x^{\prime}}g(x^{\prime},x,s)\mu_{\infty}({\mathrm{d}}x^{\prime},{\mathrm{d}}x,{\mathrm{d}}s)

\displaystyle=\int_{s=0}^{T}\int_{x}g(x,x,s)\nu_{\infty}({\mathrm{d}}x,{\mathrm{d}}s),

(4.19)

\displaystyle\int_{s=0}^{T}\int_{x}\int_{x^{\prime}}g(x^{\prime},x,s)\mathring{\mu}_{\infty}({\mathrm{d}}x^{\prime},{\mathrm{d}}x,{\mathrm{d}}s)

\displaystyle=\int_{s=0}^{T}\int_{x}\left(\int_{x^{\prime}}g(x^{\prime},x,s)\phi(x^{\prime}){\mathrm{d}}x^{\prime}\right)\nu_{\infty}({\mathrm{d}}x,{\mathrm{d}}s).

(4.20)

Additionally, it holds that for all
f\in{\mathscr{C}}_{pd}^{1}({\mathbb{R}}^{2}\times[0,T])
,

\displaystyle I^{2}_{n}(f,T)

\displaystyle\,{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm def}}{{=}}}\,\int_{s=0}^{T}\int_{x}\int_{y}\mathfrak{L}^{1}_{n}f(y,x,s,T)X_{n}(x,s)^{2}{\mathrm{d}}y{\mathrm{d}}x{\mathrm{d}}s

\displaystyle\quad-\int_{s=0}^{T}\int_{x}\int_{y}\mathfrak{L}^{1}_{n}f(y,x,s,T)X_{n}({\varepsilon}_{n}y+x,s)X_{n}(x,s){\mathrm{d}}y{\mathrm{d}}x{\mathrm{d}}s

\displaystyle\quad\xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{{\mathbb{P}}}I^{2}_{\infty}(f,T)\,{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm def}}{{=}}}\,\int_{r^{\prime}=0}^{T}\int_{y^{\prime}}\biggl(\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{y^{\prime\prime}}\int_{y}\phi(y^{\prime\prime})(\log\rvert y^{\prime\prime}-y\rvert)\phi(y){\mathrm{d}}y{\mathrm{d}}y^{\prime\prime}

\displaystyle\quad\quad-\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{y^{\prime\prime}}\int_{y}\phi(y^{\prime\prime})(\log\lvert y^{\prime\prime}\rvert)\phi(y){\mathrm{d}}y{\mathrm{d}}y^{\prime\prime}\biggr)f(y^{\prime},r^{\prime})\nu_{\infty}({\mathrm{d}}y^{\prime},{\mathrm{d}}r^{\prime}),

where
\mathfrak{L}^{1}_{n}\,{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm def}}{{=}}}\,\mathfrak{L}^{1}_{{\varepsilon}_{n}}
for
\mathfrak{L}^{1}_{\varepsilon}
defined in (
3.17
).

The terms
I^{1}_{n}(f,T)
and
I^{2}_{n}(f,T)
share a similar structure, as each is defined as a difference of integrals. While
I^{1}_{n}(f,T)
involves only the measure
X_{n}(x,s)^{2}{\mathrm{d}}x{\mathrm{d}}s
,
I^{2}_{n}(f,T)
incorporates both
X_{n}(x,s)^{2}{\mathrm{d}}x{\mathrm{d}}s
and
X_{n}({\varepsilon}_{n}y+x,s)X_{n}(x,s){\mathrm{d}}x{\mathrm{d}}s
. Accordingly, our approach for analyzing
I^{2}_{n}(f,T)
adapts the methods from Proposition
4.1
. In particular, we employ the Fubini theorem and the stochastic Fubini theorem to bring Lebesgue integrals inside stochastic integrals, thereby absorbing pointwise singularities in the limit. Nevertheless, determining the precise limit of
I^{2}_{n}(f,T)
still necessitates a far more refined analysis. This increased complexity stems from perturbing the spatial variable in
X_{n}
, specifically by using
X_{n}({\varepsilon}_{n}y+x,s)X_{n}(x,s){\mathrm{d}}x{\mathrm{d}}s
.

Lemmas
4.4
and
4.5
below form the core technical machinery for Proposition
4.3
, formulated under the general parameter
{\varepsilon}
rather than
{\varepsilon}_{n}
, as the analysis depends only on the setting established before Corollary
3.6
. Lemma
4.4
analyzes the difference of integrals defining
I^{2}_{{\varepsilon}}(f,T)
by replacing
\mathfrak{L}^{1}_{\varepsilon}f(y,x,s,T)
with a general function
g(y,x,s,T)
to simplify notation. Using the mild formulation (
2.18
) to expand
X_{\varepsilon}({\varepsilon}y+x,s)-X_{\varepsilon}(x,s)
, together with the Fubini arguments considered above, we obtain the decomposition (
4.21
) involving the refined functionals
I^{2,k}_{{\varepsilon}}(g,T)
. Lemma
4.5
then investigates the limits of these functionals and related terms. At the conclusion of the proof of Proposition
4.3
, we set
g=\mathfrak{L}^{1}_{\varepsilon}f
. Ultimately, starting from the original expression for
I^{2}_{{\varepsilon}}(f,T)
, the goal of this proposition is to transfer the spatial perturbation in
X_{\varepsilon}({\varepsilon}y+x,s)
to the spatial variables of
\mathfrak{L}^{1}_{\varepsilon}f(y,x,s,T)
. The decomposition using
I^{2,k}_{{\varepsilon}}(g,T)
facilitates this transfer, allowing Lemma
4.2
to be applied where appropriate to analyze the limiting behavior.

Lemma 4.4.

For all
T\in(0,\infty)
and
{g}={g}(y,x,s,T)
with
(y,x,s)\mapsto{g}(y,x,s,T)\in L^{1}({\mathbb{R}}^{2}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\times[0,T])
,

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\int_{s=0}^{T}\int_{x}\int_{y}{g}(y,x,s,T)[X_{\varepsilon}({\varepsilon}y+x,s)-X_{\varepsilon}(x,s)]X_{\varepsilon}(x,s){\mathrm{d}}y{\mathrm{d}}x{\mathrm{d}}s\\
&\quad=I^{2,1}_{{\varepsilon}}({g},T)+I^{2,2}_{{\varepsilon}}({g},T)+I^{2,3}_{{\varepsilon}}({g},T)+I^{2,4}_{{\varepsilon}}({g},T).\end{split}

(4.21)

Here, the left-hand side of (
4.21
) defines an absolutely convergent integral, as justified by (
3.5
), and

\displaystyle I^{2,1}_{{\varepsilon}}({g},T)

\displaystyle\,{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm def}}{{=}}}\,\int_{s=0}^{T}\int_{x}\int_{y}{g}(y,x,s,T)[P_{s}X_{0}({\varepsilon}y+x)-P_{s}X_{0}(x)]P_{s}X_{0}(x){\mathrm{d}}y{\mathrm{d}}x{\mathrm{d}}s,

\displaystyle I^{2,2}_{{\varepsilon}}({g},T)

\displaystyle\,{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm def}}{{=}}}\,\int_{r^{\prime}=0}^{T}\int_{y^{\prime}}\int_{z^{\prime\prime}}\int_{s=r^{\prime}}^{T}\int_{x}\int_{y}{g}(y,x,s,T)[P_{s-r^{\prime}}({\varepsilon}y+x,y^{\prime})-P_{s-r^{\prime}}(x,y^{\prime})]

\displaystyle\quad\times P_{s-r^{\prime}}(x,z^{\prime\prime}){\mathrm{d}}y{\mathrm{d}}x{\mathrm{d}}sX_{\varepsilon}(z^{\prime\prime},r^{\prime}){\mathrm{d}}z^{\prime\prime}{M}_{\varepsilon}({\mathrm{d}}y^{\prime},{\mathrm{d}}r^{\prime}),

\displaystyle I^{2,3}_{{\varepsilon}}({g},T)

\displaystyle\,{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm def}}{{=}}}\,\int_{r^{\prime\prime}=0}^{T}\int_{y^{\prime\prime}}\int_{z^{\prime}}\int_{s=r^{\prime\prime}}^{T}\int_{x}\int_{y}{g}(y,x,s,T)[P_{s-r^{\prime\prime}}({\varepsilon}y+x,z^{\prime})-P_{s-r^{\prime\prime}}(x,z^{\prime})]

\displaystyle\quad\times P_{s-r^{\prime\prime}}(x,y^{\prime\prime}){\mathrm{d}}y{\mathrm{d}}x{\mathrm{d}}sX_{\varepsilon}(z^{\prime},r^{\prime\prime}){\mathrm{d}}z^{\prime}{M}_{\varepsilon}({\mathrm{d}}y^{\prime\prime},{\mathrm{d}}r^{\prime\prime}),

\displaystyle I^{2,4}_{{\varepsilon}}({g},T)

\displaystyle\,{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm def}}{{=}}}\,\int_{r^{\prime}=0}^{T}\int_{y^{\prime}}\int_{y^{\prime\prime}}\int_{s=r^{\prime}}^{T}\int_{x}\int_{y}{g}(y,x,s,T)[P_{s-r^{\prime}}({\varepsilon}y+x,{\varepsilon}y^{\prime\prime}+y^{\prime})-P_{s-r^{\prime}}(x,{\varepsilon}y^{\prime\prime}+y^{\prime})]

\displaystyle\quad\times P_{s-r^{\prime}}(x,y^{\prime}){\mathrm{d}}y{\mathrm{d}}x{\mathrm{d}}s\Lambda_{\varepsilon}X_{\varepsilon}({\varepsilon}y^{\prime\prime}+y^{\prime},r^{\prime})X_{\varepsilon}(y^{\prime},r^{\prime})\phi(y^{\prime\prime}){\mathrm{d}}y^{\prime\prime}{\mathrm{d}}y^{\prime}{\mathrm{d}}r^{\prime},

where all Lebesgue integrals are absolutely convergent, and all stochastic integrals are well-defined in Itô’s sense. Moreover, (
4.21
) applies with the choice of
{g}(y,x,s,T)\equiv\mathfrak{L}_{\varepsilon}^{1}f(y,x,s,T)
for
f(x,s)\in{\mathscr{C}}_{pd}^{0}({\mathbb{R}}^{2}\times[0,T])
, in which case

I^{2}_{{\varepsilon}}(f,T)
defined by (
3.14
) satisfies the following decomposition:

\displaystyle I^{2}_{{\varepsilon}}(f,T)=-I^{2,1}_{{\varepsilon}}(\mathfrak{L}_{\varepsilon}^{1}f,T)-I^{2,2}_{{\varepsilon}}(\mathfrak{L}_{\varepsilon}^{1}f,T)-I^{2,3}_{{\varepsilon}}(\mathfrak{L}_{\varepsilon}^{1}f,T)-I^{2,4}_{{\varepsilon}}(\mathfrak{L}_{\varepsilon}^{1}f,T).

(4.22)

Proof.
We begin by proving (
4.21
). To do so, we first expand
[X_{\varepsilon}({\varepsilon}y+x,s)-X_{\varepsilon}(x,s)]X_{\varepsilon}(x,s)
using the mild form (
2.18
), which yields the following equations:

\displaystyle X_{\varepsilon}({\varepsilon}y+x,s)-X_{\varepsilon}(x,s)

\displaystyle=[P_{s}X_{0}({\varepsilon}y+x)-P_{s}X_{0}(x)]

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{r^{\prime}=0}^{s}\int_{y^{\prime}}[P_{s-r^{\prime}}({\varepsilon}y+x,y^{\prime})-P_{s-r^{\prime}}(x,y^{\prime})]{M}_{\varepsilon}({\mathrm{d}}y^{\prime},{\mathrm{d}}r^{\prime}),

\displaystyle X_{\varepsilon}(x,s)

\displaystyle=P_{s}X_{0}(x)+\int_{r^{\prime}=0}^{s}\int_{y^{\prime}}P_{s-r^{\prime}}(x,y^{\prime}){M}_{\varepsilon}({\mathrm{d}}y^{\prime},{\mathrm{d}}r^{\prime}).

We regard the right-hand sides as
a+b
and
c+d
and use a direct multiplication:
(a+b)(c+d)=ac+bc+ad+bd
. The product of the two stochastic integrals is further expanded by using integration by parts. Hence, we obtain the following expansion:

\displaystyle[X_{\varepsilon}({\varepsilon}y+x,s)-X_{\varepsilon}(x,s)]X_{\varepsilon}(x,s)

\displaystyle\quad=[P_{s}X_{0}({\varepsilon}y+x)-P_{s}X_{0}(x)]P_{s}X_{0}(x)

\displaystyle\quad\quad+\int_{r^{\prime}=0}^{s}\int_{y^{\prime}}[P_{s-r^{\prime}}({\varepsilon}y+x,y^{\prime})-P_{s-r^{\prime}}(x,y^{\prime})]{M}_{\varepsilon}({\mathrm{d}}y^{\prime},{\mathrm{d}}r^{\prime})P_{s}X_{0}(x)

\displaystyle\quad\quad+[P_{s}X_{0}({\varepsilon}y+x)-P_{s}X_{0}(x)]\int_{r^{\prime}=0}^{s}\int_{y^{\prime}}P_{s-r^{\prime}}(x,y^{\prime}){M}_{\varepsilon}({\mathrm{d}}y^{\prime},{\mathrm{d}}r^{\prime})

\displaystyle\quad\quad+\int_{r^{\prime}=0}^{s}\int_{y^{\prime}}[P_{s-r^{\prime}}({\varepsilon}y+x,y^{\prime})-P_{s-r^{\prime}}(x,y^{\prime})]

\displaystyle\quad\quad\times\biggl(\int_{r^{\prime\prime}=0}^{r^{\prime}}\int_{y^{\prime\prime}}P_{s-r^{\prime\prime}}(x,y^{\prime\prime}){M}_{\varepsilon}({\mathrm{d}}y^{\prime\prime},{\mathrm{d}}r^{\prime\prime})\biggr){M}_{\varepsilon}({\mathrm{d}}y^{\prime},{\mathrm{d}}r^{\prime})

\displaystyle\quad\quad+\int_{r^{\prime\prime}=0}^{s}\int_{y^{\prime\prime}}\biggl(\int_{r^{\prime}=0}^{r^{\prime\prime}}\int_{y^{\prime}}[P_{s-r^{\prime}}({\varepsilon}y+x,y^{\prime})-P_{s-r^{\prime}}(x,y^{\prime})]{M}_{\varepsilon}({\mathrm{d}}y^{\prime},{\mathrm{d}}r^{\prime})\biggr)

\displaystyle\quad\quad\times P_{s-r^{\prime\prime}}(x,y^{\prime\prime}){M}_{\varepsilon}({\mathrm{d}}y^{\prime\prime},{\mathrm{d}}r^{\prime\prime})

\displaystyle\quad\quad+\int_{r^{\prime}=0}^{s}\int_{y^{\prime}}\int_{y^{\prime\prime}}[P_{s-r^{\prime}}({\varepsilon}y+x,{\varepsilon}y^{\prime\prime}+y^{\prime})-P_{s-r^{\prime}}(x,{\varepsilon}y^{\prime\prime}+y^{\prime})]P_{s-r^{\prime}}(x,y^{\prime})

\displaystyle\quad\quad\times\Lambda_{\varepsilon}X_{\varepsilon}({\varepsilon}y^{\prime\prime}+y^{\prime},r^{\prime})X_{\varepsilon}(y^{\prime},r^{\prime})\phi(y^{\prime\prime}){\mathrm{d}}y^{\prime\prime}{\mathrm{d}}y^{\prime}{\mathrm{d}}r^{\prime}.

(4.23)
