This figure illustrates the unweighted graph
\mathcal{G}_{\oslash}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}
associated with
Q^{\lambda,\phi;\mathbf{i}_{1},\mathbf{i}_{2},\mathbf{i}_{3},\mathbf{i}_{4},\mathbf{i}_{5};{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}}_{{\varepsilon};s_{1},s_{2},s_{3},s_{4},s_{5},t}f(x_{0})
for the case of
N=4
, where
x_{0}^{k}
’s are distinct,
\mathbf{i}_{1}=(2,1)
,
\mathbf{i}_{2}=(3,2)
,
\mathbf{i}_{3}=(2,1)
,
\mathbf{i}_{4}=(4,3)
,
\mathbf{i}_{5}=(2,1)
, and
{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}=(0,1,1,0,0)
. A pair of joined bullet points represents a vertex of the form
(x^{i_{\ell}\prime}_{\ell},x^{i_{\ell}}_{\ell},s_{\ell})
or
(z^{\prime}_{\ell},z_{\ell},\tau_{\ell})
, while any other bullet point indicates a vertex of another type described in (
6.11
). The figure highlights six subgraphs, each marked with a different color (see the electronic version of the paper). This coloring convention is used in subsequent figures as well.

Step 1 (Weighted graphs
\mathcal{G}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{{\varepsilon}}
).

Definition 6.2 (Vertices of
\mathcal{G}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{\varepsilon}
).

The vertex set
\mathcal{V}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{\varepsilon}
of
\mathcal{G}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{\varepsilon}
consists of the following points:

\displaystyle(x_{0}^{k},s_{0})=(x_{0}^{k},0),\;\mbox{for }1\leq k\leq N;

(6.11a)

\displaystyle(x_{\ell}^{i_{\ell}\prime},x_{\ell}^{i_{\ell}},s_{\ell}),\;(x_{\ell}^{k},s_{\ell}),\;\mbox{for }1\leq\ell\leq m,\;k\notin\mathbf{i}_{\ell};

(6.11b)

\displaystyle(z^{\prime}_{\ell},z_{\ell},\tau_{\ell}),\;(z_{\ell}^{k},\tau_{\ell}),\;\mbox{for $1\leq\ell\leq m$ such that }\sigma_{\ell}=1,\;k\notin\mathbf{i}_{\ell};

(6.11c)

\displaystyle(x_{m+1}^{k},s_{m+1})=(x_{m+1}^{k},t),\;\mbox{for }1\leq k\leq N,

(6.11d)

where
x_{\ell}^{k},z_{\ell}^{\prime},z_{\ell},z_{\ell}^{k}
, for all
1\leq\ell\leq m+1
and
k
, are
distinct

{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
-variables such that they are also different from
x_{0}^{k}
,
1\leq k\leq N
, and
s_{\ell},\tau_{\ell}
are time variables subject to the following condition:

\displaystyle\begin{split}0<s_{1}\leq\tau_{1}<s_{2}\leq\tau_{2}<\cdots<s_{m}\leq\tau_{m}<t\\
\mbox{with }s_{\ell}<\tau_{\ell}\mbox{ if $\sigma_{\ell}=1$ and $s_{\ell}=\tau_{\ell}$ if $\sigma_{\ell}=0$.}\end{split}

(6.12)

We allow
x_{0}^{k}=x_{0}^{\ell}
for some
k\neq\ell
, in which case
(x_{0}^{k},s_{0})
and
(x_{0}^{\ell},s_{0})
are identified as the same vertex.
Also, we call
x_{0}^{k},x_{\ell}^{i_{\ell}\prime},x_{\ell}^{i_{\ell}},x_{\ell}^{k},z_{\ell}^{\prime},z_{\ell},z_{\ell}^{k},x_{m+1}^{k}
in (
6.11
) the
spatial components
of the vertices, and
{\sf Space}(\mathsf{v})
denotes the set of all the spatial components of a vertex
\mathsf{v}
. The
temporal components
of the vertices refer to
s_{0},s_{\ell},\tau_{\ell},t
in (
6.11
), and
{\sf Time}(\mathsf{v})
denotes the temporal component of
\mathsf{v}
.
\blacklozenge

To define the weights of edges, we use the following notations, where

\boldsymbol{w}=(w^{1},\cdots,w^{N})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2N}
,
\mathbf{i}=(i\prime,i)\in\mathcal{E}_{N}
,
W
is a two-dimensional standard Brownian motion, and
0\leq\tau_{1}<\tau_{2}<\infty
:

\displaystyle\widehat{\sigma}_{\ell}

\displaystyle\,{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm def}}{{=}}}\,1-\sigma_{\ell},

(6.13)

\displaystyle w^{\mathbf{i}\prime}

\displaystyle\,{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm def}}{{=}}}\,\frac{w^{i\prime}+w^{i}}{{\sqrt{2}}},\;w^{\mathbf{i}}\,{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm def}}{{=}}}\,\frac{w^{i\prime}-w^{i}}{{\sqrt{2}}}\;\Longrightarrow\;w^{i\prime}=\frac{w^{\mathbf{i}\prime}+w^{\mathbf{i}}}{{\sqrt{2}}},\;w^{i}=\frac{w^{\mathbf{i}\prime}-w^{\mathbf{i}}}{{\sqrt{2}}},

(6.14)

\displaystyle k/\mathbf{i}

\displaystyle\,{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm def}}{{=}}}\begin{cases}i,&k=i\prime,\\
k,&k\in\{1,\cdots,N\}\setminus\{i\prime\},\end{cases}

(6.15)

\displaystyle k^{/\varnothing}

\displaystyle\,{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm def}}{{=}}}\,k,\quad\forall\;k\in\{1,\cdots,N\},

(6.16)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{w}^{/\mathbf{j}}

\displaystyle\,{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm def}}{{=}}}\,(w^{1/\mathbf{j}},\cdots,w^{N/\mathbf{j}})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2N},\quad\forall\;\mathbf{j}\in\{\mathbf{i},\varnothing\},

(6.17)

\displaystyle P_{\tau_{1},\tau_{2}}(x,\widetilde{y})

\displaystyle\,{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm def}}{{=}}}\,P_{\tau_{2}-\tau_{1}}(\widetilde{y}-x),

(6.18)

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{s}}^{\lambda,\phi}_{\varepsilon}(\tau_{1},\tau_{2};x,\widetilde{y})

\displaystyle\,{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm def}}{{=}}}\,{\mathbb{E}}_{{\varepsilon}x}^{W}\left[\Lambda_{\varepsilon}^{2}\exp\left\{\Lambda_{\varepsilon}\int_{0}^{\tau_{2}-\tau_{1}}{\mathrm{d}}r\varphi_{\varepsilon}(W_{r})\right\}\delta_{{\varepsilon}\widetilde{y}}(W_{\tau_{2}-\tau_{1}})\right].

(6.19)

The variable
x
in (
6.18
) and (
6.19
) will be called the
initial states
of the heat kernel and
{\mathfrak{s}}^{\lambda,\phi}_{\varepsilon}
; the variable
\widetilde{y}
will be called their
terminal states
. Note that
{\mathfrak{s}}^{\lambda,\phi}_{\varepsilon}(\tau_{1},\tau_{2};x,\cdot)
is understood as a density incorporating the informal effect of
\int\delta_{{\varepsilon}\widetilde{y}}(W_{\tau})F(\widetilde{y}){\mathrm{d}}\widetilde{y}={\varepsilon}^{-2}F({\varepsilon}^{-1}W_{\tau})
, so we may assume
{\mathfrak{s}}^{\lambda,\phi}_{\varepsilon}>0
pointwise. We will use this density only through the integrals.

Given
\mathbf{i}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{i}_{m}\in\mathcal{E}_{N}
with
\mathbf{i}_{1}\neq\cdots\neq\mathbf{i}_{m}
,
{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}=(\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{m})\in\{0,1\}^{m}
, and the vertices defined in (
6.11
), we now specify the weights for the weighted graph in question. Roughly speaking, these weights reflect the following interactions, using the kernels in (
6.18
) and (
6.19
) such that
{\mathfrak{s}}^{\lambda,\phi}_{\varepsilon}
governs the interactions: when
\sigma_{\ell}=1
, the two particles indexed by
i_{\ell}^{\prime}
and
i_{\ell}
are required to “almost contact,” in the sense of approaching each other within a distance of order
{\varepsilon}
, at the times
s_{\ell}
and
\tau_{\ell}
and possibly at many other points within the interval
[s_{\ell},\tau_{\ell})
; when
\sigma_{\ell}=0
, it is only required that such a near contact occur at time
s_{\ell}
. For more details on how these interactions arise in the expectation in (
6.4
), see
[
18
, Section 4, especially Section 4.1]
.

Specifically, the weights formalizing the above interpretation are defined below, collectively providing a dynamical picture of interacting particles that experience almost-contact interactions when viewed sequentially in increasing order of
1\leq\ell\leq m
:

\displaystyle{\mathsf{w}}_{0,1;k}

\displaystyle\,{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm def}}{{=}}}\,\left\{\begin{array}[]{lr}P_{0,s_{1}}(x_{0}^{i_{1}\prime},x_{1}^{i_{1}}+{\varepsilon}{\sqrt{2}}x^{\mathbf{i}_{1}}_{1}),&\hskip 123.76965ptk=i_{1}\prime,\\
P_{0,s_{1}}(x_{0}^{i_{1}},x_{1}^{i_{1}}),&\hskip 123.76965ptk=i_{1},\\
P_{0,s_{1}}(x_{0}^{k},x_{1}^{k}),&\hskip 123.76965ptk\in\{1,\cdots,N\}\setminus\mathbf{i}_{1},\end{array}\right.

\displaystyle{\mathsf{w}}_{\ell,\ell.5;k}

\displaystyle\,{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm def}}{{=}}}\,\left\{\begin{array}[]{lr}\sigma_{\ell}\begin{bmatrix}P_{s_{\ell},\tau_{\ell}}({\sqrt{2}}x_{\ell}^{i_{\ell}}+{\varepsilon}x_{\ell}^{\mathbf{i}_{\ell}},z^{\prime}_{\ell})\\
\vskip-8.5359pt\\
{\mathfrak{s}}^{\lambda,\phi}_{\varepsilon}(s_{\ell},\tau_{\ell};x_{\ell}^{\mathbf{i}_{\ell}},z_{\ell})\end{bmatrix}_{\times},&\hskip 93.89418ptk=i_{\ell}.5,\\
\sigma_{\ell}P_{s_{\ell},\tau_{\ell}}(x_{\ell}^{k},z_{\ell}^{k}),&\hskip 93.89418ptk\in\{1,\cdots,N\}\setminus\mathbf{i}_{\ell},\end{array}\right.

\displaystyle{\mathsf{w}}_{\ell.5,\ell+1;k}

\displaystyle\,{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm def}}{{=}}}\,\left\{\begin{array}[]{lr}\displaystyle\sigma_{\ell}P_{\tau_{\ell},s_{\ell+1}}\Big(\frac{z_{\ell}^{\prime}+{\varepsilon}z_{\ell}}{{\sqrt{2}}},x_{\ell+1}^{i_{\ell}\prime/\mathbf{i}_{\ell+1}}+\mathds{1}_{\big\{{i_{\ell}\prime=i_{\ell+1}\prime,\atop 1\leq\ell\leq m-1}\big\}}{\varepsilon}{\sqrt{2}}x_{\ell+1}^{\mathbf{i}_{\ell+1}}\Big),&\hskip-14.22636ptk=i_{\ell}\prime,\\
\displaystyle\sigma_{\ell}P_{\tau_{\ell},s_{\ell+1}}\Big(\frac{z_{\ell}^{\prime}-{\varepsilon}z_{\ell}}{{\sqrt{2}}},x_{\ell+1}^{i_{\ell}/\mathbf{i}_{\ell+1}}+\mathds{1}_{\big\{{i_{\ell}=i_{\ell+1}\prime,\atop 1\leq\ell\leq m-1}\big\}}{\varepsilon}{\sqrt{2}}x_{\ell+1}^{\mathbf{i}_{\ell+1}}\Big),&\hskip-14.22636ptk=i_{\ell},\\
\sigma_{\ell}P_{\tau_{\ell},s_{\ell+1}}\Big(z_{\ell}^{k},x_{\ell+1}^{k/\mathbf{i}_{\ell+1}}+\mathds{1}_{\big\{{k=i_{\ell+1}\prime,\atop 1\leq\ell\leq m-1}\big\}}{\varepsilon}{\sqrt{2}}x_{\ell+1}^{\mathbf{i}_{\ell+1}}\Big),&\hskip-14.22636ptk\in\{1,\cdots,N\}\setminus\mathbf{i}_{\ell},\end{array}\right.

\displaystyle{\mathsf{w}}_{\ell,\ell+1;k}

\displaystyle\,{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm def}}{{=}}}\,\left\{\begin{array}[]{lr}\displaystyle\widehat{\sigma}_{\ell}\Lambda_{\varepsilon}^{1/2}P_{s_{\ell},s_{\ell+1}}\Big(x_{\ell}^{i_{\ell}}+{\varepsilon}{\sqrt{2}}x_{\ell}^{\mathbf{i}_{\ell}},x_{\ell+1}^{i_{\ell}\prime/\mathbf{i}_{\ell+1}}+\mathds{1}_{\big\{{i_{\ell}\prime=i_{\ell+1}\prime,\atop 1\leq\ell\leq m-1}\big\}}{\varepsilon}{\sqrt{2}}x_{\ell+1}^{\mathbf{i}_{\ell+1}}\Big),&\hskip-28.45274ptk=i_{\ell}\prime,\\
\displaystyle\widehat{\sigma}_{\ell}\Lambda_{\varepsilon}^{1/2}P_{s_{\ell},s_{\ell+1}}\Big(x_{\ell}^{i_{\ell}},x_{\ell+1}^{i_{\ell}/\mathbf{i}_{\ell+1}}+\mathds{1}_{\big\{{i_{\ell}=i_{\ell+1}\prime,\atop 1\leq\ell\leq m-1}\big\}}{\varepsilon}{\sqrt{2}}x_{\ell+1}^{\mathbf{i}_{\ell+1}}\Big),&\hskip-28.45274ptk=i_{\ell},\\
\widehat{\sigma}_{\ell}P_{s_{\ell},s_{\ell+1}}\Big(x_{\ell}^{k},x_{\ell+1}^{k/\mathbf{i}_{\ell+1}}+\mathds{1}_{\big\{{k=i_{\ell+1}\prime,\atop 1\leq\ell\leq m-1}\big\}}{\varepsilon}{\sqrt{2}}x_{\ell+1}^{\mathbf{i}_{\ell+1}}\Big),&\hskip-56.9055ptk\in\{1,\cdots,N\}\setminus\mathbf{i}_{\ell},\end{array}\right.

where
x_{m+1}^{k/\mathbf{i}_{m+1}}=x_{m+1}^{k}
by (
6.10
) an (
6.16
), and
[\cdot]_{\times}
denotes a multiplication column, as defined in (
2.41
).

Let us explain the definitions of the weights in more detail. First, all the weights in (
6.1.2
) and (
6.1.2
) are nonzero if and only if
\sigma_{\ell}=1
as
{\mathfrak{s}}^{\lambda,\phi}_{\varepsilon}>0
, and all the weights in (
6.1.2
) are nonzero if and only if
\sigma_{\ell}=0
. So these two subcollections of weights complement each other.
Second, in each of (
6.1.2
) and
(
6.1.2
),
x_{\ell+1}^{k/\mathbf{i}_{\ell+1}}
takes into account the possible interaction at time
s_{\ell+1}
, and one and only one indicator function takes the value
1
. Furthermore,
the heat kernels in (
6.1.2
)–(
6.1.2
) show
\mathcal{O}({\varepsilon})
-perturbations in the initial and terminal states.
Without these perturbations, some of the states become identical. For example, the heat kernels in the weights labelled by
i_{\ell}\prime
and
i_{\ell}
in (
6.1.2
) use the same initial condition
z_{\ell}^{\prime}/{\sqrt{2}}
when the perturbations are removed.

Definition 6.3.

(1
∘
) By writing “
\mathsf{w}:\mathsf{v}_{0}{\;\leftarrow\!\!\!\!\rightarrow\;}\mathsf{v}_{1}
,” we draw an edge for a nonzero weight
\mathsf{w}
as a straight line segment to link the two vertices
\mathsf{v}_{0}
and
\mathsf{v}_{1}
. By “
\mathsf{w}:\mathsf{v}_{0}{\,\leftrightsquigarrow\,}\mathsf{v}_{1}
,” a coiled line segment is drawn instead.

(2
∘
) Given a nonzero weight
\mathsf{w}
as defined in (
6.1.2
)–(
6.1.2
), we define
{\sf Initial}(\mathsf{w})
as the subset of
\bigcup_{\mathsf{v}\in\mathcal{V}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{\varepsilon}}{\sf Space}(\mathsf{v})
that specifies the initial states for all heat kernels and any
{\mathfrak{s}}^{\lambda,\phi}_{{\varepsilon}}
involved in
\mathsf{w}
. Similarly,
{\sf Terminal}(\mathsf{w})
denotes the corresponding subset determined by the terminal states.
\blacklozenge

Definition 6.4 (Weighted edges of
\mathcal{G}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{\varepsilon}
).

For any nonzero weight
\mathsf{w}
from (
6.1.2
)–(
6.1.2
),
we draw a unique edge for
\mathsf{w}
according to the following rule:

\displaystyle\mathsf{w}:\begin{cases}\mathsf{v}_{0}{\,\leftrightsquigarrow\,}\mathsf{v}_{1},&\mbox{if $\mathsf{w}={\mathsf{w}}_{\ell,\ell.5;k}$ for some $1\leq\ell\leq m$, and $k=i_{\ell}.5$ (cf. \eqref{weight2})},\\
\mathsf{v}_{0}{\;\leftarrow\!\!\!\!\rightarrow\;}\mathsf{v}_{1},&\mbox{otherwise,}\end{cases}

where
\mathsf{v}_{0},\mathsf{v}_{1}
are the unique vertices satisfying
{\sf Initial}(\mathsf{w})\cap{\sf Space}(\mathsf{v}_{0})\neq\varnothing
and
{\sf Terminal}(\mathsf{w})\cap{\sf Space}(\mathsf{v}_{1})\neq\varnothing
.

\blacklozenge

The uniqueness of
(\mathsf{v}_{0},\mathsf{v}_{1})
follows from our criterion for distinguishing vertices, as specified below (
6.11
), and our rule of identifying
(x_{0}^{k},s_{0})
and
(x_{0}^{\ell},s_{0})
as the same vertex when
x_{0}^{k}=x_{0}^{\ell}
, as mentioned below (
6.12
).
Consequently, there is at most one edge connecting any two distinct vertices with temporal components in
[s_{1},t]
. In contrast, multiple edges can connect two vertices whose temporal components lie in
[0,s_{1}]
. For example, in the graph over
[0,t]
in Figure
4
, the two green edges illustrate these multiple edges over
[0,s_{1}]
. This example is essentially the only case in which multiple edges can occur, as made precise in the following lemma. This scenario will be central to our analysis of the mixed moments.

Lemma 6.5.

There are multiple edges in
\mathcal{G}_{\varepsilon}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}
if and only if
x_{0}^{i_{1}\prime}=x_{0}^{i_{1}}
. In this case, the multiple edges occur in linking
(x_{0}^{i_{1}\prime},s_{0})=(x_{0}^{i_{1}},s_{0})
and
(x_{1}^{i_{1}\prime},x_{1}^{i_{1}},s_{1})
; there are exactly two such edges.

Up to this point, let us clarify how
\mathcal{G}_{\varepsilon}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}
depend on the underlying variables by writing

\mathcal{G}_{\varepsilon}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}=\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{i}_{1},\cdots,\mathbf{i}_{m};{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}}_{{\varepsilon};x_{0},\boldsymbol{x}_{m},x_{m+1}^{1},\cdots,x_{m+1}^{N},\boldsymbol{z}}(\boldsymbol{u}_{m},u_{m+1},\boldsymbol{v}_{m}).

Here,
\boldsymbol{x}_{m}{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm def}}{{=}}}\bigcup_{\mathsf{v}}{\sf Space}(\mathsf{v})
for
\mathsf{v}
ranging over the vertices in (
6.11b
),
\boldsymbol{z}\,{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm def}}{{=}}}\bigcup_{\mathsf{v}}{\sf Space}(\mathsf{v})
for
\mathsf{v}
ranging over the vertices in (
6.11c
), and we use the notations
\boldsymbol{u}_{m},u_{m+1},\boldsymbol{v}_{m}
in (
6.7
) and (
6.9
). Also,
\mathcal{G}_{\oslash}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}
denotes
the unweighted graph associated with
\mathcal{G}_{\varepsilon}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}
.

Step 2 (Subgraph decomposition).

In this step, we introduce a decomposition of
\mathcal{G}_{\varepsilon}^{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}}\setminus\{(x_{0}^{k},0)\}_{k=1}^{N}
into subgraphs denoted by

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}^{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}}_{{\varepsilon};\ell}=\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{i}_{1},\cdots,\mathbf{i}_{m};{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}}_{{\varepsilon};\ell;x_{0},\boldsymbol{x}_{m},x_{m+1}^{1},\cdots,x_{m+1}^{N},\boldsymbol{z}}(\boldsymbol{u}_{m},u_{m+1},\boldsymbol{v}_{m}),\quad\ell\in\{0,1,\cdots,m\}.

(6.31)

Each subgraph
\mathcal{G}^{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}}_{{\varepsilon};\ell}
is to be defined via its
unweighted
counterpart, denoted
\mathcal{G}_{\oslash;\ell}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}
; the edge weights for
\mathcal{G}_{{\varepsilon};\ell}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}
are then inherited from the corresponding edges in
\mathcal{G}_{\varepsilon}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}
. Moreover, our construction focuses exclusively on specifying the edge sets of
\mathcal{G}_{\oslash;\ell}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}
, as the vertex set
\mathcal{V}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{\ell}
of
\mathcal{G}_{\oslash;\ell}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}
consists of all vertices
\mathsf{v}
in
\mathcal{G}_{\oslash}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}
for which there is an edge in
\mathcal{G}_{\oslash;\ell}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}
adjacent to
\mathsf{v}
from the left of
\mathsf{v}
. Here and throughout, we interpret
“left” and “right”
with respect to increasing time, from left to right, as shown in Figure
2
.

To specify
\{\mathcal{G}^{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}}_{\oslash;\ell}\}_{\ell=0}^{m}
, we begin by introducing the following terminology on paths and related notions that can be drawn readily from
\mathcal{G}_{\oslash}^{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}}
.

Definition 6.6.

(1
∘
) A
path

\mathcal{P}
in
\mathcal{G}_{\oslash}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}
is a finite sequence of distinct edges such that (i) one is adjacent to the next and (ii) any given vertex of
\mathcal{G}_{\oslash}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}
is adjacent to at most two edges of
\mathcal{P}
. Moreover, if the set of
{\sf Time}(\mathsf{v})
, defined below (
6.12
), for all vertices
\mathsf{v}
adjacent to edges of
\mathcal{P}
can be linearly ordered and
t_{\min}
and
t_{\max}
denote the minimal and maximum of these
{\sf Time}(\mathsf{v})
, then
\mathcal{P}
is said to begin at
t_{\min}
and end at
t_{\max}
. Moreover, if we specify when a path ends, it implicitly implies that no two edges in the path span the same time interval.

(2
∘
) A path is
entanglement-free
if it contains neither (i) an edge represented by a coiled line segment nor (ii) a pair of edges adjacent to a vertex
\mathsf{v}=(x_{\ell}^{i_{\ell}^{\prime}},x_{\ell}^{i_{\ell}},s_{\ell})
, with one edge on the left and the other on the right of
\mathsf{v}
.

(3
∘
) A path is
left-maximal
if it cannot be extended to an entanglement-free path by adding more edges drawn as straight line segments over smaller time intervals.

(4
∘
) A
usual path
is a path such that (i) it begins at time
0
and ends at time
t
and (ii) none of its edges are adjacent to a vertex of the form
(x_{\ell}^{i_{\ell}\prime},x_{\ell}^{i_{\ell}},s_{\ell})
or to a vertex of the form
(z^{\prime}_{\ell},z_{\ell},\tau_{\ell})
.

(5
∘
) The
duration
of an edge adjacent to vertices
\mathsf{v}_{0}
and
\mathsf{v}_{1}
is
\lvert{\sf Time}(\mathsf{v}_{0})-{\sf Time}(\mathsf{v}_{1})\rvert
.
The duration of a path
\mathcal{P}
, denoted by
\lvert\mathcal{P}\rvert
, is the sum of the durations of its edges.

(6
∘
) Given a subgraph
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
, a vertex
\mathsf{v}
is a
left end-vertex
of
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
if
\mathsf{v}
is adjacent to
\mathsf{e}
for some edge
\mathsf{e}
of
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
, but there is no edge
\mathsf{e}^{\prime}
of
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
such that
\mathsf{v}
is adjacent to
\mathsf{e}^{\prime}
and
\mathsf{v}
defines the right endpoint of
\mathsf{e}^{\prime}
. Also, an edge of
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
is a
left end-edge
of
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
if it is adjacent to a left end-vertex of
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
. The
right end-vertices
and
right end-edges
of
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
are similarly defined. Moreover, since these definitions of left and right end-vertices use only edges of
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
, they allow straightforward extensions to any given set of edges.

\blacklozenge

The following properties are implicit in the above definitions: First, the definition of paths in (1
∘
) does not preclude the existence of two edges traversing the same time interval. For example, Figure
2
depicts two paths, marked green and blue, that stretch backward at times
s_{1}
and
s_{2}
, respectively. Also, Figure
2
shows two edges marked yellow such that they span
[s_{5},t]
and are adjacent to the same vertex at time
s_{5}
. These two edges thus define a path as well. Moreover, these paths in Figure
2
contrast the usual paths defined in (4
∘
), as the latter paths now obey the rule in (1
∘
) that two edges within a path spanning the same time interval are prohibited if we specify when the path ends. Finally, an entanglement-free path is equivalent to a path that contains no edges represented as colied line segments and “deflects” at any vertex in (
6.11b
)–(
6.11c
) represented by a triplet.

Definition 6.7 (Subgraphs
\{\mathcal{G}^{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}}_{\oslash;\ell}\}_{\ell=0}^{m}
).

(1
∘
) The edge set of
\mathcal{G}_{\oslash;0}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}
consists of the two unique edges whose right end-vertices coincide at time
s_{1}
.

(2
∘
) For any
\ell=1,\cdots,m-1
, the edge set of
\mathcal{G}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{\oslash;\ell}
consists of the following edges:

•

the edges of the two unique left-maximal entanglement-free paths, denoted by
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{\ell}
and
\mathcal{P}_{\ell}
, such that they end at time
s_{\ell+1}
and meet at the vertex
(x_{\ell+1}^{i_{\ell+1}\prime},x_{\ell+1}^{i_{\ell+1}},s_{\ell+1})
;

•

if
\sigma_{\ell}=1
, an additional edge given by the unique coiled line segment over
[s_{\ell},\tau_{\ell}]
.

Moreover, by concatenating the paths
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{\ell}
and
\mathcal{P}_{\ell}
, we obtain an entanglement-free path, which we call the
spine
of
\mathcal{G}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{\oslash;\ell}
and denote by
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{\ell}\oplus\mathcal{P}_{\ell}
.

(3
∘
) The edge set of
\mathcal{G}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{\oslash;m}
consists of the following edges:

•

the edges of all the left-maximal entanglement-free paths ending at time
t
;

•

if
\sigma_{m}=1
, an additional edge given by the unique coiled line segment over
[s_{m},\tau_{m}]
.
\blacklozenge

Let us highlight several properties of these subgraphs to facilitate later applications. First, note that the edge sets for
\ell=1,2,\ldots,m
satisfy the following:

•

For
\ell=1,2,\cdots,m-1
, the durations
\lvert\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{\ell}\rvert
and
\lvert\mathcal{P}_{\ell}\rvert
are bounded below by
s_{\ell+1}-\tau_{\ell}
; recall (
6.12
).
Lemma
6.8
below extends this observation by using the assumption
\mathbf{i}_{\ell}\neq\mathbf{i}_{\ell+1}
and will play a key role in the forthcoming proofs.

Lemma 6.8 (Lemma 4.9 of
[
18
]
).

For any fixed
\ell=1,\cdots,m-1
, at least one of the paths
\mathcal{P}_{\ell}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{P}_{\ell}
begins at time
\tau_{\ell-1}
or a smaller time (with
s_{0}=\tau_{0}=0
for
\ell=1
). Then
\lvert\mathcal{P}_{\ell}^{\prime}\oplus\mathcal{P}_{\ell}\rvert
is bounded below by
2(s_{\ell+1}-\tau_{\ell})+(s_{\ell}-\tau_{\ell-1})=2u_{\ell+1}+u_{\ell}
, where the relationship between
s_{\ell}
and
\tau_{\ell}
in (
6.12
) is used and

u_{\ell}
and
u_{\ell+1}
are defined in (
6.7
).

•

For
\ell=m
, since every usual path is a left-maximal entanglement-free path ending at time
t
by definition, all the edges of the usual paths are edges of
\mathcal{G}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{\oslash;m}
. In particular, there are no usual paths if
\mathbf{i}_{1}\cup\cdots\cup\mathbf{i}_{m}=\{1,\cdots,N\}
.

The next lemma concludes this step by confirming that
\{\mathcal{G}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{{\varepsilon};\ell}\}_{\ell=0}^{m}
decomposes
\mathcal{G}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{{\varepsilon}}
.

Lemma 6.9.

The edge set of
\mathcal{G}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{{\varepsilon}}
is the disjoint union of the edge sets of
\{\mathcal{G}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{{\varepsilon};\ell}\}_{\ell=0}^{m}
; the vertex set of
\mathcal{G}^{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}}_{{\varepsilon}}
is the disjoint union of
\{(x_{0}^{k},0)\}_{k=1}^{N}
and the vertex sets of
\{\mathcal{G}^{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}}_{{\varepsilon};\ell}\}_{\ell=0}^{m}
.

Proof.
The required property about edge sets has been established in
[
18
, Lemma 4.10]
. Consequently, the property for vertex sets holds if no two subgraphs share a vertex. To see this, it suffices to note that subgraphs are connected only via the vertices in (
6.11
) represented as triplets, while each such vertex belongs to one and only one subgraph by the definition of
\mathcal{V}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{\ell}
given below (
6.31
).

\blacksquare

Step 3 (Graphical integrals).

The
graphical integrals
refer to the unweighted integrals
\int{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{V}_{\ell}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}F
and the weighted integrals
\int{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{G}_{{\varepsilon};\ell}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}F
, which are defined as follows:

\displaystyle\int{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{V}_{\ell}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}F

\displaystyle\,{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm def}}{{=}}}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2\lvert\mathcal{I}_{\ell}\rvert}}{\mathrm{d}}\mu_{\ell}(a_{i};i\in\mathcal{I}_{\ell})F(a_{i};i\in\mathcal{I}_{\ell}),

(6.32a)

\displaystyle\int{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{G}_{{\varepsilon};\ell}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}F

\displaystyle\,{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm def}}{{=}}}\int{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{V}_{\ell}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}\Pi^{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}}_{{\varepsilon};\ell}F.

(6.32b)

Here,
\{a_{i};i\in\mathcal{I}_{\ell}\}=\bigcup\{{\sf Space}(\mathsf{v});\mathsf{v}\in\mathcal{V}_{\ell}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}\}
with
a_{i}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
,

\displaystyle\Pi^{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}}_{{\varepsilon};\ell}=\Pi^{\lambda,\phi;\mathbf{i}_{1},\cdots,\mathbf{i}_{m};{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}}_{{\varepsilon};\ell;\boldsymbol{x}_{m},x_{m+1}^{1},\cdots,x_{m+1}^{N},\boldsymbol{z}}(\boldsymbol{u}_{m},u_{m+1},\boldsymbol{v}_{m}){\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm def}}{{=}}}\prod\{\mbox{edge weights in $\mathcal{G}_{{\varepsilon};\ell}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}$}\},

(6.33)

and
{\mathrm{d}}\mu_{\ell}={\mathrm{d}}\mu_{\ell}(a_{i};i\in\mathcal{I}_{\ell})
is the product of all measures
from the following list that use
a_{i}
for some
i\in\mathcal{I}_{\ell}
as one variable of integration:

\displaystyle{\quad\;}{\mathrm{d}}x_{\ell}^{i_{\ell}\prime}\otimes{\mathrm{d}}x_{\ell}^{i_{\ell}}\varphi(x_{\ell}^{\mathbf{i}_{\ell}}),\;{\mathrm{d}}x_{\ell}^{k},\;\mbox{for }1\leq\ell\leq m,\;k\notin\mathbf{i}_{\ell};

(6.34a)

\displaystyle{\quad\;}{\mathrm{d}}z^{\prime}_{\ell}\otimes{\mathrm{d}}z_{\ell}\varphi(z_{\ell}),\;{\mathrm{d}}z_{\ell}^{k},\;\mbox{for $1\leq\ell\leq m$ such that }\sigma_{\ell}=1,\;k\notin\mathbf{i}_{\ell};

(6.34b)

\displaystyle{\quad\;}{\mathrm{d}}x_{m+1}^{k},\;\mbox{for }1\leq k\leq N.

(6.34c)

For example, given
0\leq\ell\leq m-1
, as
(x_{\ell+1}^{i_{\ell+1}\prime},x_{\ell+1}^{i_{\ell+1}},s_{\ell+1})
must be a vertex of
\mathcal{V}_{\ell}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}
, the measure
{\mathrm{d}}x_{\ell+1}^{i_{\ell+1}\prime}\otimes{\mathrm{d}}x_{\ell+1}^{i_{\ell+1}}\varphi(x_{\ell}^{\mathbf{i}_{\ell+1}})
is part of
{\mathrm{d}}\mu_{\ell}
. By the definition of
\mathcal{V}_{\varepsilon}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}
in (
6.11
) and Lemma
6.9
, all of the measures in
(
6.34
) are used to define the graphical integrals, but none of them are used twice to define
\int{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{V}_{\ell}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}F
and
\int{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{V}_{\ell^{\prime}}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}F^{\prime}
for
\ell\neq\ell^{\prime}
.

6.1.3
Bounds on the mixed moments

We now begin the proof of Theorem
3.4
, focusing primarily on establishing (1
∘
); the arguments for (2
∘
) and (3
∘
) will follow by appropriately modifying certain steps of this proof. To prove Theorem
3.4
(1
∘
), we first introduce two lemmas, stated as Lemmas
6.10
and
6.11
below. Lemma
6.11
demonstrates series expansions for the mixed moments in Theorem
3.4
(1
∘
), relying on the series expansions for second moments given in Lemma
6.10
and an extension of the series in (
6.4
).

For the statement of Lemma
6.10
, recall that
[\cdot]_{\times}
and
[\cdot]_{\otimes}
are defined in and below (
2.41
).

Lemma 6.10.

With
\mathbf{i}_{0}\,{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm def}}{{=}}}\,(2,1)
, it holds that, for all
f\geq 0
,

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}^{({B}^{1},{B}^{2})}_{({x}^{1}_{0},{x}^{2}_{0})}\Bigg[\exp\Bigg\{\Lambda_{\varepsilon}\int_{0}^{{t}}{\mathrm{d}}r\varphi_{\varepsilon}({B}^{\mathbf{i}_{0}}_{r})\Bigg\}f({B}_{{t}})\Bigg]

(6.35)

\displaystyle\quad={\mathbb{E}}^{({B}^{1},{B}^{2})}_{({x}^{1}_{0},{x}^{2}_{0})}[f({B}_{{t}})]+\int_{0}^{{t}}{\mathrm{d}}{s}_{1}{\mathbb{E}}^{({B}^{1},{B}^{2})}_{({x}^{1}_{0},{x}^{2}_{0})}\Bigg[\varphi_{\varepsilon}({B}^{\mathbf{i}_{0}}_{{s}_{1}})\Lambda_{\varepsilon}\exp\Bigg\{\Lambda_{\varepsilon}\int_{{s}_{1}}^{{t}}{\mathrm{d}}r\varphi_{\varepsilon}({B}^{\mathbf{i}_{0}}_{r})\Bigg\}f({B}_{{t}})\Bigg]

\displaystyle\quad={\mathbb{E}}^{({B}^{1},{B}^{2})}_{({x}^{1}_{0},{x}^{2}_{0})}[f({B}_{{t}})]+\int_{0}^{{t}}{\mathrm{d}}{s}_{1}

\displaystyle\quad\quad{\mathbb{E}}^{({B}^{1},{B}^{2})}_{({x}^{1}_{0},{x}^{2}_{0})}\Bigg[\varphi_{\varepsilon}({B}^{\mathbf{i}_{0}}_{{s}_{1}})\left(\Lambda_{\varepsilon}+\int_{{s}_{1}}^{{{t}}}{\mathrm{d}}{\tau}_{1}\Lambda_{\varepsilon}^{2}\exp\Bigg\{\Lambda_{\varepsilon}\int_{{s}_{1}}^{{\tau}_{1}}{\mathrm{d}}r\varphi_{\varepsilon}({B}^{\mathbf{i}_{0}}_{r})\Bigg\}\varphi_{\varepsilon}(B^{\mathbf{i}_{0}}_{{\tau}_{1}})\right)f({B}_{{t}})\Bigg]

(6.36)

\displaystyle\quad={\mathbb{E}}^{({B}_{1},{B}_{2})}_{({x}_{0}^{1},{x}_{0}^{2})}[{f}({B}_{{t}})]+\sum_{{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}}\in\{0,1\}}\int_{0}^{t}{\mathrm{d}}s_{1}\int_{[s_{1},t)}{\mathrm{d}}\tau_{1}\Bigg(\prod_{\ell:{\sigma}_{\ell}=0}\delta_{s_{\ell}}({\tau}_{\ell})\Bigg)\int{\mathrm{d}}{\mathcal{G}}^{{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}}}_{{\varepsilon};0}\int{\mathrm{d}}{\mathcal{G}}^{{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}}}_{{\varepsilon};1}f.

(6.37)

Proof.
The first two equalities follow from the fundamental theorem of calculus, applied to functions of the form
s\mapsto\exp\{\int_{s}^{t}a(r)\,\mathrm{d}r\}
and
\tau\mapsto\exp\{\int_{s}^{\tau}a(r)\,\mathrm{d}r\}
.
To obtain (
6.37
), it suffices to identify the weights defined in (
6.1.2
)–(
6.1.2
) from the following formulas, which can be deduced from the Markov property of Brownian motion:

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}{\mathrm{d}}s_{1}{\mathbb{E}}^{B}_{x_{0}}[\varphi_{\varepsilon}(B^{\mathbf{i}_{0}}_{s_{1}})\Lambda_{\varepsilon}f(B^{1}_{t},B^{2}_{t})]

\displaystyle\quad=\int_{0}^{t}{\mathrm{d}}s_{1}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{4}}\begin{bmatrix}{\mathrm{d}}x_{1}^{2}\\
{\mathrm{d}}x_{1}^{1}\end{bmatrix}_{\otimes}\varphi(x_{1}^{\mathbf{i}_{0}})\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{4}}\begin{bmatrix}{\mathrm{d}}x_{2}^{2}\\
{\mathrm{d}}x_{2}^{1}\end{bmatrix}_{\otimes}\begin{bmatrix}P_{0,s_{1}}(x_{0}^{2},x_{1}^{1}+{\varepsilon}{\sqrt{2}}x^{\mathbf{i}_{0}}_{1})\\
P_{0,s_{1}}(x_{0}^{1},x_{1}^{1})\end{bmatrix}_{\times}

\displaystyle\quad\quad\times\begin{bmatrix}\Lambda_{\varepsilon}^{1/2}P_{s_{1},t}(x_{1}^{1}+{\varepsilon}{\sqrt{2}}x_{1}^{\mathbf{i}_{0}},x_{2}^{2})\\
\displaystyle\Lambda_{\varepsilon}^{1/2}P_{s_{1},t}(x_{1}^{1},x_{2}^{1})\end{bmatrix}_{\times}f(x^{1}_{2},x_{2}^{2}),

(6.38)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}{\mathrm{d}}s_{1}\int_{s_{1}}^{t}{\mathrm{d}}\tau_{1}{\mathbb{E}}^{B}_{x_{0}}\left[\varphi_{\varepsilon}(B^{\mathbf{i}_{0}}_{s})\Lambda_{\varepsilon}^{2}\exp\Bigg\{\Lambda_{\varepsilon}\int_{{s}_{1}}^{{\tau}_{1}}{\mathrm{d}}r\varphi_{\varepsilon}({B}^{\mathbf{i}_{0}}_{r})\Bigg\}\varphi_{\varepsilon}(B^{\mathbf{i}_{0}}_{\tau_{1}})f(B_{t})\right]

\displaystyle\quad=\int_{0}^{t}{\mathrm{d}}s_{1}\int_{s_{1}}^{t}{\mathrm{d}}\tau_{1}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{4}}\begin{bmatrix}{\mathrm{d}}x_{1}^{2}\\
{\mathrm{d}}x_{1}^{1}\end{bmatrix}_{\otimes}\varphi(x_{1}^{\mathbf{i}_{0}})\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{4}}\begin{bmatrix}{\mathrm{d}}z_{1}^{\prime}\\
{\mathrm{d}}z_{1}\end{bmatrix}_{\otimes}\varphi(z_{1})\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{4}}\begin{bmatrix}{\mathrm{d}}x_{2}^{2}\\
{\mathrm{d}}x_{2}^{1}\end{bmatrix}_{\otimes}\begin{bmatrix}P_{0,s_{1}}(x_{0}^{2},x_{1}^{1}+{\varepsilon}{\sqrt{2}}x^{\mathbf{i}_{0}}_{1})\\
P_{0,s_{1}}(x_{0}^{1},x_{1}^{1})\end{bmatrix}_{\times}

\displaystyle\quad\quad\times\begin{bmatrix}P_{s_{1},\tau_{1}}({\sqrt{2}}x_{1}^{1}+{\varepsilon}x_{1}^{\mathbf{i}_{0}},z^{\prime}_{1})\\
\vskip-8.5359pt\\
{\mathfrak{s}}^{\lambda,\phi}_{\varepsilon}(s_{1},\tau_{1};x_{1}^{\mathbf{i}_{0}},z_{1})\end{bmatrix}_{\times}\begin{bmatrix}P_{\tau_{1},t}(\frac{z_{1}^{\prime}+{\varepsilon}z_{1}}{{\sqrt{2}}},x_{2}^{2})\\
P_{\tau_{1},t}(\frac{z_{1}^{\prime}-{\varepsilon}z_{1}}{{\sqrt{2}}},x_{2}^{1})\end{bmatrix}_{\times}f(x^{1}_{2},x_{2}^{2}).

(6.39)

More details of the derivation of (
6.38
) and (
6.39
) can be found in
[
18
, Lemma 4.4]
. Equation (
6.37
) now follows by using the definitions in (
6.32
).

\blacksquare

The next result, Lemma
6.11
, requires combining the definitions from Section
6.1.2
for both
N=2
and
N=4
, since its formulation simultaneously involves a two-particle and a four-particle system. To distinguish between these systems, we will append “
\;\,\widetilde{\mbox{}}\,\;
” to the notation associated with a two-particle system. For example,
B^{1}
and
B^{2}
in (
6.35
) with
N=2
will henceforth be rewritten as
\widetilde{B}^{1}
and
\widetilde{B}^{2}
. Let us also adopt the following convention: for
0<t<t^{\prime}<\infty
,

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\int{\mathrm{d}}\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}^{\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}}}_{{\varepsilon};0}\int{\mathrm{d}}\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}^{\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}}}_{{\varepsilon};1}\int{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{G}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{{\varepsilon};0}\int{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{G}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{{\varepsilon};1}\cdots\int{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{G}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{{\varepsilon};m}f=\int{\mathrm{d}}\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}^{\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}}}_{{\varepsilon};0}\int{\mathrm{d}}\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}^{\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}}}_{{\varepsilon};1}F\end{split}

(6.40)

for
F
defined by the following function:

F:(\widetilde{x}_{2}^{1},\widetilde{x}_{2}^{2})\mapsto\int{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{G}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{{\varepsilon};0}\int{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{G}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{{\varepsilon};1}\cdots\int{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{G}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{{\varepsilon};m}f\mid_{x_{0}^{3}=\widetilde{x}^{1}_{2},x_{0}^{4}=\widetilde{x}^{2}_{2}}

and
\int{\mathrm{d}}\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}^{\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}}}_{{\varepsilon};0}\int{\mathrm{d}}\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}^{\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}}}_{{\varepsilon};1}F
defined by using
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}^{\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}}}_{\varepsilon}
over
[0,t^{\prime}-t]
. Here, as before, the iterated graphical integral
\int{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{G}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{{\varepsilon};0}\int{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{G}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{{\varepsilon};1}\cdots\int{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{G}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{{\varepsilon};m}f
refers to integration with respect to
\mathcal{G}^{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}}_{\varepsilon}
over
[0,t]
with
N=4
, where the initial state at time
0
is
(x_{0}^{1},x_{0}^{2},x_{0}^{3},x_{0}^{4})
and the terminal state at time
t
is
(x_{m+1}^{1},x_{m+1}^{2},x_{m+1}^{3},x_{m+1}^{4})
. In contrast, for
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}^{\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}}}_{\varepsilon}
, the particles begin at
(\widetilde{x}_{0}^{1},\widetilde{x}_{0}^{2})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
at time
0
and evolve to
(\widetilde{x}_{2}^{1},\widetilde{x}_{2}^{2})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
at time
t^{\prime}-t
.

Lemma 6.11.

For any
{\varepsilon}\in(0,{{\varepsilon}_{\ref{def:ovl}}(\lambda)}]
,
0<t<t^{\prime}<\infty
, and
x_{0}^{1},x_{0}^{2},\widetilde{x}_{0}^{1},\widetilde{x}_{0}^{2}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
, it holds that

\displaystyle\Lambda_{\varepsilon}^{2}{\mathbb{E}}[X_{\varepsilon}(x^{1}_{0},t)X_{\varepsilon}(x^{2}_{0},t)X_{\varepsilon}(\widetilde{x}_{0}^{1},t^{\prime})X_{\varepsilon}(\widetilde{x}_{0}^{2},t^{\prime})]

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\quad=\Lambda_{\varepsilon}^{2}{\mathbb{E}}^{(\widetilde{B}^{1},\widetilde{B}^{2})}_{(\widetilde{x}_{0}^{1},\widetilde{x}_{0}^{2})}\Biggl[\exp\Biggl\{\Lambda_{\varepsilon}\int_{0}^{t^{\prime}-t}{\mathrm{d}}r\varphi_{\varepsilon}(\widetilde{B}^{\mathbf{i}_{0}}_{r})\Biggr\}\\
&\quad\quad\times{\mathbb{E}}^{(B^{1},B^{2},B^{3},B^{4})}_{(x_{0}^{1},x_{0}^{2},\widetilde{B}^{1}_{t^{\prime}-t},\widetilde{B}^{2}_{t^{\prime}-t})}\Biggl[\exp\Biggl\{\sum_{\mathbf{i}\in\mathcal{E}_{4}}\Lambda_{\varepsilon}\int_{0}^{t}{\mathrm{d}}r\varphi_{\varepsilon}(B^{\mathbf{i}}_{r})\Biggr\}\prod_{j=1}^{4}X_{0}(B^{j}_{t})\Biggr]\Biggr].\end{split}

(6.41)

Also, with the notation used in (
6.8
) and the function
\widetilde{f}_{t}(\widetilde{x}^{1}_{2},\widetilde{x}^{2}_{2})\,{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm def}}{{=}}}\,{\mathbb{E}}^{(B^{1},B^{2},B^{3},B^{4})}_{(x_{0}^{1},x_{0}^{2},\widetilde{x}^{1}_{2},\widetilde{x}^{2}_{2})}[f(B_{t})]

for nonnegative
f
defined on
({\mathbb{R}}^{2})^{4}
, we have

\displaystyle\Lambda_{\varepsilon}^{2}{\mathbb{E}}^{(\widetilde{B}^{1},\widetilde{B}^{2})}_{(\widetilde{x}_{0}^{1},\widetilde{x}_{0}^{2})}\Biggl[\exp\Bigg\{\Lambda_{\varepsilon}\int_{0}^{t^{\prime}-t}{\mathrm{d}}r\varphi_{\varepsilon}(\widetilde{B}^{\mathbf{i}_{0}}_{r})\Biggr\}

\displaystyle\times{\mathbb{E}}^{(B^{1},B^{2},B^{3},B^{4})}_{(x_{0}^{1},x_{0}^{2},\widetilde{B}^{1}_{t^{\prime}-t},\widetilde{B}^{2}_{t^{\prime}-t})}\Biggl[\exp\Biggl\{\sum_{\mathbf{i}\in\mathcal{E}_{4}}\Lambda_{\varepsilon}\int_{0}^{t}{\mathrm{d}}r\varphi_{\varepsilon}(B^{\mathbf{i}}_{r})\Biggr\}f(B_{t})\Biggr]\Biggr]

\displaystyle\quad=\Lambda_{\varepsilon}^{2}{\mathbb{E}}^{(\widetilde{B}^{1},\widetilde{B}^{2})}_{(\widetilde{x}_{0}^{1},\widetilde{x}_{0}^{2})}\Biggl[\exp\Biggl\{\Lambda_{\varepsilon}\int_{0}^{t^{\prime}-t}{\mathrm{d}}r\varphi_{\varepsilon}(\widetilde{B}^{\mathbf{i}_{0}}_{r})\Biggr\}\widetilde{f}_{t}(\widetilde{B}_{t^{\prime}-t})\Biggr]

\displaystyle\quad\quad+\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}\sum_{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptstyle\mathbf{i}_{1},\cdots,\mathbf{i}_{m}\in\mathcal{E}_{4}}}{{\mathbf{i}_{1}\neq\cdots\neq\mathbf{i}_{m}}}}\sum_{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}\in\{0,1\}^{m}}\Lambda_{\varepsilon}^{2}{\mathbb{E}}^{(\widetilde{B}^{1},\widetilde{B}^{2})}_{(\widetilde{x}_{0}^{1},\widetilde{x}_{0}^{2})}\Biggl[\int_{\Delta_{m}(t)}{\mathrm{d}}\boldsymbol{u}_{m}\otimes{\mathrm{d}}\boldsymbol{v}_{m}\Biggl(\,\prod_{\scriptstyle\ell:\sigma_{\ell}=0\atop\scriptstyle 1\leq\ell\leq m}\delta_{0}(v_{\ell})\Biggr)

\displaystyle\quad\quad\times\int{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{G}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{{\varepsilon};0}\cdots\int{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{G}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{{\varepsilon};m}f\mid_{(x_{0}^{3},x_{0}^{4})=(\widetilde{B}^{1}_{t^{\prime}-t},\widetilde{B}^{2}_{t^{\prime}-t})}\Biggr]

\displaystyle\quad\quad+\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}\sum_{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptstyle\mathbf{i}_{1},\cdots,\mathbf{i}_{m}\in\mathcal{E}_{4}}}{{\mathbf{i}_{1}\neq\cdots\neq\mathbf{i}_{m}}}}\sum_{\scriptstyle\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}}\in\{0,1\}\atop\scriptstyle{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}\in\{0,1\}^{m}}\Lambda_{\varepsilon}^{2}\int_{0}^{t^{\prime}-t}{\mathrm{d}}\widetilde{s}_{1}\int_{[\widetilde{s}_{1},t^{\prime}-t)}{\mathrm{d}}\widetilde{\tau}_{1}\int_{\Delta_{m}(t)}{\mathrm{d}}\boldsymbol{u}_{m}\otimes{\mathrm{d}}\boldsymbol{v}_{m}

\displaystyle\quad\quad\times\Biggl(\,\prod_{\scriptstyle\ell:\widetilde{\sigma}_{\ell}=0\atop\scriptstyle\ell=1}\delta_{\widetilde{s}_{\ell}}(\widetilde{\tau}_{\ell})\Biggr)\Biggl(\,\prod_{\scriptstyle\ell:\sigma_{\ell}=0\atop\scriptstyle 1\leq\ell\leq m}\delta_{0}(v_{\ell})\Biggr)

\displaystyle\quad\quad\times\int{\mathrm{d}}\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}^{\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}}}_{{\varepsilon};0}\int{\mathrm{d}}\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}^{\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}}}_{{\varepsilon};1}\int{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{G}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{{\varepsilon};0}\int{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{G}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{{\varepsilon};1}\cdots\int{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{G}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{{\varepsilon};m}f.

(6.42)

Proof.
To prove (
6.41
),
define a Borel measure
\mathbf{m}^{{\varepsilon},N}_{x_{0};t}({\mathrm{d}}y_{0})
on
{\mathbb{R}}^{2N}
by the following equation:

\displaystyle\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2N}}\mathbf{m}^{{\varepsilon},N}_{x_{0};t}({\mathrm{d}}y_{0})f(y_{0})={\mathbb{E}}^{B}_{x_{0}}\Biggl[\exp\Biggl\{\sum_{\mathbf{i}\in\mathcal{E}_{N}}\Lambda_{\varepsilon}\int_{0}^{t}{\mathrm{d}}r\varphi_{\varepsilon}(B^{\mathbf{i}}_{r})\Biggr\}f(B_{t})\Biggr].

(6.43)

Then for
0<t<t^{\prime}<\infty
,
by using the Markov property of
X_{\varepsilon}
at time
t
, (
6.1
) with
N=2
, and (
6.43
) with
N=2
, we get

\displaystyle\Lambda_{\varepsilon}^{2}{\mathbb{E}}[X_{\varepsilon}(x^{1}_{0},t)X_{\varepsilon}(x^{2}_{0},t)X_{\varepsilon}(\widetilde{x}_{0}^{1},t^{\prime})X_{\varepsilon}(\widetilde{x}_{0}^{2},t^{\prime})]

\displaystyle\quad=\Lambda_{\varepsilon}^{2}{\mathbb{E}}\left[X_{\varepsilon}(x^{1}_{0},t)X_{\varepsilon}(x^{2}_{0},t)\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{4}}\mathbf{m}^{{\varepsilon},2}_{\widetilde{x}^{1}_{0},\widetilde{x}^{2}_{0};t^{\prime}-t}({\mathrm{d}}y^{3},{\mathrm{d}}y^{4})X_{\varepsilon}(y^{3},t)X_{\varepsilon}(y^{4},t)\right]

\displaystyle\quad=\Lambda_{\varepsilon}^{2}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{4}}\mathbf{m}^{{\varepsilon},2}_{\widetilde{x}^{1}_{0},\widetilde{x}^{2}_{0};t^{\prime}-t}({\mathrm{d}}y^{3},{\mathrm{d}}y^{4}){\mathbb{E}}[X_{\varepsilon}(x^{1}_{0},t)X_{\varepsilon}(x^{2}_{0},t)X_{\varepsilon}(y^{3},t)X_{\varepsilon}(y^{4},t)].

To finish the proof of (
6.41
), it suffices to apply (
6.1
) with
N=4
and
(
6.43
) with
N=2
in the same order to the right-hand side.

Next, to get (
6.42
), we use (
6.4
) and (
6.8
). Note that all of the summands in (
6.4
) are nonnegative. Hence, by the monotone convergence theorem, we can write the left-hand side of (
6.42
)
as a series of expectations:

\displaystyle\Lambda_{\varepsilon}^{2}{\mathbb{E}}^{(\widetilde{B}^{1},\widetilde{B}^{2})}_{(\widetilde{x}_{0}^{1},\widetilde{x}_{0}^{2})}\Biggl[\exp\Biggl\{\Lambda_{\varepsilon}\int_{0}^{t^{\prime}-t}{\mathrm{d}}r\varphi_{\varepsilon}(\widetilde{B}^{\mathbf{i}_{0}}_{r})\Biggr\}{\mathbb{E}}^{(B^{1},B^{2},B^{3},B^{4})}_{(x_{0}^{1},x_{0}^{2},\widetilde{B}^{1}_{t^{\prime}-t},\widetilde{B}^{2}_{t^{\prime}-t})}\Biggl[\exp\Biggl\{\sum_{\mathbf{i}\in\mathcal{E}_{4}}\Lambda_{\varepsilon}\int_{0}^{t}{\mathrm{d}}r\varphi_{\varepsilon}(B^{\mathbf{i}}_{r})\Biggr\}f(B_{t})\Biggr]\Biggr]

\displaystyle\quad=\Lambda_{\varepsilon}^{2}{\mathbb{E}}^{(\widetilde{B}^{1},\widetilde{B}^{2})}_{(\widetilde{x}_{0}^{1},\widetilde{x}_{0}^{2})}\Biggl[\exp\Biggl\{\Lambda_{\varepsilon}\int_{0}^{t^{\prime}-t}{\mathrm{d}}r\varphi_{\varepsilon}(\widetilde{B}^{\mathbf{i}_{0}}_{r})\Biggr\}{\mathbb{E}}^{(B^{1},B^{2},B^{3},B^{4})}_{(x_{0}^{1},x_{0}^{2},\widetilde{B}^{1}_{t^{\prime}-t},\widetilde{B}^{2}_{t^{\prime}-t})}[f(B_{t})]\Biggr]

\displaystyle\quad\quad+\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}\sum_{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptstyle\mathbf{i}_{1},\cdots,\mathbf{i}_{m}\in\mathcal{E}_{4}}}{{\mathbf{i}_{1}\neq\cdots\neq\mathbf{i}_{m}}}}\sum_{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}\in\{0,1\}^{m}}\Lambda_{\varepsilon}^{2}{\mathbb{E}}^{(\widetilde{B}^{1},\widetilde{B}^{2})}_{(\widetilde{x}_{0}^{1},\widetilde{x}_{0}^{2})}\Biggl[\exp\Biggl\{\Lambda_{\varepsilon}\int_{0}^{t^{\prime}-t}{\mathrm{d}}r\varphi_{\varepsilon}(\widetilde{B}^{\mathbf{i}_{0}}_{r})\Biggr\}\int_{\Delta_{m}(t)}{\mathrm{d}}\boldsymbol{u}_{m}\otimes{\mathrm{d}}\boldsymbol{v}_{m}

\displaystyle\quad\quad\times\Biggl(\,\prod_{\scriptstyle\ell:\sigma_{\ell}=0\atop\scriptstyle 1\leq\ell\leq m}\delta_{0}(v_{\ell})\Biggr)\int{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{G}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{{\varepsilon};0}\int{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{G}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{{\varepsilon};1}\cdots\int{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{G}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{{\varepsilon};m}f\mid_{x_{0}^{3}=\widetilde{B}^{1}_{t^{\prime}-t},x_{0}^{4}=\widetilde{B}^{2}_{t^{\prime}-t}}\Biggr].

(6.44)

With the function
\widetilde{f}_{t}
defined below (
6.41
), the first term on the right-hand side of (
6.44
) can be written as the first term on the right-hand side of (
6.42
).
Also, applying (
6.37
) with
t
replaced by
t^{\prime}-t
to the second term on the right-hand side of (
6.44
) yields the last two terms on the right-hand side of (
6.42
). The proof is complete.

\blacksquare

Figure 5:
\mathbf{i}_{1}=(3,2)

The following definition will facilitate subsequent applications of (
6.40
) and Lemma
6.11
.

Definition 6.12.

Let
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}^{\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}}}_{\varepsilon}\oplus\mathcal{G}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{\varepsilon}
denote the graph obtained by glueing
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\varepsilon}^{\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}}}
over
[0,t^{\prime}-t]
and
\mathcal{G}_{\varepsilon}^{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}}
over
[0,t]
such that the following identification of vertices holds:

(\widetilde{x}_{2}^{1},t^{\prime}-t)
is identified with
(x_{0}^{3},0)
and
(\widetilde{x}_{2}^{2},t^{\prime}-t)
is identified with
(x_{0}^{4},0)
.
\blacklozenge

In the remainder of Section
6.1.3
, our objective is to establish bounds for the right-hand side of (
6.42
), and thereby, end the proof of Theorem
3.4
. To obtain bounds for the right-hand side of (
6.42
), we will show that the first term there can be uniformly bounded with respect to
(\widetilde{x}^{1}_{0},\widetilde{x}^{2}_{0})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
and
t\in[0,T]
for any
0<T<\infty
. The primary focus, however, is on the second and third terms, which require a more intricate analysis that simultaneously encompasses both two- and four-particle systems.

To address the second and third terms, each represented by a triple series, the key observation is that the bounds from
[
18
, Proposition 4.14]
for other moments of
X_{\varepsilon}
can be extended when we interpret the summands in those triple series in terms of the combined graph
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}^{\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}}}_{\varepsilon}\oplus\mathcal{G}^{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}}_{\varepsilon}
, rather than considering
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}^{\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}}}_{\varepsilon}
and
\mathcal{G}^{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}}_{\varepsilon}
separately. This extension relies on the fact that by using edges of
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}^{\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}}}_{\varepsilon}
, some of the paths in
\mathcal{G}^{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}}_{\varepsilon}
can be extended in any case of
\mathbf{i}_{1}
. Morover, to identify these extended paths, it suffices to distinguish
\mathbf{i}_{1}
in three scenarios: (i)
\mathbf{i}_{1}=(2,1)
; (ii)
\mathbf{i}_{1}=(4,3)
; and (iii)
\mathbf{i}_{1}=(i,j)
for
i\in\{4,3\}
and
j\in\{2,1\}
. [This classification is due to symmetry; recall (
6.41
) and (
6.42
). More details appear in the proof of Theorem
3.4
.] Thus, we focus on the following three scenarios:

(i)

\mathbf{i}_{1}=(2,1)
: the duration of the spine in
\mathcal{G}^{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}}_{1;{\varepsilon}}
can be increased by
t^{\prime}-t-\widetilde{\tau}_{1}
(see Figure
4
).

(ii)

\mathbf{i}_{1}=(4,3)
: the durations of both edges in
\mathcal{G}^{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}}_{{\varepsilon};0}
can be extended by
t^{\prime}-t-\widetilde{\tau}_{1}
(see Figure
4
).

(iii)

\mathbf{i}_{1}=(3,2)
:
the duration of an edge in
\mathcal{G}^{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}}_{{\varepsilon};0}
can be extended by
t^{\prime}-t-\widetilde{\tau}_{1}
(see Figure
5
).

We now introduce a collection of tools and establish two principal sets of bounds, formulated as Propositions
6.14
and
6.17
. First, we use the following notation extending (
6.19
):

\displaystyle\begin{split}\overline{{\mathfrak{s}}}^{\lambda,\phi}_{\varepsilon}(\tau)&\,{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm def}}{{=}}}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{4}}{\mathrm{d}}x\otimes{\mathrm{d}}\tilde{y}\phi(x){\mathfrak{s}}^{\lambda,\phi}_{\varepsilon}(0,\tau;x/{\sqrt{2}},\tilde{y})\varphi(\tilde{y})\\
&=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{4}}{\mathrm{d}}x^{i_{\ell}\prime}_{\ell}\otimes{\mathrm{d}}z_{\ell}\varphi(x_{\ell}^{\mathbf{i}_{\ell}}){\mathfrak{s}}^{\lambda,\phi}_{\varepsilon}(0,\tau;x^{\mathbf{i}_{\ell}}_{\ell},z_{\ell})\varphi(z_{\ell})\\
&=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}{\mathrm{d}}x\phi(x){\mathbb{E}}_{{\varepsilon}x}^{{\sqrt{2}}W}\left[\Lambda_{\varepsilon}^{2}\exp\left\{\Lambda_{\varepsilon}\int_{0}^{\tau}{\mathrm{d}}r\varphi_{\varepsilon}(W_{r})\right\}\varphi_{\varepsilon}(W_{\tau})\right],\end{split}

(6.45)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{S}^{\lambda,\phi}_{\varepsilon}(q)

\displaystyle\,{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm def}}{{=}}}\int_{0}^{\infty}{\mathrm{d}}\tau{\mathrm{e}}^{-q\tau}\overline{{\mathfrak{s}}}^{\lambda,\phi}_{\varepsilon}(\tau).

(6.46)

The following lemma collects some key properties of
\overline{{\mathfrak{s}}}^{\lambda,\phi}_{\varepsilon}(\tau)
and
\mathfrak{S}^{\lambda,\phi}_{\varepsilon}(q)
.

Lemma 6.13.

Recall that
\Lambda_{\varepsilon}
is defined by (
1.5
), using a given constant
\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}
.

(1
∘
)

There exists
q_{\ref{def:Sconv}}=q_{\ref{def:Sconv}}(\lambda,\lVert\varphi\rVert_{\infty})\in(0,\infty)
such that

\displaystyle\lim_{{\varepsilon}\to 0}\mathfrak{S}^{\lambda,\phi}_{\varepsilon}(q)=\frac{4\pi}{\log(q/\beta)},\quad\forall\;q\in(q_{\ref{def:Sconv}},\infty),

(6.47)

where
\beta>0
is defined as in (
1.17
).

(2
∘
)

For all
t\in(0,\infty)
and
{\varepsilon}\in(0,{{\varepsilon}_{\ref{def:ovl}}(\lambda)}]
,

\displaystyle\sup_{y\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}\int_{0}^{t}{\mathrm{d}}\tau\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}{\mathrm{d}}\tilde{y}{\mathfrak{s}}^{\lambda,\phi}_{\varepsilon}(0,\tau;y,\tilde{y})\varphi(\tilde{y})

\displaystyle=\sup_{y\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}\int_{0}^{t}{\mathrm{d}}\tau\Lambda_{\varepsilon}^{2}{\mathbb{E}}^{W}_{{\varepsilon}y}\left[\exp\left\{\Lambda_{\varepsilon}\int_{0}^{\tau}{\mathrm{d}}r\varphi_{\varepsilon}(W_{r})\right\}\varphi_{\varepsilon}(W_{\tau})\right]

\displaystyle\leq C(\lambda,\lVert\varphi\rVert_{\infty})\lvert\Lambda_{\varepsilon}\rvert[1+\log^{+}({\varepsilon}^{-2}t)]{\mathrm{e}}^{q_{\ref{ineq:apriori}}(\lambda,\lVert\varphi\rVert_{\infty})t}.

(6.48)

Proof.
The proof of (
6.47
) and the proof of (
6.48
) whenever
{\varepsilon}\in(0,{\varepsilon}(\lambda,\lVert\varphi\rVert_{\infty}))
are already obtained in

[
18
, Proposition 5.2, p.178]
and
[
18
, Lemma 5.4, p.180]
, respectively, provided that
{\varepsilon}(\lambda,\lVert\varphi\rVert_{\infty})>0
is a small enough constant.
Moreover, by enlarging
q_{\ref{ineq:apriori}}(\lambda,\lVert\varphi\rVert_{\infty})
if necessary, it is plain that (
6.48
) also holds for all
{\varepsilon}\in[{\varepsilon}(\lambda,\lVert\varphi\rVert_{\infty}),{{\varepsilon}_{\ref{def:ovl}}(\lambda)}]
.

\blacksquare

We now proceed to the proofs of two principal sets of bounds. The following proposition establishes the first set of results, concerning the iterated graphical integrals in (
6.42
) for
f\equiv\mathds{1}
. For the statement, recall the change of variables in (
6.7
), and we use the following notations:
\prod_{\ell=j}^{k}=\prod_{\ell\in\varnothing}\equiv 1
for all
k<j
, and

\displaystyle\widehat{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}}=(\widehat{\sigma}_{1},\cdots,\widehat{\sigma}_{m})\,{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm def}}{{=}}}\,(1-\sigma_{1},\cdots,1-\sigma_{m}),\quad\lVert{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}\rVert\,{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm def}}{{=}}}\,\sum_{\ell=1}^{m}\sigma_{\ell},\quad\forall\;{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}\in\{0,1\}^{m}.

Proposition 6.14.

Let
{\varepsilon}\in(0,{{\varepsilon}_{\ref{def:ovl}}(\lambda)}]
,
0<t<t^{\prime}<\infty
,
x_{0}^{1},x_{0}^{2},\widetilde{x}_{0}^{1},\widetilde{x}_{0}^{2}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
,
m\in\mathbb{N}
,
\mathbf{i}_{1}\neq\cdots\neq\mathbf{i}_{m}
with
\mathbf{i}_{1},\cdots,\mathbf{i}_{m}\in\mathcal{E}_{4}
,
\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}}\in\{0,1\}
, and
{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}\in\{0,1\}^{m}
. Consider the corresponding iterated graphical integrals in (
6.42
).

(1
∘
)

For
\mathbf{i}_{1}=(2,1)
,

\displaystyle\int{\mathrm{d}}\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}^{\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}}_{{\varepsilon};0}\int{\mathrm{d}}\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}^{\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}}_{{\varepsilon};1}\int{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{G}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{{\varepsilon};0}\int{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{G}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{{\varepsilon};1}\cdots\int{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{G}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{{\varepsilon};m}\mathds{1}

\displaystyle\quad\leq\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}{\mathrm{d}}\widetilde{\overline{z}}P_{2\widetilde{s}_{1}}(\widetilde{x}_{0}^{2}-{\varepsilon}\widetilde{\overline{z}},\widetilde{x}_{0}^{1})\phi(\widetilde{\overline{z}})\Biggl(\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}{\mathrm{d}}\widetilde{z}_{1}{\mathfrak{s}}^{\lambda,\phi}_{\varepsilon}(\widetilde{s}_{1},\widetilde{\tau}_{1};\widetilde{\overline{z}}/{\sqrt{2}},\widetilde{z}_{1})\varphi(\widetilde{z}_{1})\widetilde{\sigma}_{1}+\widehat{\widetilde{\sigma}}_{1}\Biggr)

\displaystyle\quad\quad\times\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}{\mathrm{d}}\overline{z}P_{2u_{1}}(x_{0}^{2}-{\varepsilon}\overline{z},x_{0}^{1})\phi(\overline{z})\Biggl(\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}{\mathrm{d}}z_{1}{\mathfrak{s}}^{\lambda,\phi}_{\varepsilon}(0,v_{1};\overline{z}/{\sqrt{2}},z_{1})\varphi(z_{1})\sigma_{1}+\widehat{\sigma}_{1}\Biggr)

\displaystyle\quad\quad\times\Biggl(\prod_{\scriptstyle\ell:\sigma_{\ell}=1\atop\scriptstyle 2\leq\ell\leq m}\overline{{\mathfrak{s}}}^{\lambda,\phi}_{\varepsilon}(v_{\ell})\Biggr)\Lambda_{\varepsilon}^{\lVert\widehat{\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}}}\rVert+\lVert\widehat{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}}\rVert}

\displaystyle\quad\quad\times\Biggl(\frac{\mathds{1}_{m\geq 2}}{t^{\prime}-t-\widetilde{\tau}_{1}+2u_{2}+u_{1}}+\mathds{1}_{m=1}\Biggr)\left(\prod_{\ell=2}^{m-1}\frac{1}{2u_{\ell+1}+u_{\ell}}\right),

(6.49)

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}^{(\widetilde{B}^{1},\widetilde{B}^{2})}_{(\widetilde{x}_{0}^{1},\widetilde{x}_{0}^{2})}\Bigg[\int{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{G}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{{\varepsilon};0}\int{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{G}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{{\varepsilon};1}\cdots\int{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{G}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{{\varepsilon};m}\mathds{1}\mid_{(x_{0}^{3},x_{0}^{4})=(\widetilde{B}^{1}_{t^{\prime}-t},\widetilde{B}^{2}_{t^{\prime}-t})}\Bigg]

\displaystyle\quad\leq\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}{\mathrm{d}}\overline{z}P_{2u_{1}}(x_{0}^{2}-{\varepsilon}\overline{z},x_{0}^{1})\phi(\overline{z})\Biggl(\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}{\mathrm{d}}z_{1}{\mathfrak{s}}^{\lambda,\phi}_{\varepsilon}(0,v_{1};\overline{z}/{\sqrt{2}},z_{1})\varphi(z_{1})\sigma_{1}+\widehat{\sigma}_{1}\Biggr)

\displaystyle\quad\quad\times\Biggl(\prod_{\scriptstyle\ell:\sigma_{\ell}=1\atop\scriptstyle 2\leq\ell\leq m}\overline{{\mathfrak{s}}}^{\lambda,\phi}_{\varepsilon}(v_{\ell})\Biggr)\Lambda_{\varepsilon}^{\lVert\widehat{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}}\rVert}\Biggl(\frac{\mathds{1}_{m\geq 2}}{t^{\prime}-t+2u_{2}+u_{1}}+\mathds{1}_{m=1}\Biggr)\left(\prod_{\ell=2}^{m-1}\frac{1}{2u_{\ell+1}+u_{\ell}}\right).

(6.50)

(2
∘
)

For
\mathbf{i}_{1}=(4,3)
or
(3,2)
,

\displaystyle\int{\mathrm{d}}\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}^{\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}}_{{\varepsilon};0}\int{\mathrm{d}}\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}^{\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}}_{{\varepsilon};1}\int{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{G}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{{\varepsilon};0}\int{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{G}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{{\varepsilon};1}\cdots\int{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{G}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{{\varepsilon};m}\mathds{1}

\displaystyle\quad\leq\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}{\mathrm{d}}\widetilde{\overline{z}}P_{2\widetilde{s}_{1}}(\widetilde{x}_{0}^{2}-{\varepsilon}\widetilde{\overline{z}},\widetilde{x}_{0}^{1})\phi(\widetilde{\overline{z}})\left(\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}{\mathrm{d}}\widetilde{z}_{1}{\mathfrak{s}}^{\lambda,\phi}_{\varepsilon}(\widetilde{s}_{1},\widetilde{\tau}_{1};\widetilde{\overline{z}}/{\sqrt{2}},\widetilde{z}_{1})\varphi(\widetilde{z}_{1})\widetilde{\sigma}_{1}+\widehat{\widetilde{\sigma}}_{1}\right)

\displaystyle\quad\quad\times\Biggl(\prod_{\scriptstyle\ell:\sigma_{\ell}=1\atop\scriptstyle 1\leq\ell\leq m}\overline{{\mathfrak{s}}}^{\lambda,\phi}_{\varepsilon}(v_{\ell})\Biggr)\Lambda_{\varepsilon}^{\lVert\widehat{\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}}}\rVert+\lVert\widehat{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}}\rVert}\Biggl(\frac{1}{t^{\prime}-t-\widetilde{\tau}_{1}+u_{1}}\Biggr)\left(\prod_{\ell=1}^{m-1}\frac{1}{2u_{\ell+1}+u_{\ell}}\right),

(6.51)

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}^{(\widetilde{B}^{1},\widetilde{B}^{2})}_{(\widetilde{x}_{0}^{1},\widetilde{x}_{0}^{2})}\Bigg[\int{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{G}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{{\varepsilon};0}\int{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{G}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{{\varepsilon};1}\cdots\int{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{G}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{{\varepsilon};m}\mathds{1}\mid_{(x_{0}^{3},x_{0}^{4})=(\widetilde{B}^{1}_{t^{\prime}-t},\widetilde{B}^{2}_{t^{\prime}-t})}\Bigg]

\displaystyle\quad\leq\Biggl(\prod_{\scriptstyle\ell:\sigma_{\ell}=1\atop\scriptstyle 1\leq\ell\leq m}\overline{{\mathfrak{s}}}^{\lambda,\phi}_{\varepsilon}(v_{\ell})\Biggr)\Lambda_{\varepsilon}^{\lVert\widehat{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}}\rVert}\Biggl(\frac{1}{t^{\prime}-t+u_{1}}\Biggr)\left(\prod_{\ell=1}^{m-1}\frac{1}{2u_{\ell+1}+u_{\ell}}\right).

(6.52)

Proof.
This proof is divided into five steps. Steps 1 proves an explicit formula for
\int{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{G}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{{\varepsilon};m}\mathds{1}
. Step 2 proves a bound for
\int{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{G}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{{\varepsilon};2}\cdots\int{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{G}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{{\varepsilon};m}\mathds{1}
when
m\geq 2
. By using the results of Steps 1 and 2,
Step 3 proves both bounds in (1
∘
), Step 4 proves (
6.51
) and (
6.52
) for
\mathbf{i}=(4,3)
, and Step 5 proves (
6.51
) and (
6.52
) for
\mathbf{i}=(3,2)
. In particular, by following these steps, the reader may regard this proof as an extension of
[
18
, Proof of (4.53) for Proposition 4.14 (2
∘
), pp.167–170]
, with a modified method for bounding the graphical integrals
\int{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{G}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{{\varepsilon};0}\mathds{1}
and
\int{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{G}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{{\varepsilon};1}\mathds{1}
. See Steps 3–5 for these modifications. Also, we note the following simple identity that will be used repeatedly in what follows: for all
x^{\prime},x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
and
s,t,{\varepsilon}\in(0,\infty)
and all nonnegative
F
,

\displaystyle\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{4}}\begin{bmatrix}{\mathrm{d}}z^{\prime}\\
{\mathrm{d}}z\end{bmatrix}_{\otimes}\begin{bmatrix}P_{t}(x^{\prime},z+{\varepsilon}(z^{\prime}-z))\\
P_{s}(x,z)\end{bmatrix}_{\times}F(z^{\prime}-z)

\displaystyle=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}{\mathrm{d}}\bar{z}P_{s+t}(x^{\prime}-{\varepsilon}\bar{z},x)F(\bar{z}),

(6.53)

which holds by the Chapman–Kolmogorov equation.

Step 1.

In this step, we prove the following identity:

\displaystyle\int{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{G}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{{\varepsilon};m}\mathds{1}=\left(\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}{\mathrm{d}}z_{m}{\mathfrak{s}}^{\lambda,\phi}_{\varepsilon}(0,v_{m};x_{m}^{\mathbf{i}_{m}},z_{m})\varphi(z_{m})\sigma_{m}+\widehat{\sigma}_{m}\right)(\Lambda_{\varepsilon}^{1/2})^{\#_{m}},

(6.54)

where
v_{m}
is defined in (
6.7
), and
\#_{m}
denotes the number of left-maximal entanglement-free paths ending at time
t

such that the left end-vertices are given by
(x_{\ell_{0}}^{i_{\ell_{0}}\prime},x_{\ell_{0}}^{i_{\ell_{0}}},s_{\ell_{0}})
for some
1\leq\ell_{0}\leq m
satisfying
\sigma_{\ell_{0}}=0
.

Identity (
6.54
) holds by considering the following. Recall that
\int{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{G}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{{\varepsilon};m}
is defined with the measures from (
6.34
) such that the variables of integration are given by the spatial components of the vertices of
\mathcal{G}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{{\varepsilon};m}
. By this definition and the Chapman–Kolmogorov equation, the right-hand side of (
6.54
) for
\sigma_{m}=1
follows by integrating out the spatial components of the right end-vertices of
\mathcal{G}^{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}}_{{\varepsilon};m}
. In particular, in doing the integration, we need to take into account the possibility that some left-maximal entanglement-free paths ending at time
t
have left end-vertices given by
(x_{\ell_{0}}^{i_{\ell_{0}}\prime},x_{\ell_{0}}^{i_{\ell_{0}}},s_{\ell_{0}})
for some
1\leq\ell_{0}<m
and
\sigma_{\ell_{0}}=0
[e.g. the two paths marked yellow and begun at time
s_{4}
in Figure
2
]. For such a left-maximal entanglement-free path, one of the first two weights in (
6.1.2
) has to come into play, and it brings in a multiplicative factor of
\Lambda_{\varepsilon}^{1/2}
. This is how
(\Lambda_{\varepsilon}^{1/2})^{\#_{m}}
in (
6.54
) arises from
\int{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{G}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{{\varepsilon};m}\mathds{1}
. Also, recall that when
\sigma_{m}=1
, the weight of the coiled line segment in

\mathcal{G}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{{\varepsilon};m}
is given by the multiplication column in (
6.1.2
) for
\ell=m
, and
v_{m}=\tau_{m}-s_{m}
according to (
6.7
). Hence, when
\sigma_{m}=1
, we get

\displaystyle\int{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{G}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{{\varepsilon};m}\mathds{1}

\displaystyle=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{4}}\begin{bmatrix}{\mathrm{d}}z_{m}^{\prime}\\
{\mathrm{d}}z_{m}\end{bmatrix}_{\otimes}\varphi(z_{m})\begin{bmatrix}P_{v_{m}}({\sqrt{2}}x_{m}^{i_{m}}+{\varepsilon}x_{m}^{\mathbf{i}_{m}},z^{\prime}_{m})\\
\vskip-8.5359pt\\
{\mathfrak{s}}^{\lambda,\phi}_{\varepsilon}(0,v_{m};x_{m}^{\mathbf{i}_{m}},z_{m})\end{bmatrix}_{\times}(\Lambda_{\varepsilon}^{1/2})^{\#_{m}}

\displaystyle=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}{\mathrm{d}}z_{m}{\mathfrak{s}}^{\lambda,\phi}_{\varepsilon}(0,v_{m};x_{m}^{\mathbf{i}_{m}},z_{m})\varphi(z_{m})(\Lambda_{\varepsilon}^{1/2})^{\#_{m}},

which proves (
6.54
) for
\sigma_{m}=1
. Identity (
6.54
) for
\sigma_{m}=0
follows from a simpler argument.

Step 2.

In this step, we prove that for all
m\geq 2
and
\ell_{0}\in\{1,2\}
,

\displaystyle\int{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{G}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{{\varepsilon};\ell_{0}}\cdots\int{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{G}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{{\varepsilon};m}\mathds{1}

\displaystyle\leq\Biggl(\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}{\mathrm{d}}z_{\ell_{0}}{\mathfrak{s}}^{\lambda,\phi}_{\varepsilon}(0,v_{\ell_{0}};x_{\ell_{0}}^{\mathbf{i}_{\ell_{0}}},z_{\ell_{0}})\varphi(z_{\ell_{0}})\sigma_{\ell_{0}}+\widehat{\sigma}_{\ell_{0}}\Biggr)

\displaystyle\quad\times\Biggl(\prod_{\scriptstyle\ell:\sigma_{\ell}=1\atop\scriptstyle\ell_{0}+1\leq\ell\leq m}\overline{{\mathfrak{s}}}^{\lambda,\phi}_{\varepsilon}(v_{\ell})\Biggr)(\Lambda_{\varepsilon}^{1/2})^{\sum_{\ell=\ell_{0}}^{m}\#_{\ell}}\left(\prod_{\ell=\ell_{0}}^{m-1}\frac{1}{2u_{\ell+1}+u_{\ell}}\right),

(6.55)

where
\overline{{\mathfrak{s}}}^{\lambda,\phi}_{\varepsilon}(\cdot)
is defined in (
6.45
),
u_{\ell}
’s are defined in (
6.7
), and
\#_{\ell}
for
1\leq\ell\leq m-1
are analogues of
\#_{m}
, defined as follows:

\displaystyle\#_{\ell}\,{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm def}}{{=}}}\,\#\left\{\mathcal{P}\in\{\mathcal{P}_{\ell}^{\prime},\mathcal{P}_{\ell}\};\begin{array}[]{ll}\mbox{the left end-vertex of $\mathcal{P}$ is $(x_{\ell_{0}}^{i_{\ell_{0}\prime}},x_{\ell_{0}}^{i_{\ell_{0}}},s_{\ell_{0}})$}\\
\mbox{for some $1\leq\ell_{0}\leq\ell$ and $\sigma_{\ell_{0}}=0$}\end{array}\right\}\in\{0,1,2\}.

[Recall that
\mathcal{P}_{\ell}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{P}_{\ell}
are paths defining the spine of
\mathcal{G}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{\oslash;\ell}
, specified in Definition
6.7
(2
∘
).]
The numbers
\#_{\ell}
defined in (
6.1.3
) serve a purpose similar to
\#_{m}
: we use this
\#_{\ell}
to keep track of the number of weights in
\mathcal{G}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{{\varepsilon};\ell}
such that they are equal to one of the first two weights in (
6.1.2
). The application of
\#_{1}
is deferred until Steps 3–5, though.

By (
6.54
), it suffices to prove (
6.55
) for
m\geq 3
. In this case,
we begin by expressing
\int{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{G}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{{\varepsilon};m-1}\int{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{G}_{{\varepsilon};m}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}\mathds{1}
more explicitly;
see the orange subgraphs in Figures
2
and
4
for examples of
\mathcal{G}^{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}}_{{\varepsilon};m-1}
.
By (
6.54
), we have

\displaystyle\int{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{G}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{{\varepsilon};m-1}\int{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{G}_{{\varepsilon};m}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}\mathds{1}

\displaystyle\quad=\int{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{G}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{{\varepsilon};m-1}\left(\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}{\mathrm{d}}z_{m}{\mathfrak{s}}^{\lambda,\phi}_{\varepsilon}(0,v_{m};x_{m}^{\mathbf{i}_{m}},z_{m})\varphi(z_{m})\sigma_{m}+\widehat{\sigma}_{m}\right)(\Lambda_{\varepsilon}^{1/2})^{\#_{m}}

(6.58)

\displaystyle\quad=\int{\mathrm{d}}\mu\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{4}}\begin{bmatrix}{\mathrm{d}}x^{i_{m}\prime}_{m}\\
{\mathrm{d}}x^{i_{m}}_{m}\end{bmatrix}_{\otimes}\varphi(x^{\mathbf{i}_{m}}_{m})(\Lambda_{\varepsilon}^{1/2})^{\#_{m-1}}\begin{bmatrix}P_{\lvert\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{m-1}\rvert}(A^{\prime},x_{m}^{i_{m}}+{\varepsilon}{\sqrt{2}}x_{m}^{\mathbf{i}_{m}})\\
P_{\lvert\mathcal{P}_{m-1}\rvert}(A,x_{m}^{i_{m}})\end{bmatrix}_{\times}

\displaystyle\quad\quad\times\left(\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}{\mathrm{d}}z_{m}{\mathfrak{s}}^{\lambda,\phi}_{\varepsilon}(0,v_{m};x_{m}^{\mathbf{i}_{m}},z_{m})\varphi(z_{m})\sigma_{m}+\widehat{\sigma}_{m}\right)(\Lambda_{\varepsilon}^{1/2})^{\#_{m}},

(6.59)

where
\lvert\mathcal{P}\rvert
is defined to be the duration of a path
\mathcal{P}
[Definition
6.6
(5
∘
)]. Let us provide further details on why (
6.59
) holds and explain the new notation introduced therein. First, the second multiplication column in (
6.59
) follows by using the Chapman–Kolmogorov equation and
the spine
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{m-1}\oplus\mathcal{P}_{m-1}
of
\mathcal{G}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{{\varepsilon};m-1}
so that

x^{i_{m}}_{m}+{\varepsilon}{\sqrt{2}}x_{m}^{\mathbf{i}_{m}}
and
x^{i_{m}}_{m}
arise as the terminal states of some of the heat kernels in (
6.1.2
) and (
6.1.2
) for
\ell=m-1
. Also, (
6.59
) uses a measure
{\mathrm{d}}\mu
and linear combinations
A^{\prime},A
of spatial components of vertices, chosen as follows:

•

When
\sigma_{m-1}=1
,
{\mathrm{d}}\mu
is defined as follows, using the measure
{\mathrm{d}}z^{\prime}_{m-1}\otimes{\mathrm{d}}z_{m-1}\varphi(z_{m-1})
from (
6.34b
) and
a weight from (
6.1.2
) with
\ell=m-1
:

{\mathrm{d}}\mu=\begin{bmatrix}{\mathrm{d}}z_{m-1}^{\prime}\\
{\mathrm{d}}z_{m-1}\end{bmatrix}_{\otimes}\varphi(z_{m-1})\begin{bmatrix}P_{s_{m-1},\tau_{m-1}}({\sqrt{2}}x_{m-1}^{i_{m-1}}+{\varepsilon}x_{m-1}^{\mathbf{i}_{m-1}},z^{\prime}_{m-1})\\
\vskip-8.5359pt\\
{\mathfrak{s}}^{\lambda,\phi}_{\varepsilon}(s_{m-1},\tau_{m-1};x_{m-1}^{\mathbf{i}_{m-1}},z_{m-1})\end{bmatrix}_{\times}.

•

When
\sigma_{m-1}=0
,
\int{\mathrm{d}}\mu
only acts as a “placeholder,” so we only require that
{\mathrm{d}}\mu
satisfy
\int{\mathrm{d}}\mu\mathds{1}=1
. Specifically,
\mu
can be an arbitrary measure satisfying
\int{\mathrm{d}}\mu\mathds{1}=1
, but not using any spatial components of vertices for its variables of integration.

•

The spatial components defining
A^{\prime}
and
A
in (
6.59
) are chosen from the left end-vertices of
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{m-1}
and
\mathcal{P}_{m-1}
, and
A^{\prime}
and
A
may depend on some of the variables of integration in
{\mathrm{d}}\mu
in the case of
\sigma_{m-1}=1
. In particular,
A^{\prime}
and
A
are independent of
x_{m}^{i_{m}\prime}
and
x_{m}^{i_{m}}
.

Next, we bound
\int{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{G}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{{\varepsilon};m-1}\int{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{G}_{{\varepsilon};m}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}\mathds{1}
. By (
6.2
), (
6.53
) and (
6.59
), we get:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\int{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{G}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{{\varepsilon};m-1}\int{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{G}_{{\varepsilon};m}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}\mathds{1}&=\int{\mathrm{d}}\mu\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}{\mathrm{d}}\bar{z}\phi(\bar{z})(\Lambda_{\varepsilon}^{1/2})^{\#_{m-1}}P_{\lvert\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{m-1}\oplus\mathcal{P}_{m-1}\rvert}(A^{\prime}-{\varepsilon}\bar{z},A)\\
&\quad\times\left(\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}{\mathrm{d}}z_{m}{\mathfrak{s}}^{\lambda,\phi}_{\varepsilon}(0,v_{m};\overline{z}/{\sqrt{2}},z_{m})\varphi(z_{m})\sigma_{m}+\widehat{\sigma}_{m}\right)(\Lambda_{\varepsilon}^{1/2})^{\#_{m}}\end{split}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\int{\mathrm{d}}\mu\mathds{1}\right)\frac{(\Lambda_{\varepsilon}^{1/2})^{\#_{m-1}}}{2\pi\lvert\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{m-1}\oplus\mathcal{P}_{m-1}\rvert}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}{\mathrm{d}}\bar{z}\phi(\bar{z})

\displaystyle\quad\times\left(\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}{\mathrm{d}}z_{m}{\mathfrak{s}}^{\lambda,\phi}_{\varepsilon}(0,v_{m};\overline{z}/{\sqrt{2}},z_{m})\varphi(z_{m})\sigma_{m}+\widehat{\sigma}_{m}\right)(\Lambda_{\varepsilon}^{1/2})^{\#_{m}}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\int{\mathrm{d}}\mu\mathds{1}\right)(\Lambda_{\varepsilon}^{1/2})^{\#_{m-1}}\left(\frac{1}{2u_{m}+u_{m-1}}\right)\left(\overline{{\mathfrak{s}}}^{\lambda,\phi}_{\varepsilon}(v_{m})\sigma_{m}+\widehat{\sigma}_{m}\right)(\Lambda_{\varepsilon}^{1/2})^{\#_{m}}

\displaystyle=\biggl(\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}{\mathrm{d}}z_{m-1}{\mathfrak{s}}^{\lambda,\phi}_{\varepsilon}(0,v_{m-1};x_{m-1}^{\mathbf{i}_{m-1}},z_{m-1})\varphi(z_{m-1})\sigma_{m-1}+\widehat{\sigma}_{m-1}\biggr)

\displaystyle\quad\times\Biggl(\prod_{\scriptstyle\ell:\sigma_{\ell}=1\atop\scriptstyle m\leq\ell\leq m}\overline{{\mathfrak{s}}}^{\lambda,\phi}_{\varepsilon}(v_{\ell})\Biggr)(\Lambda_{\varepsilon}^{1/2})^{\sum_{\ell=m-1}^{m}\#_{\ell}}\Bigg(\prod_{\ell={m-1}}^{m-1}\frac{1}{2u_{\ell+1}+u_{\ell}}\Bigg),

(6.60)

where the first inequality uses the bound
P_{r}(x)\leq 1/(2\pi r)
,
the second inequality uses the bound in Lemma
6.8
, the definition (
6.45
) of
\overline{{\mathfrak{s}}}^{\lambda,\phi}_{\varepsilon}
, and the assumption that
\phi
is a probability density, and
the last equality uses the choice of
{\mathrm{d}}\mu
specified below (
6.59
).

Finally, by recalling how (
6.54
) is used in (
6.58
), it should become apparent that the derivation of (
6.60
) naturally extends to provide similar bounds for
\int{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{G}_{{\varepsilon};\ell}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}\cdots\int{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{G}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{{\varepsilon};m}\mathds{1}
, proceeding inductively in the order
\ell=m-2,m-3,\ldots,\ell_{0}
, for all
\ell_{0}\in\{1,2\}
. Consequently, (
6.55
) also holds when
\ell=\ell_{0}
.

Step 3.

We prove the bounds in (1
∘
) in this step, starting with the proof of (
6.49
), which uses
\mathbf{i}_{1}=(2,1)
. See Figure
4
for an example of the underlying graphs.

For
m\geq 2
, one of
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{1},\mathcal{P}_{1}
defining the spine of
\mathcal{G}^{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}}_{{\varepsilon};1}
in
\mathcal{G}^{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}}_{\varepsilon}
can be extended to a left-maximal entanglement-free path in
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\varepsilon}^{\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}}}\oplus\mathcal{G}_{\varepsilon}^{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}}
(in the sense analogous to the one in Definition
6.6
) such that the duration is extended by
t^{\prime}-t-\widetilde{\tau}_{1}
, where
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}^{\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}}}_{{\varepsilon}}\oplus\mathcal{G}^{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}}_{{\varepsilon}}
is defined in Definition
6.12
and, as in (
6.12
),
\widetilde{\tau}_{1}=\widetilde{s}_{1}
if
\widetilde{\sigma}_{1}=0
. More precisely, the extension must be via the vertex
(\widetilde{x}_{2}^{1},t^{\prime}-t)=(x_{0}^{3},0)
or the vertex
(\widetilde{x}_{2}^{2},t^{\prime}-t)=(x_{0}^{4},0)
, as illustrated in Figure
4
.
Write
\mathcal{P}_{1}^{\prime\rm e},\mathcal{P}_{1}^{\rm e}
for
\mathcal{P}_{1}^{\prime},\mathcal{P}_{1}
after this extension. Then with notation analogous to
\lvert\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{1}\oplus\mathcal{P}_{1}\rvert
,

\displaystyle\lvert\mathcal{P}^{\prime{\mathrm{e}}}_{1}\oplus\mathcal{P}^{\mathrm{e}}_{1}\rvert\geq t^{\prime}-t-\widetilde{\tau}_{1}+2u_{2}+u_{1},

(6.61)

where the contribution
2u_{2}+u_{1}
follows from Lemma
6.8
for
\ell=1
.

With (
6.61
), we can bound the left-hand side of our main objective of Step 3, namely (
6.49
), by applying the derivation of (
6.55
) with
\ell_{0}=2
after a slight modification. Specifically, we have the following inequalities, starting by applying (
6.55
) with
\ell_{0}=2
to get the first inequality:

\displaystyle\int{\mathrm{d}}\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}^{\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}}}_{{\varepsilon};0}\int{\mathrm{d}}\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}^{\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}}}_{{\varepsilon};1}\int{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{G}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{{\varepsilon};0}\int{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{G}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{{\varepsilon};1}\cdots\int{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{G}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{{\varepsilon};m}\mathds{1}

\displaystyle\quad\leq\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{4}}\begin{bmatrix}{\mathrm{d}}\widetilde{x}_{1}^{2}\\
{\mathrm{d}}\widetilde{x}_{1}^{1}\end{bmatrix}_{\otimes}\varphi(\widetilde{x}_{1}^{\mathbf{i}_{0}})\begin{bmatrix}P_{\widetilde{s}_{1}}(\widetilde{x}_{0}^{2},\widetilde{x}_{1}^{1}+{\varepsilon}{\sqrt{2}}\widetilde{x}^{\mathbf{i}_{0}}_{1})\\
P_{\widetilde{s}_{1}}(\widetilde{x}_{0}^{1},\widetilde{x}_{1}^{1})\end{bmatrix}_{\times}

\displaystyle\quad\quad\times\Bigg(\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{4}}\begin{bmatrix}{\mathrm{d}}\widetilde{z}_{1}^{\prime}\\
{\mathrm{d}}\widetilde{z}_{1}\end{bmatrix}_{\otimes}\varphi(\widetilde{z}_{1})\begin{bmatrix}P_{\widetilde{v}_{1}}({\sqrt{2}}\widetilde{x}_{1}^{1}+{\varepsilon}\widetilde{x}_{1}^{\mathbf{i}_{0}},\widetilde{z}^{\prime}_{1})\\
\vskip-8.5359pt\\
{\mathfrak{s}}^{\lambda,\phi}_{\varepsilon}(\widetilde{s}_{1},\widetilde{\tau}_{1};\widetilde{x}_{1}^{\mathbf{i}_{0}},\widetilde{z}_{1})\end{bmatrix}_{\times}\widetilde{\sigma}_{1}+\Lambda_{\varepsilon}\widehat{\widetilde{\sigma}}_{1}\Bigg)

\displaystyle\quad\quad\times\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{4}}\begin{bmatrix}{\mathrm{d}}x^{2}_{1}\\
{\mathrm{d}}x^{1}_{1}\end{bmatrix}_{\otimes}\varphi(x^{\mathbf{i}_{1}}_{1})\begin{bmatrix}P_{u_{1}}(x_{0}^{2},x_{1}^{1}+{\varepsilon}{\sqrt{2}}x^{\mathbf{i}_{1}}_{1})\\
P_{u_{1}}(x_{0}^{1},x_{1}^{1})\end{bmatrix}_{\times}

\displaystyle\quad\quad\times\Biggl(\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}{\mathrm{d}}z_{1}{\mathfrak{s}}^{\lambda,\phi}_{\varepsilon}(0,v_{1};x_{1}^{\mathbf{i}_{1}},z_{1})\varphi(z_{1})\sigma_{1}+\widehat{\sigma}_{1}\Biggr)

\displaystyle\quad\quad\times\Biggl(\prod_{\scriptstyle\ell:\sigma_{\ell}=1\atop\scriptstyle 2\leq\ell\leq m}\overline{{\mathfrak{s}}}^{\lambda,\phi}_{\varepsilon}(v_{\ell})\Biggr)(\Lambda_{\varepsilon}^{1/2})^{\sum_{\ell=1}^{m}\#_{\ell}}\Biggl(\frac{1}{t^{\prime}-t-\widetilde{\tau}_{1}+2u_{2}+u_{1}}\Biggr)\left(\prod_{\ell=2}^{m-1}\frac{1}{2u_{\ell+1}+u_{\ell}}\right)

(6.62)

\displaystyle\quad\leq\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}{\mathrm{d}}\widetilde{\overline{z}}P_{2\widetilde{s}_{1}}(\widetilde{x}_{0}^{2}-{\varepsilon}\widetilde{\overline{z}},\widetilde{x}_{0}^{1})\phi(\widetilde{\overline{z}})\Biggl(\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}{\mathrm{d}}\widetilde{z}_{1}{\mathfrak{s}}^{\lambda,\phi}_{\varepsilon}(\widetilde{s}_{1},\widetilde{\tau}_{1};\widetilde{\overline{z}}/{\sqrt{2}},\widetilde{z}_{1})\varphi(\widetilde{z}_{1})\widetilde{\sigma}_{1}+\widehat{\widetilde{\sigma}}_{1}\Biggr)

\displaystyle\quad\quad\times\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}{\mathrm{d}}\overline{z}P_{2u_{1}}(x_{0}^{2}-{\varepsilon}\overline{z},x_{0}^{1})\phi(\overline{z})\Biggl(\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}{\mathrm{d}}z_{1}{\mathfrak{s}}^{\lambda,\phi}_{\varepsilon}(0,v_{1};\overline{z}/{\sqrt{2}},z_{1})\varphi(z_{1})\sigma_{1}+\widehat{\sigma}_{1}\Biggr)

\displaystyle\quad\quad\times\Biggl(\prod_{\scriptstyle\ell:\sigma_{\ell}=1\atop\scriptstyle 2\leq\ell\leq m}\overline{{\mathfrak{s}}}^{\lambda,\phi}_{\varepsilon}(v_{\ell})\Biggr)\Lambda_{\varepsilon}^{\lVert\widehat{\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}}}\rVert+\lVert\widehat{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}}\rVert}\Biggl(\frac{1}{t^{\prime}-t-\widetilde{\tau}_{1}+2u_{2}+u_{1}}\Biggr)\left(\prod_{\ell=2}^{m-1}\frac{1}{2u_{\ell+1}+u_{\ell}}\right).

(6.63)

In more detail, the integrators
{\mathrm{d}}\widetilde{x}^{2}_{1}\otimes{\mathrm{d}}\widetilde{x}^{1}_{1}\varphi(\widetilde{x}_{1}^{\mathbf{i}_{0}})
and
{\mathrm{d}}x^{2}_{1}\otimes{\mathrm{d}}x^{1}_{1}\varphi(x^{\mathbf{i}_{1}}_{1})

on the right-hand side of (
6.62
) are the measures, from (
6.34a
), defining
\int{\mathrm{d}}\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}^{\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}}}_{{\varepsilon};0}
and
\int{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{G}^{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}}_{{\varepsilon};0}
, respectively. Also, to get (
6.63
), we have used (
6.53
) twice after integrating out
\widetilde{z}_{1}^{\prime}
, and

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell=1}^{m}\#_{\ell}=2\sum_{\ell=1}^{m}\widehat{\sigma}_{\ell}=2\lVert\widehat{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}}\rVert

(6.64)

because the total number of weights that appear in
\mathcal{G}^{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}}_{\varepsilon}
as one of the first two in (
6.1.2
) is
2\sum_{\ell=1}^{m}\widehat{\sigma}_{\ell}
. The last inequality is the required inequality in (
6.49
) for
m\geq 2
. The case of
m=1
is easier. We can just use (
6.54
), rather than
(
6.55
) with
\ell_{0}=2
, to obtain a simpler analogue of (
6.62
) as follows and simplify in the same way as before:

\displaystyle\int{\mathrm{d}}\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}^{\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}}}_{{\varepsilon};0}\int{\mathrm{d}}\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}^{\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}}}_{{\varepsilon};1}\int{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{G}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{{\varepsilon};0}\int{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{G}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{{\varepsilon};1}\mathds{1}

\displaystyle=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{4}}\begin{bmatrix}{\mathrm{d}}\widetilde{x}_{1}^{2}\\
{\mathrm{d}}\widetilde{x}_{1}^{1}\end{bmatrix}_{\otimes}\varphi(\widetilde{x}_{1}^{\mathbf{i}_{0}})\begin{bmatrix}P_{\widetilde{s}_{1}}(\widetilde{x}_{0}^{2},\widetilde{x}_{1}^{1}+{\varepsilon}{\sqrt{2}}\widetilde{x}^{\mathbf{i}_{0}}_{1})\\
P_{\widetilde{s}_{1}}(\widetilde{x}_{0}^{1},\widetilde{x}_{1}^{1})\end{bmatrix}_{\times}

\displaystyle\quad\times\Bigg(\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{4}}\begin{bmatrix}{\mathrm{d}}\widetilde{z}_{1}^{\prime}\\
{\mathrm{d}}\widetilde{z}_{1}\end{bmatrix}_{\otimes}\varphi(\widetilde{z}_{1})\begin{bmatrix}P_{\widetilde{v}_{1}}({\sqrt{2}}\widetilde{x}_{1}^{1}+{\varepsilon}\widetilde{x}_{1}^{\mathbf{i}_{0}},\widetilde{z}^{\prime}_{1})\\
\vskip-8.5359pt\\
{\mathfrak{s}}^{\lambda,\phi}_{\varepsilon}(\widetilde{s}_{1},\widetilde{\tau}_{1};\widetilde{x}_{1}^{\mathbf{i}_{0}},\widetilde{z}_{1})\end{bmatrix}_{\times}\widetilde{\sigma}_{1}+\Lambda_{\varepsilon}\widehat{\widetilde{\sigma}}_{1}\Bigg)

\displaystyle\quad\times\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{4}}\begin{bmatrix}{\mathrm{d}}x^{2}_{1}\\
{\mathrm{d}}x^{1}_{1}\end{bmatrix}_{\otimes}\varphi(x^{\mathbf{i}_{1}}_{1})\begin{bmatrix}P_{u_{1}}(x_{0}^{2},x_{1}^{1}+{\varepsilon}{\sqrt{2}}x^{\mathbf{i}_{1}}_{1})\\
P_{u_{1}}(x_{0}^{1},x_{1}^{1})\end{bmatrix}_{\times}

\displaystyle\quad\times\Biggl(\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}{\mathrm{d}}z_{1}{\mathfrak{s}}^{\lambda,\phi}_{\varepsilon}(0,v_{1};x_{1}^{\mathbf{i}_{1}},z_{1})\varphi(z_{1})\sigma_{1}+\widehat{\sigma}_{1}\Biggr)(\Lambda_{\varepsilon}^{1/2})^{\#_{1}}.

The proof of (
6.50
) is similar. For
m\geq 2
, an analogue of the bound in (
6.61
)
applies, now improved to

\lvert\mathcal{P}^{\prime{\mathrm{e}}}_{1}\oplus\mathcal{P}^{\mathrm{e}}_{1}\rvert\geq t^{\prime}-t+2u_{2}+u_{1}
.
An inspection of the derivation of (
6.63
) then leads to (
6.50
) for
m\geq 2
. The case of
m=1
is again simpler, as the required bound can be deduced from the following identity, which is implied by (
6.54
):

\displaystyle\int{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{G}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{{\varepsilon};0}\int{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{G}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{{\varepsilon};1}\mathds{1}

\displaystyle=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{4}}\begin{bmatrix}{\mathrm{d}}x^{2}_{1}\\
{\mathrm{d}}x^{1}_{1}\end{bmatrix}_{\otimes}\varphi(x^{\mathbf{i}_{1}}_{1})\begin{bmatrix}P_{u_{1}}(x_{0}^{2},x_{1}^{1}+{\varepsilon}{\sqrt{2}}x^{\mathbf{i}_{1}}_{1})\\
P_{u_{1}}(x_{0}^{1},x_{1}^{1})\end{bmatrix}_{\times}

\displaystyle\quad\times\Biggl(\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}{\mathrm{d}}z_{1}{\mathfrak{s}}^{\lambda,\phi}_{\varepsilon}(0,v_{1};x_{1}^{\mathbf{i}_{1}},z_{1})\varphi(z_{1})\sigma_{1}+\widehat{\sigma}_{1}\Biggr)(\Lambda_{\varepsilon}^{1/2})^{\#_{1}}.

Step 4.
We now begin the proof of (2
∘
), establishing (
6.51
) and (
6.52
) for
\mathbf{i}_{1}=(4,3)
in this step. The proofs of these inequalities for
\mathbf{i}_{1}=(3,2)
will be presented in Step 5.

To obtain (
6.51
) for
\mathbf{i}_{1}=(4,3)
and all
m\geq 1
, it is necessary to obtain a sufficiently sharp bound for the iterated graphical integral
\int{\mathrm{d}}\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}^{\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}}}_{{\varepsilon};0}\int{\mathrm{d}}\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}^{\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}}}_{{\varepsilon};1}\int{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{G}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{{\varepsilon};0}\int{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{G}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{{\varepsilon};1}\cdots\int{\mathrm{d}}\mathcal{G}^{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{{\varepsilon};m}\mathds{1}
as a starting point. The crucial observation for this purpose is the following: the two edges defining
\mathcal{G}^{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}}_{{\varepsilon};0}
can be extended to left-maximal entanglement-free paths within
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\varepsilon}^{\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}}}\oplus\mathcal{G}_{\varepsilon}^{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}}
. More precisely, the duration of each edge is extended from
u_{1}=s_{1}
to
t^{\prime}-t-\widetilde{\tau}_{1}+u_{1}
: one extension passes through the vertex
(\widetilde{x}_{2}^{1},t^{\prime}-t)=(x_{0}^{3},0)
, and the other through
